# SA Business Toolkit

*Complete single-file edition. 31 documents plus 5 templates, in reading order.*

Written for one person running a business in South Africa.

> **This toolkit was written by an AI assistant (Claude, made by Anthropic).** Every fee, threshold, form number and legal requirement was checked against official South African sources, listed in the Sources and verification register at the end. AI can be wrong and South African rules change often. Before you pay money, sign something or file something, open the official link and confirm the number yourself. This is general information, not legal, tax or financial advice.

## South African small business toolkit

A free toolkit for one person starting and running a business in South Africa. You are the owner. You are also the only worker.

Written in simple English for readers who do not speak English as a first language, and for people who have never run a business before.

---

### Read this first

**This toolkit was written by an AI assistant (Claude, made by Anthropic).** Every fee, threshold, form number and legal requirement in it was checked against official South African sources on 13 September 2026, and those sources are listed with links in [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register).

AI can still be wrong, and South African rules change often. Before you pay money, sign something, or file something, open the official link in the sources file and confirm the number yourself.

This is general information. It is not legal advice, tax advice, or financial advice. Full details are in [How this was made and how to check it](#doc-how-this-was-made-and-how-to-check-it).

---

### Where to start

If you have 5 minutes: read [Core: start here](#doc-core-start-here).

If you have an hour: read all of `01-core/`, then open your business type in `04-business-types/`.

If you already know what you need and just want to do it: open [Master checklist](#doc-master-checklist).

If you do not understand a word or an abbreviation: [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

Full instructions are in [How to use this toolkit](#doc-how-to-use-this-toolkit).

### The short version of the whole toolkit

You do not need most of what people tell you to get.

You can legally start selling today, as yourself, with no company registration, no logo, and no website. Earn money first. Add the formal parts as the business grows.

The four things that actually matter early:

1. A separate bank account for business money
2. Filing with SARS, even at zero income
3. A licence, if your business type needs one, before you trade
4. Records kept for five years

Three numbers changed in 2026 and most websites still have them wrong:

- Compulsory VAT registration: **R2.3 million**, not R1 million, from 1 April 2026
- Voluntary VAT registration: **R120,000**, not R50,000
- Turnover tax: **R2.3 million** threshold, with **0% on the first R600,000**

---

Use it, copy it, change it, give it to someone else. No attribution required.

## How to use this toolkit

### What this toolkit is

A set of plain text files you read in order, plus a set of prompts you copy into an AI chat tool.

It is not software. There is nothing to install. Every file opens in any text editor, on any phone, or in Google Docs.

---

### Choose your path

#### Path 1: I have not started yet

Read in this order. Do not skip.

1. [Core: start here](#doc-core-start-here) — the order to do things
2. [Pick your business type](#doc-pick-your-business-type) — find your business type
3. Your business type file in `04-business-types/`
4. [What you need to sell things](#doc-what-you-need-to-sell-things) — check whether you need a licence
5. [Branding prompts](#doc-branding-prompts), then [Brand applications and polish](#doc-brand-applications-and-polish) — build your name and look, then test it
6. [Free tools](#doc-free-tools) — set up your free tools
7. [Which template to use when](#doc-which-template-to-use-when) — build your quote and invoice
8. [Tax and SARS](#doc-tax-and-sars) — sort out SARS
9. [Register: what you actually need](#doc-register-what-you-actually-need) — decide about a company
10. If you decide yes, [Running a Pty Ltd](#doc-running-a-pty-ltd) and [Paying yourself from a Pty Ltd](#doc-paying-yourself-from-a-pty-ltd)

Expect this to take a weekend of reading and two to four weeks of doing.

#### Path 2: I am already trading and want to get compliant

1. [Master checklist](#doc-master-checklist) — tick what you already have
2. [Tax and SARS](#doc-tax-and-sars) — the highest-risk gap for most people
3. Your business type file in `04-business-types/`
4. [Register: what you actually need](#doc-register-what-you-actually-need), specifically the POPIA section

#### Path 4: I am a one-person Pty Ltd selling vehicles, and I want to grow

This is a worked path for one specific reader. Read in this order.

1. [Core: start here](#doc-core-start-here)
2. [Running a Pty Ltd](#doc-running-a-pty-ltd) — what your company already owes each year
3. [Paying yourself from a Pty Ltd](#doc-paying-yourself-from-a-pty-ltd) — before you move a rand out of the company account
4. [Vehicle dealer](#doc-vehicle-dealer) — the three registration layers, the CPA position, and why VAT and turnover tax work differently for you
5. [Vehicles for your business](#doc-vehicles-for-your-business) — the BRNC and proxy process in full
6. [Tax and SARS](#doc-tax-and-sars) — read the SBC section; skip the turnover tax section, it is wrong for a dealer
7. [Which template to use when](#doc-which-template-to-use-when) — build the VAT-registered invoice and the sale agreement with a defect schedule
8. [Branding prompts](#doc-branding-prompts) — name the trading brand, not the company
9. [Working from home and meeting safely](#doc-working-from-home-and-meeting-safely) — if you have no yard: zoning for stock at home, and every viewing and test drive
10. [Adding new lines of business](#doc-adding-new-lines-of-business) — before you add tyres, servicing, or anything else

The sequence for the first 90 days, roughly:

Weeks 1 to 2: confirm the SARS registered representative, file beneficial ownership, open the company bank account, apply for the BRNC at the traffic department, lodge the SAPS 601 dealer application.

Weeks 2 to 4: zoning confirmation for the premises (or for storing stock at home), motor trade insurance in place, motor trade numbers if needed, sale agreement and defect schedule built, SAPS register set up.

Weeks 4 to 8: VAT decision made with a tax practitioner, VAT101 lodged if registering, VAT264 process in place, first stock bought with full paperwork.

Ongoing from day one: rolling 12-month turnover tracked against R2.3 million, loan account checked monthly, stock list with cost per vehicle maintained.

---

### How to use the AI prompts

The prompts in `02-branding-and-marketing/` are written to be copied and pasted into an AI chat tool. They work in Claude, ChatGPT, Gemini, or Microsoft Copilot. The free versions are fine.

#### The rules

**One prompt at a time.** Paste it. Wait for the answer. Do not paste the next prompt until you are happy with the current one.

**Never accept the first answer.** Every branding prompt gives you 5 options. Read all 5. Pick the closest one even if it is not right. Then use the refine prompt to ask for 5 new versions that keep what you liked and fix what you did not. Do this two or three times. The third round is usually much better than the first.

**Start a new chat for each big task.** A chat that runs too long makes the AI forget earlier details. Start fresh for naming, then fresh again for social content.

**Keep your answers.** Save the brand brief, the name, the colours and the voice guide in one document. Later prompts ask you to paste them in.

**Fill in every [SQUARE BRACKET].** The prompts have placeholders. If you leave them, the AI invents answers and the output is generic.

**The AI will be wrong about South African law.** It is a writing helper, not a lawyer or an accountant. Never let it tell you what a fee is, what a form is called, or what a threshold is. Check that in [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register) and on the official site.

#### The refine prompt

Use this after every step in [Branding prompts](#doc-branding-prompts):

```
I like option [NUMBER] best.

What works: [say what you like, in your own words]
What does not work: [say what is wrong]

Give me 5 new options that keep what works and fix what does not.
Do not repeat the options you already gave me.
Number them 1 to 5. After the 5 options, stop and ask me again.
```

#### If the AI runs ahead

Some AI tools will answer three steps at once. When that happens, say:

```
Stop. Go back to step [X] only. Give me 5 options for that step and
nothing else. Wait for my answer before moving on.
```

---

### How the legal parts are written

Some paragraphs in this toolkit stay close to the wording of a law, a regulator, or another source listed in [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register). They are kept close to the source on purpose, so you can check them. They can be hard to read.

Every hard one is followed by a line that starts like this:

> **In plain words:**...

That line says the same thing in everyday English. If the legal sentence confuses you, skip it and read the plain line. You lose nothing.

Each heavy file also opens with a short table called "Words used in this file". Read that first. Every abbreviation in the toolkit is also explained in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

### How to read the facts in this toolkit

Anything with a rand figure, a percentage, a date, a form number or a section of an Act came from a source. All of those sources are listed in [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register) with a link.

Sources are marked **[Official]** when they come from government or a regulator. Those are the ones to trust. Everything else is a pointer.

#### Before you act on a number

Open [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register), find the topic, click the official link, and confirm the figure. This takes two minutes and it is worth it, because:

- Tax thresholds change in February and take effect in March or April
- CIPC and licensing fees change without announcement
- Municipal rules differ between every municipality in the country
- Provincial fees differ between all nine provinces

If a figure in this toolkit disagrees with the official site, the official site is right.

---

### What this toolkit deliberately leaves out

Employment. The moment you hire someone, the Basic Conditions of Employment Act, PAYE, UIF and COIDA all apply, and mistakes there are expensive. Get proper advice before your first hire.

Raising money. Investors, loans, and grants each have their own rules. Nothing here covers them.

Anything above roughly R2 million turnover. At that point VAT, payroll and formal accounting change the picture, and you need a real accountant rather than a toolkit.

Trusts, partnerships and non-profits. This toolkit covers sole proprietors and private companies only.

Companies with more than one shareholder. The two Pty Ltd files assume you are the sole shareholder and the sole director. With a co-owner you also need a shareholders agreement, and your financial reporting obligation may change, because the owner-managed exemption depends on every shareholder also being a director.

Pricing. How much to charge is the hardest question a new business faces, and nothing here answers it. The toolkit tells you what a quote must contain, not what number to put on it.

Cash flow forecasting. The cash book in [Which template to use when](#doc-which-template-to-use-when) records what already happened. It does not predict whether you can pay next month's bills.

Insurance detail. Each business type file says what cover to ask for. None of them compares products or prices. Use a broker.

---

### Common mistakes this toolkit is trying to prevent

**Spending money before earning any.** The most common failure. R15,000 on registration, logo and website before a single sale.

**Trading before a required licence is issued.** You cannot fix this afterwards.

**Registering a company you cannot afford to keep compliant.** R175 to register, far more each year to maintain.

**Filing nothing because you earned nothing.** Penalties grow monthly on returns you never submitted.

**Charging VAT without being registered.** That is fraud, not an administrative error.

**Mixing business and personal money.** It makes every other problem harder to fix.

**Using a "voetstoots" or "no refunds" clause.** For most consumer sales in South Africa, it does not work.

---

### Making the files easier to use

These are markdown files (`.md`). They open as plain text anywhere, but they look better in a markdown reader.

On a phone: open them in Google Drive, or any free markdown app.

On a computer: open them in any text editor, or upload them to Google Drive and open with a markdown viewer.

To make one printable document: open the file, copy the text, paste it into Google Docs or LibreOffice Writer, then export as PDF.

To edit them for your own use: change anything you like. They are yours now. If you give the edited version to someone else, keep [How this was made and how to check it](#doc-how-this-was-made-and-how-to-check-it) with it.

---

### If something here is wrong

It might be. Rules change, and this was written by an AI.

The test is always the same: does the official source in [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register) agree? If not, follow the official source, and correct the file so the next person who reads your copy gets it right.

## AI disclosure

### This toolkit was generated by AI

It was written by Claude, an AI assistant made by Anthropic, in a conversation on 13 September 2026.

No lawyer, accountant, or registered tax practitioner reviewed it before publication.

You should know that before you rely on anything in it.

---

### How it was made

The AI was asked to build a practical toolkit for solo business owners in South Africa. It did three things:

1. **Wrote the guidance** from its general knowledge of running small businesses.
2. **Searched the web** for every South African fee, threshold, form number and legal requirement, because its training data ends in May 2026 and several important numbers changed in the February 2026 Budget.
3. **Recorded the sources** for each of those facts in [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register), with links and dates.

The prompts in `02-branding-and-marketing/` were written by the AI. They were not tested against every AI tool, so results will vary.

---

### What was verified, and what was not

#### Verified against official sources

These were checked against SARS, CIPC, BizPortal, SAPS, the B-BBEE Commission, the Information Regulator, the Department of Transport, provincial government, and the National Consumer Commission:

- Turnover tax threshold and rate bands for 2026/2027
- VAT compulsory and voluntary registration thresholds
- Income tax thresholds by age
- Provisional tax obligations and deadlines
- Tax invoice requirements and the R50 and R5,000 tiers
- Notional input tax on second-hand goods
- CIPC company registration fees
- B-BBEE EME thresholds and affidavit rules
- POPIA information officer registration duty
- Certificate of Acceptability and R638 requirements
- Business Act licensing categories
- ECTA online selling obligations and the cooling-off right
- CPA implied warranty and the position on voetstoots
- Second-Hand Goods Act dealer registration
- BRNC, traffic register numbers and motor trade numbers
- Motor trade number tariffs (Western Cape figures)
- Roadworthy and change of ownership requirements

Each of these is listed with its link in [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register).

#### Not verified

- **Bank fees.** Deliberately left out. They change constantly and differ per account. Ask your bank for a current pricing guide.
- **Municipal fees and processes.** These differ in every municipality and are not published consistently. The toolkit tells you who to ask instead of guessing.
- **Provincial fees** other than the Western Cape motor trade tariffs, which are labelled as such.
- **Third-party service pricing** for tools like Zoho Mail or Canva. The toolkit says to check rather than quoting a figure.
- **Whether any specific business qualifies** for turnover tax, SBC rates, the home office deduction, or a particular licence. That depends on your facts.

---

### Known limitations

**Knowledge cutoff.** The AI's training data ends in May 2026. Anything after that was found by web search during the conversation, not recalled. Events after 13 September 2026 are not reflected at all.

**AI can state wrong things confidently.** This is the main risk. A fabricated form number or fee reads exactly like a real one. That is why every specific claim is linked to a source, and why you should open the link before acting.

**Secondary sources can be stale.** Many South African business websites still quote the old R1 million VAT threshold. Where the toolkit relies on a secondary source rather than an official one, it says so.

**One discrepancy was found and flagged.** Two tax sites gave different primary rebate figures. The toolkit quotes the tax threshold instead, and notes the discrepancy in [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register).

**Legal interpretation is not settled everywhere.** The scope of the CPA six-month warranty, the exact boundary of the turnover tax professional services exclusion, and the copyright position on AI-generated work are all areas where reasonable experts differ. The toolkit flags these rather than pretending they are settled.

**Generic where it should be specific.** The toolkit cannot know your municipality, your province, your turnover, or your trade. Where those matter, it says "ask" rather than inventing an answer.

---

### What this is not

It is not legal advice. It is not tax advice. It is not financial advice. It does not create a professional relationship with anyone.

No liability is accepted for any loss arising from acting on it.

---

### How to check anything in this toolkit

1. Open [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register)
2. Find the topic
3. Open the link marked **[Official]**
4. Confirm the figure or requirement yourself
5. If the official source disagrees with this toolkit, the official source is right

The main official sites:

- SARS — www.sars.gov.za
- Company registration — www.bizportal.gov.za
- CIPC — www.cipc.co.za
- Information Regulator — inforegulator.org.za
- Vehicles — www.natis.gov.za
- SAPS second-hand goods — www.saps.gov.za
- B-BBEE Commission — www.bbbeecommission.co.za
- National Consumer Commission — thencc.org.za
- All South African legislation — www.gov.za and www.saflii.org

---

### When to pay a human instead

Pay a registered professional when:

- The amount at stake is more than a month of profit
- You are choosing between company and sole proprietor with real money involved
- You are deciding between turnover tax and normal tax
- You are signing a contract you do not fully understand
- You are about to hire your first employee
- SARS, a municipality, or a regulator has contacted you
- You are buying or selling a business

One hour with a SARS-registered tax practitioner or an attorney costs less than one penalty. Verify a tax practitioner's registration through SARS eFiling before you pay them.

---

### Corrections log

A verification pass was run on 13 September 2026, after the first draft. Two factual errors were found and fixed. They are recorded here because an AI-written document that hides its corrections is less trustworthy, not more.

**Corrected: deemed remuneration for company directors.** An earlier draft stated that a private company director's PAYE is calculated on a notional monthly salary derived from the previous year's earnings. That rule, paragraph 11C of the Fourth Schedule to the Income Tax Act, was repealed with effect from 1 March 2017. Directors' PAYE is now based on actual remuneration. The error came from relying on two South African tax articles that still describe the repealed rule as current. Both were rejected and the correction is sourced in [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register).

**Corrected: Small Claims Court limit.** An earlier draft gave the limit as R20,000. It rose to R30,000 on 1 August 2026, determined in Government Gazette 55038, Government Notice 7717 of 20 July 2026. Most websites still say R20,000.

**Softened, not corrected.** Four claims were weakened because they could not be verified to a standard worth stating flatly: the annual cost of a.co.za domain, the cost of a logo vector redraw, LibreOffice's mobile capability, and the law report citation for Cohen NO v Segal. Each now gives a range or tells you to check, rather than asserting a figure.

**Third pass, same day: reader-specific review.** The toolkit was re-read as one specific person, a sole director of a Pty Ltd selling vehicles and planning to add other lines. No further factual errors were found. Five gaps were found and filled: turnover tax is wrong for a low-margin reseller and the dealer file did not say so; a dealer crosses the R2.3 million VAT threshold far faster than a service business because turnover is gross sales; unsold vehicles at year end are trading stock, not an expense; a holding deposit needs written cancellation terms to be enforceable; and nothing covered adding a second line of business, trading names, or the Small Business Corporation trap of holding shares in two companies. A new file, [Adding new lines of business](#doc-adding-new-lines-of-business), and a worked reading path in [How to use this toolkit](#doc-how-to-use-this-toolkit) now cover these.

**Fourth pass, same day: readability.** Every file was measured for reading level. The prose written for this toolkit sits at roughly Grade 5 to 8. The paragraphs that stay close to legal or official wording sat at Grade 10 to 12 and ran to 40 to 80 words. Rather than change the sourced wording, every such paragraph of 38 words or more now has a line beneath it starting "In plain words:" that says the same thing in everyday English. Each heavy file also opens with a "Words used in this file" table. Measured on the plain-words reading path, the hardest files dropped by two to three grade levels.

**Fifth pass: the files the readability pass missed.** A check of which files had received plain-words lines and word tables showed 13 of 26. The remaining files scored well on sentence length, but three of them used unexplained terms where a reader cannot skip: the invoice and quote templates, the printable checklist, and the source register. Those now have word tables or a key at the top. The zip was verified to be byte-identical to the working folder after every rebuild.

**Sixth pass: the branding prompts as prompt engineering.** The prompts were reviewed for whether an AI tool given them would produce professional, ownable output. Six weaknesses were found and fixed. The brief had no fixed structure, so downstream prompts got inconsistent input; it now uses six named headings. The image-generation step contradicted the file's own advice to prefer a wordmark; it is now split into a type-only path built in a real font and a mark-only path with no text. No prompt asked the AI to judge its own output; every prompt now ends by naming its weakest option. Two prompts asked the AI for things it cannot reliably do, confirming a name has no rude meaning in another language and computing colour contrast; both now ask for material to check and tell the reader how to check it. A worked example was added so the reader knows the standard. A note on the unsettled ownership of AI-generated logos and on font licensing was added.

**Seventh pass: working from home and meeting customers.** A new core file was added for businesses with no premises, covering home-enterprise zoning, trading by phone and WhatsApp, payment verification, common scams aimed at sellers, cash checking, and meeting strangers for inspections and test drives. Every safety and payment claim was checked against the South African Reserve Bank's published banknote guidance, SABRIC's 2024 fraud figures, BankservAfrica's PayShap limits, and the published guidance of Tracker South Africa, Cars.co.za and MotorHappy on private vehicle sales. The file is written to inform, not to alarm: the risks are described plainly, each with the habit that defeats it.

**Eighth pass: a formatting defect, and a final accuracy sweep.** The drafting tool marks sourced sentences with internal citation tags. In earlier versions those tags leaked into the files, so a reader would have seen text like `(cite index="57-1">` at the start of sourced sentences. All 239 tags were removed; the sourced text is unchanged and every source remains listed by topic in [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register). This defect was invisible to the earlier readability checks because they stripped the tags before measuring. Separately, every rand figure, percentage and deadline stated without a source was listed and checked. The provisional tax, EMP201 and ITR14 deadlines were confirmed against SARS's own calendar page, and two refinements were added: the gazetted 2026 filing-season closing dates, and the rule that a SARS deadline falling on a weekend moves to the business day before. One unverifiable price (what agents charge for company registration) was removed.

**Ninth pass: a shortcut path for people whose name is already decided.** The branding sequence assumed every reader was starting from nothing, and sent someone with a registered name through a naming prompt and a naming-focused competitor scan they did not need. A new file, [Already have your name (shortcut)](#doc-already-have-your-name-shortcut), maps which prompts to skip, supplies a visual-only replacement for the competitor scan that also asks whether the existing name creates visual constraints, and records the three checks still worth doing when a company name is registered: domain and handles, a CIPC trade mark search, and saying the name aloud to speakers of the relevant languages. The point it turns on is that a registered company name protects nothing in the market.

**Tenth pass: mood and materials.** The branding sequence had no step establishing the overall visual direction, so the logo, colour and photography prompts each made independent aesthetic decisions that could contradict each other. A new file, [Mood and materials (use after Prompt 4)](#doc-mood-and-materials-use-after-prompt-4), adds a visual-territory prompt between the name and the logo, plus a materials prompt covering signage, print, vehicle graphics and clothing. Two accuracy checks shaped it. First, popular colour psychology does not survive the literature: Elliot and Maier (2014) find no universal colour meaning, and the widely quoted "62 to 90% of judgment is colour" figure traces to a 2006 literature review rather than a buying experiment, so the prompts ask about brand fit and differentiation and explicitly instruct the AI not to supply colour meanings. Second, signage durability claims were checked against trade sources and one South African supplier, giving the cast-versus-calendered guidance and the local UV figures, both recorded in the Sources register with their limitations noted.

**Eleventh pass: a numbering error in the Drive copy.** The user noticed that the Core folder in Google Drive began at 02. When the Drive version was built, two markdown files were merged into the root START HERE: the repository README and the Core folder's own start-here document. Merging the README was correct. Merging the Core start-here was not, because it was a folder entry document rather than a readme, and removing it left the folder apparently missing its first file and without the orientation document every other folder has. A Core entry document has been created in Drive. The markdown copy was already correct and needed no change.

**Twelfth pass: the one-person company as its own reader, and three corrections.** A new file, [You are the business](#doc-you-are-the-business), covers what happens when the only person in the business cannot work, cannot get paid, or is not there. Re-reading the toolkit specifically as a sole-director, sole-employee Pty Ltd also found errors. `01-core/06` stated that COIDA applies when the company employs someone else; that was wrong, because COIDA's definition of employee includes a working director under a contract of service, so a director drawing a salary must be registered even as the only person in the company. `01-core/02` framed UIF and COIDA purely as a cost triggered by paying yourself, omitting that a sole proprietor is barred from both and therefore has no illness benefit and no injury cover at all. The glossary entry for the Small Claims Court did not record that section 7(1) of the Small Claims Courts Act allows only natural persons to institute a claim, which means incorporating costs you access to the country's cheapest debt-recovery route; the vehicle dealer document needed the same qualification, since it had cited the court without saying which way the disadvantage runs. All three are corrected and sourced.

The lesson generalises. Two of the six problems came from well-ranked South African articles that were simply out of date. Recency of the webpage is not the same as currency of the law. Where this toolkit and an official source disagree, the official source wins.

---

### If you share this toolkit

Please keep this file and [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register) with it, so the next person knows how it was made and can check the facts themselves.

If you correct something, correct it in the file, so your copy gets better rather than staying wrong.

### Change 2: mood and materials step

**Date:** 13 September 2026
**Reason:** Nothing in the branding sequence established the overall visual direction. Prompts 5, 7 and 8 each made their own aesthetic call, so the logo, palette and photography could contradict each other. Materials were also absent everywhere except as constraints.

#### Added

[Mood and materials (use after Prompt 4)](#doc-mood-and-materials-use-after-prompt-4) — new file, about 1,300 words. Prompt A sets a visual territory; Prompt B sets signage, print, vehicle and clothing materials. Includes a line to paste into Prompts 5, 7 and 8 that forces them to agree.

Two things were verified before writing it:

- **Colour psychology is weak.** Elliot and Maier (2014) find no universal colour meaning. The famous "62 to 90% of snap judgment is colour" figure traces to a 2006 literature review, not an experiment. What holds up is colour-to-personality fit (Labrecque and Milne, 2012). The prompts therefore ask about fit and difference, and instruct the AI not to give colour meanings.
- **South African UV is a real design constraint.** Zone 3 UV, vinyl fading in roughly a third of its European life, horizontal installs losing 50 to 70%. Cast versus calendered guidance added. Sources and their limitations recorded.

**Status in Drive:** DONE. Created as "01a Mood and materials - decide this before the logo".

### Known structural differences between the two copies

These are deliberate, not errors.

| Markdown | Drive | Why |
|---|---|---|
| [Start here](#doc-start-here) | Merged into "00 START HERE" | A Drive folder listing does the job a README does, so a separate one was redundant. |
| [Core: start here](#doc-core-start-here) | Now exists in Drive as "01 Start here" in the Core folder | Was wrongly merged into START HERE when Drive was built, leaving the Core folder numbered 02 to 09. Corrected 14 September 2026. |
| [Which template to use when](#doc-which-template-to-use-when) contains the templates as text | Split into a guide plus 5 fill-in Google Docs | Templates you copy and fill in are more useful than templates printed inside a guide. |
| Cash book and logbook are tables inside a markdown file | Two Google Sheets | A logbook you fill in on your phone beats one printed in a document. |
| Plain markdown blockquotes for "In plain words" | Grey shaded single-cell tables | Google Docs silently flattens blockquotes, so they would have been invisible. |
| Fenced code blocks for prompts | Grey shaded boxes in Courier | Makes the start and end of each prompt obvious for copying. |

---

### How to keep the two copies in sync

For a small change, edit the Google Doc directly. The document ID stays the same, so every link pointing at it keeps working.

For a full rewrite of one document, open the existing Google Doc, select all, delete, and paste the new content. The ID survives because the file does.

**Never download, edit and re-upload.** That creates a new file with a new ID, and every link in the toolkit would still point at the old one.

Update this changelog whenever you change either copy.

<a id="doc-core-start-here"></a>

## Start here

This toolkit is for one person running a business in South Africa. You are the owner. You are also the only worker. You have little money and little time.

Everything here is free or very cheap. Every step tells you if it is required by law, or if it is only a good idea.

### The honest truth first

You do not need most of the things people tell you to get.

You can legally start selling today, under your own name, with no company registration, no logo, no website, and no accountant. Many people spend R15,000 and three months on setup before they earn one rand. Do not do that.

Earn money first. Add the formal parts as the business grows.

### Order to do things

Work through these in order. Do not skip ahead.

1. Sell something and get paid. Even once. This proves people want it.
2. Open a separate bank account for business money. Keep business money and home money apart from day one. Ask the bank what your instant payment limits are; you will need them.

 If you will work from home with no shop, read [Working from home and meeting safely](#doc-working-from-home-and-meeting-safely) before your first sale. It is short and it covers the habits that stop you being scammed.
3. Pick a name and a simple look. Use file [Branding prompts](#doc-branding-prompts).
4. Make your quote and invoice templates. Use file [Which template to use when](#doc-which-template-to-use-when).
5. Check if your kind of business needs a licence to sell. Read [What you need to sell things](#doc-what-you-need-to-sell-things), then open your business type in `04-business-types/`. Some businesses may not trade without a licence.
6. Sort out SARS. Use file [Tax and SARS](#doc-tax-and-sars). Do this even if you earn nothing.
7. Decide if you need a registered company. Use file [Register: what you actually need](#doc-register-what-you-actually-need). Most solo businesses do not need one in year one. If you decide yes, read [Running a Pty Ltd](#doc-running-a-pty-ltd) before you register, not after.

Steps 5 and 6 are the two that get people fined. Do not leave them for later.

### What is in this toolkit

Applies to everyone, in this folder:

- [Register: what you actually need](#doc-register-what-you-actually-need) — company registration, bank, B-BBEE, POPIA. What is required and what is not.
- [Tax and SARS](#doc-tax-and-sars) — SARS, turnover tax, VAT, and how to pay no tax legally when you earn little.
- [What you need to sell things](#doc-what-you-need-to-sell-things) — licences and permits overview, and consumer law.
- [Vehicles for your business](#doc-vehicles-for-your-business) — putting a vehicle in the business name, and when that is a bad idea.

Only if you registered a company:

- [Running a Pty Ltd](#doc-running-a-pty-ltd) — what a company costs you every year, and what happens when you stop filing.
- [Paying yourself from a Pty Ltd](#doc-paying-yourself-from-a-pty-ltd) — the three legal ways to take money out, and the tax on each.
- [Adding new lines of business](#doc-adding-new-lines-of-business) — growing sideways under one company: trading names, licences per line, and why a second company can cost you your tax rate.
- [You are the business](#doc-you-are-the-business) — what happens if you cannot work, how to chase an unpaid invoice, and who can run the business if you are not there. The one document that is about you rather than the business.
- [Working from home and meeting safely](#doc-working-from-home-and-meeting-safely) — running it from home with no shop or yard, trading by phone and WhatsApp, getting paid safely, the common scams, and meeting customers to inspect or test drive without risk.

Prompts and templates:

- [Already have your name (shortcut)](#doc-already-have-your-name-shortcut) — the shortcut path if your name is already settled: which prompts to skip, and the three checks still worth doing.
- [Mood and materials (use after Prompt 4)](#doc-mood-and-materials-use-after-prompt-4) — settle the overall mood and the physical materials before choosing a logo, so the logo, colours and photos agree. Includes what South African sun does to signage.
- [Branding prompts](#doc-branding-prompts) — 11 AI prompts for the brief, name, logo, colours, type, photography, voice and about text. Each gives you 5 options. Opens with how to avoid branding that looks AI-made, and what the AI cannot do for you.
- [Brand applications and polish](#doc-brand-applications-and-polish) — the logo files you actually need, signage and print basics, and tests to run before you spend money.
- [Marketing prompts](#doc-marketing-prompts) — AI prompts for social media, WhatsApp, and Google Business Profile.
- [Which template to use when](#doc-which-template-to-use-when) — quote, invoice, terms, privacy notice, logbook, cash book.
- [Free tools](#doc-free-tools) — which free office software to use, and why.

Rules for your kind of business:

- [Pick your business type](#doc-pick-your-business-type) — find yourself in the table, then open that file. Covers vehicle dealing, food, beauty, retail and online, trades, and professional or creative work.

Reference:

- [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register) — every fact in this toolkit mapped to its law and its official link
- [Glossary](#doc-glossary) — every abbreviation explained
- [Master checklist](#doc-master-checklist) — one printable checklist

### How to use the AI prompts

The prompts are written for any AI chat tool. They work in Claude, ChatGPT, Gemini, or Copilot. The free versions are fine.

Rules for using them:

Copy one prompt at a time. Paste it. Wait for the answer. Do not paste the next prompt until you are happy with the current one.

Every branding prompt gives you 5 options. Read all 5. Pick the one closest to what you want, even if it is not perfect. Then tell the AI what you like and what you do not like, and ask for 5 new versions. Do this two or three times. The third round is usually much better than the first.

Never accept the first answer. The first answer is the average answer.

Start a new chat for each big task. A chat that is too long makes the AI forget things.

The AI will sometimes be wrong about South African law, prices, and forms. It is a writing helper, not a lawyer. Check anything legal against the official website named in this toolkit.

### If the legal words are hard

Some paragraphs are written close to the law so you can check them. Each one is followed by a line starting **In plain words:** that says the same thing simply. Read that one instead if you need to.

Each heavy file also starts with a table of the words it uses. And [Glossary](#doc-glossary) explains every abbreviation.

### About the numbers in this toolkit

Tax rules, fees, and thresholds were checked in September 2026. The tax year running now is 1 March 2026 to 28 February 2027.

South African tax thresholds changed a lot in the 2026 Budget. Older blogs and older AI answers still use the old numbers. If a website tells you the VAT threshold is R1 million, that site is out of date.

Before you pay anything or file anything, check the current figure on the official site:

- SARS: www.sars.gov.za
- Company registration: www.bizportal.gov.za
- Data protection: inforegulator.org.za
- Vehicles: www.natis.gov.za

This toolkit is general information. It is not legal advice or tax advice. For anything large or unusual, pay a registered tax practitioner for one hour of their time. One hour is cheaper than one penalty.

---

<a id="doc-register-what-you-actually-need"></a>

## Register: what you actually need

Most advice tells you to register a company first. For a one-person business earning under about R500,000 a year, that is usually the wrong first move.

Read this before you spend money.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Sole proprietor | You trade as yourself. No company. The business and you are the same person in law. |
| Pty Ltd | A registered company. A separate legal person from you. |
| CIPC | The government office that registers companies. |
| Surety | Signing to say you will personally pay a debt if the company cannot. |
| POPIA | The law about protecting customers' personal information. |
| B-BBEE affidavit | A free sworn statement of your turnover and ownership, signed at a police station. |

---

### Two ways to trade

#### Sole proprietor (trading as yourself)

You trade under your own name and your own ID number. There is no separate business to register. You can start today.

You can use a trading name like "Thabo's Plumbing". You do not register that name anywhere. You just use it on your invoices and your signage.

What this means in practice:

The business and you are the same person in law. Business income goes on your personal tax return. If the business owes money, you owe it. Your house and your car can be taken to pay a business debt. There is no protection.

Cost to set up: R0.

#### Private company (Pty) Ltd

The company is a separate legal person. It has its own registration number. It can own things, sign contracts, and be sued on its own.

If the company owes money, generally the company owes it, not you. This is the main reason to register one. But this protection breaks if you sign personal surety (most banks and landlords will demand it) or if you trade while unable to pay your debts.

Cost to set up: R125 on BizPortal without a name, or R175 if you reserve a name.

Cost to keep alive every year: much more. You must file an annual return with CIPC, a beneficial ownership filing, and an annual company tax return. Most owners pay an accountant for this. One South African accounting firm puts the realistic first-year all-in cost at R2,500 to R3,500, and ongoing compliance at R15,000 to R40,000 a year. You can do it yourself for much less, but it takes real effort.

A company that earns nothing still has to file. A dormant company that nobody files for builds up penalties and eventually gets deregistered.

### Which one should you choose

Stay a sole proprietor if:

- You sell to the public or to small businesses
- Your work cannot easily cause big financial damage to someone else
- You earn under roughly R500,000 a year
- Nobody has asked you for a company registration number

Register a Pty if:

- Corporate clients or government refuse to buy from an individual (this is common)
- You want to bid for tenders
- Your work could cause serious loss or injury, so you want a legal wall between the business and your house
- You need to register a vehicle in the business name (see [Vehicles for your business](#doc-vehicles-for-your-business))
- You will bring in a partner or investor later

Before you register, read [Running a Pty Ltd](#doc-running-a-pty-ltd). It sets out every filing a company owes each year, and what happens when those are missed. Read it first, not after registering.

#### Two trade-offs nobody mentions

**A company may give you a safety net a sole proprietor cannot have.** A sole proprietor cannot contribute to UIF and is not an employee under COIDA. A working director on a company payroll generally is an employee for COIDA, and may be for UIF. That is access to injury-on-duty cover, and possibly an illness benefit, that no sole proprietor can buy through this route.

**A company loses you the free court.** Section 7(1) of the Small Claims Courts Act 61 of 1984 says only a natural person may institute an action, and a juristic person may take part only as a defendant. If a customer owes your sole proprietorship R25,000 you can go to the Small Claims Court yourself, free, with no lawyer. If they owe your Pty the same amount, you must instruct an attorney and use the magistrates' court. Your company can still be sued there; the disadvantage runs one way.

Both are set out in [You are the business](#doc-you-are-the-business).

You can start as a sole proprietor and register a company later. That is normal and easy. Starting the other way around, with a company you cannot afford to keep compliant, is the expensive mistake.

### How to register a company yourself

Do not pay an agent for this. Agents charge several times the CIPC fee for a form you can complete yourself in an afternoon.

Go to www.bizportal.gov.za. You need a valid South African ID number.

Registration on BizPortal costs R175 in total: R50 for the name reservation and R125 for the incorporation itself. If you skip the name and register with your enterprise number, it is R125.

Steps:

1. Create a BizPortal account with your ID number.
2. Choose to register a private company.
3. Either propose up to four names, or skip the name. CIPC checks proposed names one at a time and registers the first one that clears. If you skip, your company is named after its registration number, like 2026/123456/07. You can add a name later, and you can still trade under any trading name you like.
4. Enter director details exactly as they appear on the ID. One digit wrong and the Home Affairs check fails.
5. Enter the company address and choose a financial year end. Choose February so it matches the tax year.
6. Pay by card.

Your registration certificate, form CoR 14.3, and Memorandum of Incorporation arrive by email as PDF attachments, and your income tax number is issued automatically by SARS at the same time.

After registration, two things are easy to miss, and both are free:

- Confirm the SARS registered representative (the public officer). Without this you cannot use eFiling for the company.
- File the beneficial ownership information with CIPC. This is a legal requirement and it is free.

### Choosing the name

If your name is already registered and decided, skip to [Already have your name (shortcut)](#doc-already-have-your-name-shortcut). It lists the three checks still worth doing.

Before you fall in love with a name, check three things in this order:

1. Search the name on the CIPC website or BizPortal. If a similar registered company exists, your name may be refused.
2. Check the domain. Go to any South African domain seller and see if `yourname.co.za` is free. A `.co.za` domain is cheap, usually well under R200 a year, but prices differ between sellers so compare two or three. Buy it even if you will not build a website yet.
3. Check Instagram, Facebook, and TikTok for the handle.

A trademark is different from a company name. A company name stops nobody from using the same brand. A trademark does. Trademarks cost several thousand rand and take a long time. Do not do this in year one unless your brand is the whole product.

### Business bank account

This is the one thing every business should do, even a sole proprietor with no registration.

Use a separate account for business money. Not a separate wallet. A separate bank account.

Why it matters: when SARS asks how much you earned, you need one account with only business money in it. Mixing home and business money turns one hour of bookkeeping into two days.

A sole proprietor can use a second personal account. It is legal and cheap. You can only get a business account in a business name if you have a registered company.

When comparing accounts, compare these four things and ignore the marketing:

- Monthly account fee
- Cost to deposit cash (this is the big hidden cost if customers pay cash)
- Cost per EFT and per card swipe
- Whether a card machine is included, and what percentage it takes

Ask your bank for their current business pricing guide. Do not trust a blog for bank fees.

### POPIA: register your information officer

This one surprises people. It is free and most solo owners have never heard of it.

POPIA is South Africa's data protection law. If you keep any customer information at all, names, phone numbers, addresses, it applies to you.

POPIA makes it compulsory for all private and public bodies to register an Information Officer with the Information Regulator. Neither the law nor the guidance note allows any exemption, and the Information Officer may only take up duties after being registered.

For a one-person business, the information officer is you.

Register on the Information Regulator portal at inforegulator.org.za. It costs nothing.

The Information Regulator is enforcing POPIA with administrative fines of up to R10 million. Small businesses are not the main target, but registration takes under an hour and removes the risk entirely.

You also need a short privacy notice telling customers what data you collect and why. There is a template in [Which template to use when](#doc-which-template-to-use-when).

### B-BBEE affidavit: free, do not pay for it

If your turnover is under R10 million a year, you are an Exempted Micro Enterprise (EME).

All EMEs are exempt from B-BBEE compliance and receive automatic B-BBEE Recognition Level 4. An EME that is at least 51% black owned automatically receives Level 2 status, and one that is 100% black owned receives Level 1. EMEs only need a sworn affidavit or a CIPC certificate.

The sworn affidavit is free. It is a standard template signed in front of a Commissioner of Oaths, confirming your turnover and your black ownership. The CIPC also issues free B-BBEE certificates to EMEs. A Commissioner of Oaths is available free at any police station.

The affidavit is valid for twelve months from the date it is signed. Set a reminder to renew it.

Do not pay a consultant for a B-BBEE certificate while you are under R10 million. Some sectors, including construction, ICT, and tourism, have their own sector codes with different rules, so check your sector code if you work in one of those.

### Things you can skip at the start

Selling to government (CSD registration). Only register on the Central Supplier Database if you are actually going to bid for tenders. It is free but it is admin.

UIF and COIDA. A sole proprietor with no staff does not register, and in fact cannot. The Unemployment Insurance Contributions Act excludes independent contractors from its definition of employee, and a sole proprietor is not an employee under COIDA either. That cuts both ways: it also means no illness benefit and no injury-on-duty cover, ever.

With a company it is different. If your Pty pays you a salary as a director, you are generally an employee of your own company, and PAYE, UIF and COIDA obligations start. Read that as a trade rather than a catch: you take on the admin, and you get cover a sole proprietor cannot buy through this route. See [You are the business](#doc-you-are-the-business). There are three legal ways to take money out of a company, each taxed differently. See [Paying yourself from a Pty Ltd](#doc-paying-yourself-from-a-pty-ltd) before you pay yourself the first time.

A business plan. You only need one to raise money from a bank or investor. Writing a 30-page plan for yourself is procrastination dressed as work.

A website. A Google Business Profile plus a WhatsApp Business number gets most local businesses their first hundred customers. Build the site when you have something to put on it.

A logo designer. Use the prompts in [Branding prompts](#doc-branding-prompts) and a free tool. Spend money on a designer when the brand starts earning.

A company stamp. It has no legal meaning in South Africa. Nobody needs one.

### Your registration checklist

Required for everyone:

- Separate bank account for business money
- SARS registration and filing (see [Tax and SARS](#doc-tax-and-sars))
- POPIA information officer registration
- Municipal licence, if your business type needs one (see [What you need to sell things](#doc-what-you-need-to-sell-things))

Required only in some cases:

- CIPC company registration
- VAT registration (only over R2.3 million turnover)
- CSD registration (only for government tenders)
- B-BBEE affidavit (only when a customer asks for it, and it is free)

---

<a id="doc-tax-and-sars"></a>

## Tax and SARS

Numbers here are for the tax year 1 March 2026 to 28 February 2027. They changed a lot in the February 2026 Budget. Check www.sars.gov.za before you file.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Turnover | Total sales, before costs. |
| Taxable income | Profit after allowed costs. Tax is worked out on this. |
| Provisional tax | Paying tax in advance, twice a year, because no employer deducts it for you. |
| IRP6 | The short form you fill in for each provisional tax payment. |
| ITR12 / ITR14 | The yearly tax return. ITR12 for people, ITR14 for companies. |
| Turnover tax | A simple tax for small businesses, charged on sales instead of profit. |
| VAT | 15% tax added to sales, only by businesses registered for it. |
| Deduction | A cost you may subtract from income before tax is worked out. |

---

### The short version

If your business earns under R99,000 profit a year and you have no other income, you pay no income tax. You may still have to file a return showing zero.

If your business turns over under R2.3 million a year, you do not have to register for VAT.

If your business turns over under R600,000 a year, there is a system called turnover tax where you pay 0%.

So a small solo business can legally pay no tax at all. The danger is not tax. The danger is not filing.

### What SARS wants from a sole proprietor

You do not register the business with SARS. There is no business to register. You register yourself.

Most South Africans already have an income tax number from a past job. If you do not, register on SARS eFiling at www.sarsefiling.co.za, or use the SARS MobiApp.

Business profit goes on your normal personal tax return, the ITR12, in the "local business income" section. You declare income and subtract expenses. The answer is your profit, and it is added to any salary or other income you have.

#### Provisional tax

Because nobody deducts PAYE from your business income, SARS wants payment during the year, not all at the end.

If you own a business, the Income Tax Act says you must register as a provisional taxpayer. This applies to a sole trader, a partner in a partnership, a member of a CC, and a director of a company.

> **In plain words:** If you run any kind of business, you must pay tax in advance twice a year. This is called being a provisional taxpayer. It applies whether you trade as yourself or through a company.

Provisional taxpayers submit two IRP6 returns during the tax year. The first is due within the first six months of the year and the second at the end of the year of assessment. There is also an optional third top-up payment before you submit your annual ITR12.

> **In plain words:** Twice a year you fill in a short form (the IRP6) that estimates your income and pay tax on it. Once by the end of August, once by the end of February. A third payment in September is optional.

In practice your dates are:

- First IRP6: by 31 August
- Second IRP6: by 28 or 29 February, or the business day before if that is a weekend
- Optional third payment: by 30 September
- Annual ITR12: during filing season, which usually opens in July; SARS publishes the closing dates each year

Important relief: a natural person who does not carry on a business does not have to pay provisional tax if taxable income stays below the tax threshold for the year. If you do carry on a business and your total taxable income stays under the threshold, there is no tax to pay, but the safe approach is still to submit a nil IRP6 rather than submit nothing. An IRP6 return can be requested and submitted even if the provisional tax payment is nil.

Filing a zero return costs nothing. Not filing creates penalties that grow every month.

### What SARS wants from a company

A company is registered for income tax automatically when CIPC registers it. You still need to confirm the registered representative on eFiling before you can do anything.

A company is automatically a provisional taxpayer. It files two IRP6 returns and one ITR14 annual return.

This applies even if the company earned nothing. A dormant company still files a nil ITR14 with SARS and a nil annual return with CIPC. This is the single most common way small owners build up debt to the state without noticing.

### How to pay little or no tax, legally

There are four legal routes. They are different, and you must choose the right one.

#### Route 1: stay under the tax threshold

For the 2026/2027 year, if you are under 65 you owe no income tax on earnings below R99,000 per year. The threshold is R153,250 for ages 65 to 74, and R171,300 for 75 and older.

This is taxable income, which means profit after expenses, not sales. A business with R250,000 sales and R160,000 real costs has R90,000 profit and pays nothing.

Careful: if you also have a salary, the salary is added to the profit. The threshold applies to the total, not to the business alone.

#### Route 2: claim every real expense

This is the biggest, most ignored lever for a sole proprietor. Every rand of legitimate business cost reduces taxable profit.

Things you can usually claim if they are genuinely for the business:

- Stock and materials
- Data, airtime, and internet used for work
- Tools and equipment, written off over their useful life
- Business travel, using a logbook (see [Vehicles for your business](#doc-vehicles-for-your-business))
- Bank charges on the business account
- Advertising, printing, and website costs
- Accounting and legal fees
- Rent of business premises
- A portion of home costs if you use part of your home for work

The home office deduction has strict rules. SARS generally expects a specific part of the home used regularly and exclusively for the trade, and properly equipped for it. If you work at the kitchen table, you do not qualify. If you have a converted room used only for work, you can claim a share of rent or bond interest, rates, and electricity based on floor area. Keep a photo and a floor measurement on file.

Two hard rules: the expense must be for the business, and you must be able to prove it. No slip, no claim. Take a photo of every slip the day you get it.

#### Route 3: turnover tax

This changed in 2026 and it is now a very good option for many small businesses.

SARS increased the turnover tax annual turnover limit from R1 million to R2.3 million, effective 1 April 2026, and the tax-free band for 2026/2027 is R0 to R600,000.

The rates for the 2026/2027 year:

| Turnover | Rate on the amount in that band |
|---|---|
| R0 to R600,000 | 0% |
| R600,001 to R950,000 | 1% |
| R950,001 to R1,400,000 | 2% |
| R1,400,001 to R2,300,000 | 3% |

For a business with R800,000 turnover: the first R600,000 is taxed at 0%, and the next R200,000 at 1%, so the total turnover tax for the year is R2,000.

What makes it attractive: turnover tax replaces income tax, VAT, provisional tax, capital gains tax, and dividends tax, so you pay a single tax instead of several. Record-keeping is minimal. You only track amounts received, dividends declared, and assets or liabilities over R10,000.

What makes it risky: you cannot claim any expense deductions. Costs like rent, stock purchases, and equipment cannot be set against the amount taxed, which may mean paying more tax than under the normal system.

Because tax is on turnover and not profit, turnover tax suits a business with high margins and low costs. A consultant, a service business, or a maker with cheap inputs does well. A business that buys and resells goods at a small markup does badly. A shop with R900,000 sales and R750,000 cost of stock has R150,000 profit and would pay R3,000 in turnover tax, which is more than the nothing it would pay under normal tax.

Who cannot use it: professional service providers, labour brokers, personal service providers, and certain other excluded activities. A business is excluded where more than 20% of income comes from professional services. That exclusion catches accountants, lawyers, engineers, architects, and similar. Check the SARS list for your trade before you rely on this.

How to register: through SARS eFiling or at a SARS branch. You must elect to be a micro business at the start of your year of assessment, and once registered you stay in the system unless you deregister voluntarily or exceed R2.3 million.

> **In plain words:** You choose turnover tax at the start of the tax year, on eFiling or at a branch. Once you are in, you stay in until you choose to leave or your sales go over R2.3 million.

Sole proprietors, partnerships, close corporations, companies, and co-operatives can all register, so this works whether or not you have a Pty.

Work out both options on your actual numbers before choosing. If your costs are more than about 25% of your sales, normal tax is usually better.

#### Route 4: small business corporation rates (companies only)

If you registered a Pty, check whether it qualifies as a Small Business Corporation. A qualifying SBC pays on a sliding scale starting at 0% on the first R99,000 of taxable income, instead of the flat 27% company rate.

To qualify, every year: all shareholders must be natural persons, gross income must not exceed R20 million, no shareholder may hold shares in another company apart from limited exceptions, no more than 20% of gross income may come from investment income or personal services, and the company must not be a personal service provider.

> **In plain words:** For a company to get the lower tax rates, every year: all owners must be people, not other companies. Sales must be under R20 million. You must not own shares in another company. And most of the income must come from real trade, not from investments or from one person's personal services.

The condition that disqualifies most companies is a shareholder holding shares in another company. If you own shares in any other Pty, even a dormant one you forgot about, your new company may lose SBC status.

SBC status is not automatic. If any condition is missed, the standard 27% rate applies for that year. Do not tick "yes" on the ITR14 just because the company feels small.

#### A fifth option if you have a salary as well

If you have a job and run a business on the side, a loss in the business can generally reduce your total taxable income in that year. This is real and legal.

There is a limit. Section 20A of the Income Tax Act can "ring-fence" losses from certain trades, especially where you are a high earner and the activity keeps losing money year after year, or where it looks like a hobby. If you plan to use business losses against a salary, get an hour of advice from a tax practitioner first.

#### Retirement contributions

Contributions to a retirement annuity are deductible, generally up to 27.5% of the greater of remuneration or taxable income, capped in rands. The 2026 Budget increased the retirement contribution cap to R430,000 per year, up from R350,000.

This only helps if you have taxable income to reduce and cash to spare. It is not useful in a year where you earn under the threshold.

### VAT: probably not yet

From 1 April 2026, the compulsory VAT registration threshold increased from R1 million to R2.3 million. Small businesses only need to register for VAT once annual turnover exceeds R2.3 million.

The voluntary registration threshold increased from R50,000 to R120,000 per year.

You must register within 21 business days from the date you exceed R2.3 million, or from the date it becomes clear you will exceed it. The threshold is measured over any rolling 12 months, not over a calendar year. Check your last 12 months every month once you pass about R1.5 million.

Should you register voluntarily once you pass R120,000? Usually no.

If your customers are the public, registering makes you 15% more expensive overnight, or cuts your margin by 15% if you absorb it. You also take on VAT returns every two months, forever.

If your customers are VAT-registered businesses, the 15% costs them nothing because they claim it back, and you get to claim VAT on your own purchases. Then it can be worth it.

The honest test: add up the VAT you would claim back on stock, equipment, and rent in a year. If that number is smaller than the cost of your time doing bi-monthly returns plus the accounting fee, do not register.

If you should be registered and you are not, SARS will backdate your registration to the date you became liable, and you will owe VAT on all taxable supplies from that date plus a 10% late payment penalty and interest. Deliberate non-registration can also be a criminal offence.

> **In plain words:** If you should have registered for VAT but did not, SARS will treat you as registered from the day you crossed the line. You will owe 15% on everything you sold since then, even though you never charged it, plus a penalty and interest.

Until you are registered: do not write "Tax Invoice" on your invoices, do not show a VAT line, and do not charge VAT. Charging VAT you are not registered for is fraud. See [Which template to use when](#doc-which-template-to-use-when) for a correct non-VAT invoice.

### Records

Keep everything for five years from the date you submit the return.

The minimum that works for a one-person business:

- One bank account used only for business
- A folder of photographed slips, one folder per month
- A simple spreadsheet with date, description, money in, money out, category
- Copies of every invoice you issued, numbered in order with no gaps

That spreadsheet is the whole system. You do not need accounting software in year one.

If you use turnover tax, the records are even lighter. You track amounts received, dividends declared, and assets or liabilities over R10,000.

### Your tax year calendar

- 1 March: new tax year starts
- 31 August: first provisional return (IRP6) due
- 30 September: optional third provisional payment for the previous year
- 28/29 February: tax year ends, second provisional return (IRP6) due
- Filing season for the annual return (ITR12): SARS gazettes the dates each year. For the 2026 year the deadlines are 23 October 2026 for people who are not provisional taxpayers, and 22 January 2027 for provisional taxpayers. Confirm each year on sars.gov.za.

One rule that catches people: if a SARS deadline falls on a weekend or public holiday, it moves to the business day before, not after. 28 February 2027 is a Sunday, so the second provisional return for that year is due on Friday 26 February 2027.
- Company annual return to CIPC: due each year on the anniversary of registration
- B-BBEE affidavit: renew 12 months after signing

Put all of these in your phone calendar today with a two-week warning.

### Two things that are not legal

Taking cash and not declaring it. SARS receives data from banks, card machine providers, and crypto exchanges. The risk is not worth it.

Claiming personal spending as business expenses. Groceries, family holidays, and a partner's phone are not deductible. If SARS audits and disallows them, you pay the tax plus penalties plus interest.

---

<a id="doc-what-you-need-to-sell-things"></a>

## What you need to sell things

Some businesses may not trade until they hold a licence. Others need nothing. This file tells you which one you are.

Start with your municipality, not with Google. Rules differ between Cape Town, Johannesburg, eThekwini, and every small town. The person you want is the Environmental Health Practitioner or the Business Licensing Officer at your local municipal office.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Municipality | Your local council. It issues business licences. |
| Licence | Official permission to trade from a specific building. |
| COA | Certificate of Acceptability. The permit every food business must have. |
| EHP | The municipal health inspector. |
| CPA | Consumer Protection Act. The law giving customers rights you cannot remove. |
| ECTA | The law for selling online. |
| Cooling-off | The customer's right to cancel and get a refund without a reason. |
| Implied warranty | A guarantee the law adds to every sale, even if you never wrote one. |

---

### The general rule

Business and trade licence requirements are governed by the Business Act 71 of 1991. No person may carry on certain service-related businesses unless they hold a licence issued by the council, local authority, or municipality where the business premises are situated.

> **In plain words:** Some kinds of business need a licence from the local municipality before they may open. The licence is for the building where you trade, not for you personally.

The licence is tied to the premises, not to you. If you move, you apply again.

You may not begin trading in a business that requires a licence before the licence has been issued. Trading without a valid licence is punishable with a fine.

> **In plain words:** You cannot open until the licence is in your hand. Opening early can get you fined.

The categories that typically require a municipal trading licence include food retail and food service (restaurants, take-aways, cafés, street food vendors, catering, spaza shops) and health establishments (health, beauty, or body treatment services, including hairdressers, barbers, beauty salons, nail salons, and tattoo studios).

> **In plain words:** The two big groups that need a municipal licence are anyone selling or preparing food, and anyone working on people's bodies: hair, nails, beauty, massage, tattoos.

Entertainment venues, cinemas, night clubs, and businesses that hire out equipment also usually fall under it. Check your local by-laws.

### If you sell food

This is the most regulated area for small businesses, and the one people most often get wrong.

You need a Certificate of Acceptability (COA).

Regulation R638 of 2018 sets general hygiene requirements for food premises and requires all processed food handling businesses that sell food for profit to hold a Certificate of Acceptability.

The COA applies to any food premises where food is handled, prepared, stored, transported, or sold to the public. It applies to a restaurant, a spaza shop, a catering company, and a lodge kitchen equally.

How to get it:

Contact the Environmental Health Department of the municipality where your premises are located and request the COA application form. An Environmental Health Practitioner (EHP) will inspect the premises to check compliance with R638. If you comply, the COA is issued. If not, the EHP gives you a list of required improvements.

> **In plain words:** Ask your municipality's health department for the food certificate form. A health inspector will visit. If your kitchen passes, you get the certificate. If not, they give you a list of things to fix.

The thing that fails most applications: training under R638 Regulation 10 is not optional. You cannot get a COA without accredited food safety training for the person in charge and every food handler.

Other things a food business may need, depending on what you do:

- A business licence under the Business Act 71 of 1991
- A zoning certificate, if your premises are not zoned for business use
- A fire compliance certificate (SANS 10400-T)
- A gas certificate of compliance, if you cook with gas
- A liquor licence, if you sell alcohol

Realistically, expect two to six months depending on how quickly your municipality moves and how ready your premises are. CIPC registration takes days, training one to three weeks, premises preparation two to eight weeks, and the COA application two to eight weeks. A liquor licence alone can add three to twelve months.

> **In plain words:** Getting a food business legal takes two to six months from start to finish. Alcohol takes much longer. Do not sign a lease expecting to open next month.

Selling from home is possible but watched closely. You generally need zoning approval and a kitchen area separated from the family living space.

The COA is specific to the premises and cannot be transferred to a new location or a new owner. It has no fixed expiry under R638, but it becomes void when key conditions change.

Food labels are a separate rule set. Regulation R146 of 2012 governs the labelling and advertising of foodstuffs, including allergen warnings. If you package anything, read R146 before you print labels.

One exception worth knowing: unprocessed agricultural products sold as they come out of the ground are treated differently from processed food. If you only sell raw produce, confirm your position with your municipality rather than assuming.

### If you sell beauty or body treatments

Hairdressing, nails, beauty therapy, massage, and tattooing are health establishments under the Business Act. You need a municipal licence and usually a health inspection.

Tattoo and piercing studios face extra scrutiny on sharps disposal and sterilisation. Speak to your EHP before you sign a lease.

If you make or import cosmetics, the Foodstuffs, Cosmetics and Disinfectants Act 54 of 1972 applies to the product itself. Labelling and ingredient claims are regulated. Do not claim a product treats a medical condition unless you are prepared for it to be classified as a medicine.

### If you sell alcohol

You need a liquor licence from your provincial liquor authority, not from the municipality. Each province has its own board and its own forms.

This is slow and it is not cheap. Budget months, not weeks. You cannot sell alcohol while the application is pending.

### If you sell medicines, supplements, or health products

SAHPRA regulates medicines, complementary medicines, and medical devices. Selling scheduled medicines requires a licensed pharmacy or a licensed dispenser.

Health supplements are in a grey area that is getting stricter. If your product makes a health claim, check SAHPRA's position before you launch.

### If you sell second-hand goods

The Second-Hand Goods Act 6 of 2009 applies. Dealers in used goods, including scrap metal and used electronics, must register with SAPS and keep a register of what they buy, from whom, and when.

This catches more people than expected. If you buy used phones to resell, this applies to you.

### If you sell online

Two laws apply on top of everything above: the Consumer Protection Act 68 of 2008 and the Electronic Communications and Transactions Act 25 of 2002 (ECTA).

Your website or online shop must show the information listed in ECTA section 43. Section 43 requires suppliers to disclose the full price of goods or services including additional costs, and to provide information on their legal status and physical address.

At minimum, put this on your site:

- Your full legal name and, if registered, your company registration number
- A physical address (not only a PO box)
- An email address and phone number
- The full price, including delivery and any other cost
- Your returns and refund policy
- Your privacy notice

Your checkout must include a step where the customer can review the entire order, including products, quantities, pricing, delivery costs, and terms, and correct any errors before finalising. This satisfies section 43(2). Failing to provide it gives the consumer a 14-day cancellation right under section 43(3).

> **In plain words:** Before the customer pays, your website must show them a summary of everything they are buying, with all costs, and let them fix mistakes. If you skip this step, the customer gets 14 days to cancel.

The cooling-off right:

Section 44 of ECTA lets a consumer cancel an electronic transaction without reason and without penalty within seven days after receiving the goods, or after the agreement for services was concluded. It applies where the consumer is a natural person, regardless of the transaction value, subject to the exclusions in section 42(2).

> **In plain words:** A person who buys from your website can change their mind and send it back within seven days for a full refund. They do not need a reason. This applies to any amount.

The only charge you may pass to the consumer for the cancellation is the direct cost of returning the goods.

The seven-day window is statutory and cannot be contracted out of. A "no refunds" sign on an online store is unenforceable against a cooling-off claim. Do not copy a shop-front returns policy onto a website.

There are exceptions. For services the cooling-off right lapses as soon as the services are used, and certain transactions are excluded, including some financial services and auctions.

Delivery timing: under section 46, you must execute the order within 30 days of receiving it unless you agreed otherwise with the customer. If you cannot perform because the goods are unavailable, you must notify the customer immediately and refund any payment within 30 days of that notification.

> **In plain words:** You must deliver within 30 days unless you agreed a different time with the customer. If you cannot deliver at all, tell them immediately and refund them within 30 days.

Practical point: if you promise delivery in 3 days on your product page, that promise becomes your agreement. Promise what you can actually do.

### If you import anything

You need an importer's code from SARS Customs. Applying is free through the SARS website, and it is separate from your income tax registration.

You will pay import duty plus 15% VAT on the landed value at the border, even if you are not VAT registered. That VAT is a real cost to you if you cannot claim it back, so build it into your pricing before you order.

Some goods need a Letter of Authority from the National Regulator for Compulsory Specifications (NRCS) before they may be sold in South Africa. This covers many electrical products, lighting, and some automotive parts. Importers get caught by this constantly. Check before you order a container.

### If you sell to government

You need to register on the Central Supplier Database at www.csd.gov.za. It is free.

You also need a valid tax compliance status with SARS, and a B-BBEE affidavit or certificate (see [Register: what you actually need](#doc-register-what-you-actually-need)).

### If you provide professional or regulated services
