Some work may only be done by registered professionals. Common examples: electricians issuing certificates of compliance, plumbers, gas installers, engineers, estate agents, financial advisors, security services, and health professionals.

If your trade has a professional body, registration with that body is usually not optional. Working without it can void your customer's insurance as well as expose you.

### If none of the above applies

Many businesses need no licence at all. Consulting, design, writing, software, tutoring, photography, admin services, bookkeeping, cleaning, gardening, and most crafts fall here.

You still need the general obligations: SARS, POPIA, and honest advertising under the Consumer Protection Act.

### Rules that apply to everyone selling anything

The Consumer Protection Act gives your customers rights you cannot sign away.

Consumers are entitled to goods that are reasonably suitable for their ordinary purpose, of good quality and in good working order, free from defects, reasonably durable, and compliant with applicable standards.

There is a six-month implied warranty on goods. If goods fail within six months, the customer can generally demand a repair, replacement, or refund, and the choice is usually theirs, not yours.

Your advertising must be true. Do not claim a result you cannot deliver.

Your prices must be displayed. If two prices are displayed, the lower one generally applies.

### Your licence checklist

Work through these questions before you take your first order:

1. Does my business type appear in the Business Act categories? If yes, apply for the municipal licence first.
2. Do I handle food? If yes, get accredited training, then apply for the COA.
3. Is my premises zoned for this activity? Ask the municipal town planning department.
4. Does my product need a Letter of Authority or SAHPRA approval?
5. Am I selling online? If yes, put the ECTA section 43 information on the site and write a returns policy that respects the seven-day rule.
6. Does my trade have a professional body I must register with?

If you answered no to all six, you can trade today.

---

<a id="doc-vehicles-for-your-business"></a>

## Vehicles and your business

Most one-person businesses should not put the vehicle in the business name. Read the tax section before you do the paperwork.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| eNaTIS | The national computer system that records every vehicle and its owner. |
| BRNC | The certificate that lets a company own vehicles. Like an ID number for the company on eNaTIS. |
| Traffic register number | The same idea, for a business that is not a company. |
| Proxy | The one person allowed to do vehicle paperwork for the company. |
| Registering authority | The traffic department or licensing office. |
| Fringe benefit | A perk from your company, like private use of a company car, that SARS taxes as income. |
| Logbook | Your written record of business kilometres. SARS will not allow a travel claim without it. |

---

### If you are a sole proprietor

You have two routes, and for most people the first one is correct.

#### Route 1: register it against your own ID

There is no separate business in law, so the vehicle is registered against your ID number. This is normal and it is not a problem to solve.

You still claim the business use of the vehicle against your tax. You do that with a logbook, not with the registration.

For a single owner-operator with one vehicle, stop here. This is the right answer.

#### Route 2: apply for a traffic register number

A traffic register number lets a non-legal person appear on the vehicle record instead of only a personal ID.

If you are an organisation that serves as a non-legal person, for example a partnership, a one-man business, a municipal, provincial or government authority, a church, congregation or welfare organisation, a recreational organisation, an educational organisation, a trust or a statutory body, you must apply for a traffic register number to register motor vehicles in South Africa.

> **In plain words:** If you trade under a business name but are not a registered company (for example a one-person business or a partnership), you can still get a special number from the traffic department so vehicles can be registered under that name.

Apply at your registering authority. Bring proof of address, ID photographs, and the prescribed fee.

This is worth doing if you run several vehicles under a trading name, or if you deal in vehicles. It is unnecessary admin if you have one bakkie.

### If you have a registered company

A company has no ID number, so the traffic system needs another way to identify it.

eNaTIS, the national traffic register, identifies every vehicle owner by an identity number. A person uses their SA ID. A company does not have one, so the registering authority issues it a business register number the first time it needs to own a vehicle. The Business Register Number Certificate (BRNC) is the document recording that number, issued at your local traffic department under the National Road Traffic Act, 1996.

> **In plain words:** The vehicle system needs an ID number for every owner. A company has no ID number, so the traffic department gives it one. The certificate showing that number is the BRNC. It is a different document from your CIPC certificate, and you need both.

It is not your CIPC registration certificate. The CoR 14.3 proves your company exists. The BRNC lets it own vehicles. The counter will ask for both.

If a vehicle is to be registered in the name of a business, the business must first apply for a BRNC. A copy of the BRNC must be submitted with all vehicle transactions such as licence renewals and transfers of ownership.

> **In plain words:** Get the BRNC first. After that, every time you renew a disc or sell a vehicle, you bring a copy of the BRNC along.

A trust or other non-legal-person organisation uses a Traffic Register Number Certificate instead, following the same idea.

#### You must nominate a proxy

A company cannot stand in a queue. One named human acts for it.

A proxy is a person authorised to act on behalf of a company or trust for vehicle transactions. The proxy's details are recorded on the eNaTIS system, and a copy of their ID must be submitted with the BRNC whenever you renew a licence or change ownership.

> **In plain words:** A proxy is the one named human who is allowed to do vehicle paperwork for the company. Their name is recorded on the system, and they bring their ID every time.

Only directors, company secretaries, or trustees may act as proxy. For a one-person company, that is you.

Consequence to plan for: only the proxy or representative can renew the licence or handle transactions on behalf of the company. If you are away when the disc expires, someone must be formally authorised, or the renewal waits.

#### How to get the BRNC

There is no online option. eNaTIS has no public portal for business register numbers, so a counter visit is unavoidable. The proxy should go in person with their original ID.

What to bring:

The Application for Business Registration (ABR) form, available at your local traffic department. Proof of business address such as a utility bill or bank statement not older than three months. An application letter on your business letterhead, signed by a director. A certified copy of the proxy's ID. Your CIPC incorporation documents. A proxy appointment letter or resolution authorising the proxy to act for the company. Your SARS VAT registration certificate, but only if you are VAT registered.

> **In plain words:** Bring: the ABR form (get it at the traffic department), proof of the business address under 3 months old, a signed letter on your letterhead, a certified copy of the proxy's ID, your CIPC documents, a letter or resolution naming the proxy, and your VAT certificate only if you are VAT registered.

The proxy's ID and the CIPC documents need certified copies stamped within the last 3 months. Any police station, post office, or commissioner of oaths does this free.

Two letters on your letterhead trip up most applicants: the proxy nomination and the BRNC request. Write both, sign both, bring both.

#### Cost and time

There is no published national fee. Each registering authority sets its own admin charge, and reported figures range from under R100 to around R300 at the counter.

Processing typically takes 2 to 3 weeks, depending on the registering authority. Some offices issue the certificate on the spot, others take up to 3 weeks. Ask before you leave the counter and get a contact number to follow up.

#### Common reasons applications fail

Proof of address that is not FICA compliant. Outdated or uncertified copies. Errors on the ABR form, such as an incorrect registration number or a mismatched address. Appointing someone who is not a director, company secretary, or trustee as proxy. Missing CIPC documents.

> **In plain words:** The usual reasons for rejection: proof of address the bank would not accept, copies that are old or not certified, mistakes on the form, naming someone who is not a director as proxy, or missing CIPC papers.

Errors on the ABR form cannot be corrected once submitted. You would have to restart the process. Check every field twice before you hand it over.

#### After you have the BRNC

Once the BRNC exists, you register the vehicle itself using form RLV (Application for Registration and Licensing of Motor Vehicle), together with the BRNC, the proxy's ID, and the seller's documents.

One BRNC covers as many vehicles as the company owns. You do not repeat the process per vehicle.

Two things to check with your registering authority, because reports differ: whether the BRN is valid only in the province where it was issued, and what happens to registered vehicles if the company is later deregistered. Ask at the counter and write down the answer.

### The decision most people get wrong

Putting the vehicle in the company name feels like the professional choice. For a one-person business it is often the more expensive choice.

#### If the company owns the vehicle

The company can claim wear and tear on the vehicle, plus fuel, insurance, tyres, licence, and repairs.

But you are a director, and you will drive that vehicle privately at some point. Private use of a company vehicle is a taxable fringe benefit. SARS adds a monthly amount to your personal income based on the value of the vehicle, and you pay tax on it. The benefit is reduced if you keep a logbook proving business use, but you still have to do the logbook.

You also cannot claim a travel deduction on the same vehicle, because you do not own it.

#### If you own the vehicle personally

You claim business travel against your business income based on the kilometres you actually drove for work. You do this with a logbook.

There is no fringe benefit, because there is no company asset. The admin is one spreadsheet.

#### Which is better

For a single owner-operator who uses one vehicle for both work and home, personal ownership plus a logbook is usually simpler and often cheaper in total tax.

Company ownership starts to win when the vehicle is genuinely a work asset with little private use: a bakkie that lives at the yard, a delivery van, a vehicle with your branding on it that you would not take on holiday.

If you are choosing between the two on a vehicle worth more than about R200,000, pay a tax practitioner for one hour to run both scenarios on your actual numbers. The difference over five years is usually much bigger than the fee.

### The logbook

This is required by SARS for any vehicle travel claim. Without it, the claim is disallowed. A reconstructed logbook written the night before an audit does not survive.

Record for every business trip:

- Date
- Odometer reading at the start
- Odometer reading at the end
- Kilometres travelled
- Where you went
- Why you went (the business reason)

Record your odometer on 1 March and again on 28 or 29 February so you have total kilometres for the year.

Travel between your home and a fixed place of work is private travel, not business travel. Travel from home to a client site can be business travel. Keep the reason field honest.

The easiest system: a note in your phone, filled in the moment you park. There is a template in [Which template to use when](#doc-which-template-to-use-when).

### Other things to check

Insurance. Tell your insurer the vehicle is used for business. Private-use cover can be void on a business claim. "Business use" cover costs a little more and is worth it. If you carry goods for customers, ask specifically about goods in transit cover, which is usually separate.

Heavy and passenger vehicles. If the vehicle has a gross vehicle mass over 3,500 kg, or carries passengers for reward, extra rules apply: a Professional Driving Permit for the driver, annual roadworthy testing, and for passenger transport an operating licence from your provincial regulator. Do not start a taxi, shuttle, or courier business without checking these first.

Branding your vehicle. Vehicle signage is usually a deductible advertising cost and is often the cheapest advertising a local business can buy. Check your municipal by-laws for restrictions on signage size.

---

<a id="doc-running-a-pty-ltd"></a>

## Running a Pty Ltd

Read [Register: what you actually need](#doc-register-what-you-actually-need) first. That file helps you decide whether to register a company at all.

This file is for people who already have one, or who have decided to get one. It covers what a company costs you in time and duties every year, and what goes wrong when you ignore it.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Annual return | A yearly form telling CIPC your company still exists. Not a tax return. |
| Beneficial ownership | A free filing naming the real people who own the company. |
| Financial statements | The yearly summary of what the company earned, spent, owns and owes. |
| Public Interest Score | A points calculation that decides whether anyone must check your financial statements. |
| Audit | A full independent check of the financial statements. Expensive. Rarely needed for a small company. |
| Deregistration | CIPC closing your company because you stopped filing. |
| Solvency and liquidity test | The check a company must pass before paying out a dividend: it owns more than it owes, and can pay its bills for the next year. |
| Resolution | A written record of a decision. Needed even if you are the only director. |

---

The short version: registering a company takes one afternoon and R175. Keeping it alive and clean is the real work, and it never stops.

---

### What changes the moment you have a company

The company is a separate legal person. That is the whole point, and it has consequences in both directions.

The company owns the money, not you. Money in the company bank account is company money. You cannot simply spend it. There are three legal ways to take it out, and each has a tax consequence. See [Paying yourself from a Pty Ltd](#doc-paying-yourself-from-a-pty-ltd).

The company signs contracts, not you. Sign as "for and on behalf of [Company Name] (Pty) Ltd", with your name and "Director" underneath. If you sign your own name with no company reference, you may have bound yourself personally.

The company's name and registration number must appear on its documents. Section 32 of the Companies Act requires the registered name and registration number on invoices, receipts, delivery notes, letters, orders and notices, including electronic ones. If you market under a trading name, add "a trading name of [Registered Name] (Pty) Ltd, Reg. No. [number]". See [Adding new lines of business](#doc-adding-new-lines-of-business).

The company's debts are generally the company's. That protection is real but it is not absolute. It breaks in three common ways, covered below.

You now have two separate regulators. CIPC handles the company register. SARS handles tax. They do not talk to each other about your filings. Filing with one does not satisfy the other.

---

### Your annual duties, in order of how often they are missed

#### 1. CIPC annual return

This confirms the company still exists. It is not a tax return.

Companies file within 30 business days of their registration anniversary. It confirms the company is still active and is separate from your SARS tax return.

Unlike SARS deadlines which have fixed dates, CIPC annual returns are due on a rolling basis tied to your company's anniversary date, the date it was originally incorporated.

The fee scales with turnover. For companies the fee ranges from R100 for turnover under R1 million to R3,000 at R25 million or more when filed on time, with late filings costing R150 to R4,000.

All registered companies must file annually regardless of trading activity or turnover. A dormant company with zero turnover files at R100.

File at https://annualreturns.cipc.co.za. You need a CIPC customer code and an eServices login.

Keep the confirmation. Banks, tender adjudicators, and suppliers sometimes request it as proof of good standing.

#### 2. Beneficial ownership filing

This names the real humans behind the company. It came in as part of South Africa's response to the FATF grey listing.

It is free, and it blocks everything else if you skip it.

Since 1 July 2024, CIPC has hard-stopped annual return filing if beneficial ownership has not been filed. You cannot file your annual return without first filing beneficial ownership.

Non-compliance with beneficial ownership filing is now treated alongside annual return non-compliance and can independently trigger deregistration.

For a one-person company this takes minutes: you are the sole beneficial owner. Do it before you sit down to file the annual return, not during.

#### 3. Financial statements

You must prepare annual financial statements. Whether anyone independent has to check them depends on your Public Interest Score.

The PIS is a points calculation done at each financial year end. You get one point per employee averaged over the year, one point per R1 million or part thereof of turnover, one point per R1 million or part thereof of third-party liabilities, and points for the number of shareholders.

> **In plain words:** Add up points: one for each employee, one for each R1 million of sales, one for each R1 million you owe to others, and some for how many shareholders you have. A one-person company with modest sales scores very low.

For a one-person company with modest turnover, the score will be well under 100.

What that means:

A company with a Public Interest Score under 100 is exempt from both audit and independent review if every person who holds a beneficial interest in its shares is also a director. That is what "owner-managed" means.

> **In plain words:** If your score is under 100 and every shareholder is also a director, nobody outside the company has to check your financial statements. You still have to prepare them.

Owner-managed companies with 100 points or less are exempt completely from audit or review if they so choose, but they are still required to prepare financial statements.

So if you are the only shareholder and the only director, you almost certainly need a compilation, not an audit, and not an independent review.

Watch the boundary. If your company has even one shareholder who does not serve as a director, or if a trust, another company, or any other juristic person holds shares, the stricter non-owner-managed rules apply. Giving a family member 10% of your shares without making them a director can change your reporting obligation.

Above 100 points it gets stricter. Between 100 and 349, internally compiled statements trigger a mandatory audit, while independently compiled statements may only require an independent review. At 350 or more an audit is required regardless of ownership structure or how the statements were compiled.

> **In plain words:** Once your score passes 100, the rules tighten. Who prepares the statements starts to matter. From 350, an audit is required no matter what.

What you file with CIPC: annual returns must be filed with the latest beneficial ownership declaration and either audited financial statements, reviewed financial statements, or a Financial Accountability Supplement, depending on what applies to the company.

For a small owner-managed company, the Financial Accountability Supplement is the usual route. It is a short form of key financial information, not a full set of statements.

Cost reality: even without an audit, most one-person companies pay an accountant a few thousand rand a year to compile the statements and file the ITR14. Budget for it before you register.

#### 4. SARS company tax

A company is automatically a provisional taxpayer. Two IRP6 returns a year, plus one ITR14 annual return within 12 months of financial year end.

A dormant company still files a nil ITR14.

Full detail is in [Tax and SARS](#doc-tax-and-sars), including Small Business Corporation rates, which start at 0% on the first R99,000 of taxable income if the company qualifies.

#### 5. Registered representative and company details

Confirm the registered representative (the public officer) with SARS after registration. Without it you cannot transact for the company on eFiling.

Tell CIPC within the required period if the registered address, directors, or auditors change. Director changes are free to file.

---

### What happens if you stop filing

This is the most common way small South African companies die, and it is entirely avoidable.

If you miss the annual return for two consecutive years, CIPC starts the deregistration process. After enough missed years the company status moves from AR Deregistration Process to Final Deregistration.

Once two or more successive annual returns are outstanding, the company is automatically placed into the AR deregistration process. Banks, SARS and the Central Supplier Database may refuse to deal with it in this status.

> **In plain words:** Miss two years of annual returns and CIPC starts closing your company. While that is happening, banks and SARS may refuse to deal with you.

While non-compliant you cannot lodge any other CIPC documents. No director changes, no name changes, no address updates, and no special resolutions can be processed.

Once deregistered, the company loses its legal standing, bank accounts can be frozen, and assets can pass to the state as bona vacantia. Directors may face personal exposure for trading through a deregistered entity.

> **In plain words:** If CIPC finishes closing the company, it stops existing. The bank account can be frozen and anything the company owns can go to the state. If you keep trading anyway, you can be personally liable.

The scale of this is not theoretical. In January 2025 CIPC began deregistering more than 800,000 companies for missed annual returns and beneficial ownership filings.

Getting it back:

AR-deregistered companies can be reinstated relatively cheaply, around R200 plus the catch-up annual returns. Finally deregistered companies need a high-court reinstatement order, which is significantly more expensive.

A company deregistered for non-compliance can usually be reinstated by applying to CIPC on form CoR 40.5, proving it was trading or held assets, and settling all outstanding annual returns and fees.

> **In plain words:** You can usually bring a closed company back, but you must prove it was really trading, file everything you missed, and pay all the fees.

Two calendar reminders prevent all of this. Set them today.

---

### When the company does not protect you

People register a Pty believing their house is now safe. Three things break that.

**Personal surety.** Banks, landlords, and many suppliers will require you to sign personally as surety for the company's debt. Once you do, that specific debt is yours regardless of the company. Read what you sign, and negotiate limits where you can. A surety capped at R100,000 is very different from an unlimited one.

**Mixing money.** If you run personal expenses through the company account and treat company money as your own, you weaken the argument that the company is genuinely separate. It also creates the loan account tax problem in [Paying yourself from a Pty Ltd](#doc-paying-yourself-from-a-pty-ltd).

**Reckless or negligent trading.** The Companies Act 71 of 2008 places real duties on directors. Section 22 prohibits carrying on business recklessly, with gross negligence, or with intent to defraud. Section 76 sets out the duty to act in good faith, in the company's best interests, and with the care and skill reasonably expected. Section 77 makes directors personally liable for loss caused by breaching those duties, and section 218(2) allows anyone who suffers loss from a contravention of the Act to claim it.

In plain terms: continuing to take customer deposits when you know the company cannot deliver, or trading on while insolvent, can make the debt yours personally. The company is protection against ordinary business failure, not against knowing misconduct.

**The solvency and liquidity test.** Before the company distributes money to you as a dividend, the board must be satisfied the company passes the section 4 solvency and liquidity test: assets exceed liabilities, and the company can pay its debts as they fall due for the next 12 months. A one-person board still has to apply this, and record that it did.

---

### Records the company must keep

Separate from your tax records, the Companies Act requires the company to keep:

- The Memorandum of Incorporation
- A record of directors, past and present
- The securities register, meaning who owns the shares
- The beneficial interest register
- Board and shareholder resolutions
- Annual financial statements
- Notices and minutes of meetings

For a one-person company most of these are one page each, and most people never create them. Do it once, keep it in a folder called "Company records", and add to it as things change.

The two you will actually be asked for: your share certificate, and a resolution authorising you to open a bank account or sign a particular contract. Banks and corporate clients ask for these routinely.

Resolutions matter even when you are the only director. If you declare a dividend, write a one-paragraph resolution recording the date, the amount, and that the solvency and liquidity test was considered. It costs five minutes and it is the difference between a dividend and an unexplained withdrawal when SARS looks.

---

### Employees, including yourself

If the company pays you a salary, the company becomes an employer. That brings PAYE, and usually UIF, and possibly SDL. See [Paying yourself from a Pty Ltd](#doc-paying-yourself-from-a-pty-ltd), which covers the detail and one point where sources genuinely disagree.

COIDA almost certainly applies to you too, and this is widely misunderstood. The Act's definition of employee includes a working director of a company who has entered into a contract of service, in so far as they act within the scope of that employment. It excludes shareholders or silent partners who only receive dividends or a profit share.

So a working director paid a monthly salary qualifies as an employee, must be registered, and must submit a Return of Earnings, even where that director is the only person in the company. A sole proprietor, by contrast, is not an employee under COIDA and cannot register.

The Compensation Fund guidance says an employer must register within seven days after appointing employees, and that all employers with one or more part-time, casual, temporary, or full-time employees must register under section 80(1).

The Letter of Good Standing proving your COIDA account is current expires every year, and you need a valid one to tender for government work.

> **In plain words:** The moment you employ anyone, even part-time or casual, you have seven days to register with the Compensation Fund for workplace injury cover.

Do not read COIDA purely as a cost. It pays out for injury on duty, which is cover a sole proprietor cannot buy at any price through this route. For a one-person company it is part of the only safety net you have. See [You are the business](#doc-you-are-the-business).

Employment law is outside the scope of this toolkit. Before your first hire, get proper advice. The Basic Conditions of Employment Act and the Labour Relations Act are strict, and mistakes are expensive to unwind.

---

### What happens if you are not there

A company has perpetual succession, but it can only act through its directors, and under the Companies Act a person ceases to be a director on death.

For a sole director who is also the sole shareholder, that is a serious problem. There is nobody left who can appoint a replacement, because directors are appointed by shareholders and the shares have fallen into a deceased estate. The Master of the High Court must appoint an executor, commonly two weeks to three months, and whether that executor can then appoint a director depends on what your MOI says. If it does not allow it, the remedy is a court application.

Meanwhile nobody can sign, the bank account is stuck, and SARS and CIPC deadlines keep running.

The cheapest fix is to appoint a second director. Filing a director change with CIPC is free. Full detail, including the MOI check and the one-page record to leave behind, is in [You are the business](#doc-you-are-the-business).

### Closing a company properly

If you no longer need the company, deregister it deliberately. Do not just stop filing.

Unless it is formally deregistered, the filing requirement remains. If you no longer need the company, the correct route is voluntary deregistration, not simply stopping all filings.

The sequence, roughly: file all outstanding returns, settle all tax debt, get tax clearance, apply to CIPC for voluntary deregistration, then deregister each SARS tax type separately supporting the request with the CIPC deregistration notice.

Expect it to be slow. One South African compliance firm estimates 12 to 18 months at SARS, and advises continuing to file until deregistration is confirmed in writing. Treat that as an estimate, not a promise.

This is the strongest practical argument for not registering a company before you need one. Getting in costs R175. Getting out properly costs a year of admin.

---

### Your Pty Ltd annual checklist

Every year:

- [ ] Beneficial ownership declaration filed and current
- [ ] CIPC annual return filed within 30 business days of the registration anniversary
- [ ] Annual financial statements prepared
- [ ] Public Interest Score calculated at year end
- [ ] Financial Accountability Supplement or statements filed with the annual return
- [ ] ITR14 filed with SARS within 12 months of financial year end
- [ ] Two IRP6 provisional returns filed
- [ ] Company records folder updated with any resolutions passed

Every month, if the company pays you or anyone else a salary:

- [ ] EMP201 submitted and paid by the 7th of the following month

Twice a year, if registered as an employer:

- [ ] EMP501 reconciliation submitted

Ongoing:

- [ ] Company money kept strictly separate from personal money
- [ ] Contracts signed on behalf of the company, not in your own name
- [ ] Loan account balance checked before each financial year end

---

<a id="doc-paying-yourself-from-a-pty-ltd"></a>

## Paying yourself from a Pty Ltd

The money in the company bank account is not yours. It belongs to the company.

There are three legal ways to move it to your personal account. Each has a different tax consequence. Choosing badly, or choosing nothing at all, is the most common expensive mistake in one-person companies.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Salary | The company pays you as an employee. PAYE is deducted. |
| Dividend | The company pays out profit to you as the owner. 20% tax. |
| Loan account | The record of money owed between you and the company, outside salary and dividends. |
| Credit loan account | The company owes you. Good. |
| Debit loan account | You owe the company. Creates a tax problem. |
| PAYE | Tax deducted from a salary every month. |
| EMP201 | The monthly form declaring PAYE and UIF. |
| Official rate of interest | The rate SARS uses to decide whether a loan is too cheap. 7.75% from December 2025. |

---

### The problem this file solves

This is what it looks like when it goes wrong. One South African accounting firm describes the pattern in owner-managed companies:

Personal and living expenses and debit orders are paid through the business account. There is constant cash flow shortage within the business. The director is not paid a monthly salary. The owner spends funds as he or she sees fit, on the basis that as the only shareholder and director, all the funds and profits are theirs to use. The problem is that the owner has used funds out of the entity's account without noting that there are tax consequences.

> **In plain words:** The pattern that causes trouble: paying home expenses from the company account, never paying yourself a proper salary, and spending company money as if it were your own because you are the only owner. Each of those creates a tax bill you did not plan for.

If that describes you, read on. It is fixable, but it is much cheaper to fix before financial year end than after.

---

### Option 1: salary

The company puts you on its payroll and pays you a monthly amount.

#### What it triggers

The company becomes an employer and must register for payroll taxes.

The company needs to deduct employee's tax (PAYE) from the amounts paid to directors, and make monthly EMP201 submissions to SARS. The same applies in owner-managed businesses where there is only one director and no employees.

The EMP201 is due by the 7th of the following month. An EMP501 reconciliation is submitted twice a year.

#### Ignore anything you read about "deemed remuneration"

Many South African tax articles still describe a rule where a private company director's PAYE is calculated on a notional monthly salary worked out from last year's earnings.

That rule is gone. Paragraph 11C of the Fourth Schedule, which created the deemed remuneration formula, was repealed with effect from 1 March 2017. From 1 March 2017 deemed remuneration should no longer be used, and directors' remuneration is subject to PAYE on actual remuneration, as with normal employees.

PAYE is calculated on what the company actually pays you, month by month, the same way it works for any employee.

This is worth knowing because several widely-read payroll articles still explain the old rule as though it applies. If anyone quotes deemed remuneration at you, ask them to confirm it against the current Fourth Schedule.

What does still apply to irregular pay: section 7B of the Income Tax Act treats variable remuneration such as a bonus or commission as accruing on the date it is actually paid. A lump sum paid in month twelve is taxed in month twelve. You are not penalised for drawing irregularly, but once the company pays you remuneration at all, it must register as an employer and submit an EMP201 for the months in which it pays you.


#### UIF: sources disagree

Be careful here, because South African sources genuinely conflict on whether a sole director must contribute to UIF.

One position: directors must register even if they are the only employee of their own company. If a director earns a salary, wage, or structured remuneration through the company's payroll, the business must deduct employee's tax, and UIF rules apply to directors who are legally classified as employees.

The other position: where the director is not an employee under an employment contract and receives director's fees rather than a salary, UIF and SDL may not apply.

The distinction turns on whether you are an employee of the company under the Unemployment Insurance Act, which depends on how you are actually engaged and paid, not on what you call it.

This is a real fork with real money attached. Get an hour with a tax practitioner or payroll specialist rather than guessing. Whichever way it lands, document the basis for the decision.

SDL generally only applies once annual payroll passes a threshold, so a one-person company usually does not reach it.

One point that usually gets lost in this argument. UIF is nearly always presented as a cost to avoid. For a one-person company it may also be the only illness or maternity benefit you will ever have access to, because a sole proprietor is barred from contributing at all. Weigh that before you argue your way out of it. See [You are the business](#doc-you-are-the-business).

#### When salary makes sense

Salary is deductible for the company, which reduces company taxable income. It is taxed in your hands at your personal marginal rate, and the personal tax threshold means the first R99,000 of your total taxable income attracts no tax if you are under 65.

For a one-person company earning modest profits, a salary up to around the tax threshold is often the cleanest starting point: no tax for you, a deduction for the company, and a clear paper trail.

Salary also gives you something dividends do not: proof of regular income, which matters for a bond application or a car finance application.

---

### Option 2: dividend

The company declares a dividend out of profits after tax. You receive it as a shareholder.

#### What it costs

Dividends tax is 20%, withheld and paid over by the company.

Dividends are paid from company profits after tax and do not count as salary. You declare them through a board resolution, and they are subject to dividends tax, currently 20%.

A dividend is not deductible for the company. So the money is taxed twice: once as company profit, then again at 20% in your hands.

#### The two conditions

**Profits must exist.** A dividend may generally only be declared out of profits, not out of capital. This has been settled South African law since Cohen NO v Segal, decided in the Witwatersrand Local Division in 1970, where two directors divided the proceeds of a company property sale between themselves and then purported to declare it a dividend. The court held that a dividend may generally only be declared out of profits, and that declaring one out of capital is beyond the company's powers.

**The solvency and liquidity test.** Before declaring, the board must be satisfied the company passes the section 4 test in the Companies Act: assets exceed liabilities, and the company can pay its debts as they fall due over the next 12 months. A single-director board still has to apply this and record that it did.

#### Do the paperwork

Write a short resolution: the date, the amount, the shareholder, and a statement that the solvency and liquidity test was considered and passed. File it in your company records.

A transfer with no resolution behind it is not a dividend. It is a loan, which brings you to option 3.

---

### Option 3: loan account

The loan account records money flowing between you and the company outside of salary and dividends.

There are two directions, and they are completely different.

#### Credit loan account: the company owes you

This happens when you put your own money into the company, or pay a company expense from your personal card.

This is the good direction. If the company owes you money on loan account because you funded your business, the business can repay your loan account with funds available in the business.

Repayment of a credit loan account is not income. It is the company returning your own money. No tax.

Most one-person companies start here, because the founder funds the first few months personally. Record every rand you put in. Many owners forget, then pay tax on money they could have drawn out tax-free.

#### Debit loan account: you owe the company

This happens when you take money out without calling it salary or a dividend. Personal spending on the company card. A transfer to your personal account "to be sorted out later".

This is the expensive direction.

If a director does not carefully keep track of what is recorded against their loan account, it may go into debit. This means the director now owes the company money.

The tax problem: an interest-free or low-interest loan from a company to a shareholder triggers a deemed dividend under section 64E(4) of the Income Tax Act.

Where a debit loan exists and the interest charged on the outstanding balance is lower than the official interest rate, the difference gives rise to a dividend in specie. The dividend is deemed to have been paid on the last day of the year of assessment and the company is liable for the dividends tax.

> **In plain words:** If you owe your company money and are not paying it a proper rate of interest, SARS treats the missing interest as if the company paid you a dividend on the last day of the year. The company then owes 20% tax on it.

The official rate is published by SARS and moves. From 1 December 2025 the official rate for the fringe benefit on interest-free or low-interest loans is 7.75% per annum.

**A discrepancy worth knowing about.** Some South African sources say the deemed dividend is 20% of the whole loan balance. Others say it is only on the foregone interest, meaning the shortfall between the official rate and what you actually paid. The Tax Faculty states the position clearly: the capital is not deemed a dividend, and the interest will accrue, posing a tax risk should the loan and accrued interest be written off in future.

This toolkit takes the foregone-interest position, because that is what the wording of section 64E(4) and SARS's own guidance support. But the disagreement in the market is real, so do not rely on this file alone for a material loan account. SARS publishes a Comprehensive Guide to Dividends Tax, linked in [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register).

**Never let it be written off.** If the company writes off the loan, the owner is liable for income tax on the entire loan amount. A growing debit loan that can never be repaid is a slow-building tax bill.

#### Fixing a debit loan account

You have three options, and the first two must be done before financial year end:

Declare a dividend equal to the value of the debit balance and pay the dividends tax to SARS. Declare additional salary or a bonus equal to the net value of the loan account. Or raise interest income on the loan balance at a rate linked to the official rate, to avoid a deemed dividend arising.

> **In plain words:** Three ways to fix it: declare a dividend to cover what you owe and pay the 20% tax; or record it as extra salary and pay PAYE on it; or charge yourself interest at the official rate so there is no missing interest.

The first two options need to be exercised before the end of the financial year, so the dividend is declared in time and the EMP501 for that year reflects the salary.

Practical advice from the same source: review your loan account schedule monthly, not annually. By the time the financial statements are prepared, usually six months after year end, it is too late to declare a dividend for the closed year.

---

### Which to use

There is no single right answer. It depends on your profit, your other income, and what you need the money for.

A common structure for a one-person company, offered as a starting point and not as advice:

Draw a modest regular salary, roughly up to the personal tax threshold, so it is deductible for the company and tax-free for you. Take anything further as a declared dividend, with a resolution, once profits and solvency allow. Keep the loan account at zero or in credit, and check it monthly.

The optimal structure depends on your lifestyle needs, tax bracket, and long-term business goals.

Model it on your actual numbers once, with a tax practitioner, in your first year. The difference between a good structure and a careless one on R500,000 of profit is large enough to pay for the advice many times over.

---

### The rules that prevent all of this

1. Never pay a personal expense from the company account. Not once. Pay yourself, then pay the expense.
2. Record every rand you put into the company. That is money you can take back out tax-free.
3. Check the loan account balance before each financial year end, not after.
4. Write a resolution for every dividend, on the day you declare it.
5. If you pay yourself a salary, run it through payroll properly with an EMP201 every month.
6. Decide the UIF question with a professional rather than assuming either way.

---

<a id="doc-adding-new-lines-of-business"></a>

## Adding new lines of business to your Pty

You started with one thing. Now you want to add another: a vehicle dealer adding a tyre shop, a mechanic adding parts sales, a designer adding printing.

This file answers the questions that come up when a one-person company grows sideways. Most of them have cheaper answers than people expect, and one of them has an expensive trap.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Registered name | The company's legal name on its CIPC certificate. |
| Trading name | A different name you market under. |
| MOI | Memorandum of Incorporation. The company's rulebook. |
| SBC rates | Lower company tax rates for small businesses that meet strict conditions. |
| Ring-fence | Keeping one risky activity separate so its problems cannot reach the rest. |

---

### Can one company trade in more than one thing

Yes. A private company registered on BizPortal with the standard Memorandum of Incorporation has unrestricted objects. It may carry on any lawful business. You do not need to amend anything at CIPC to start a new activity.

What does change is everything that attaches to the activity rather than to the company:

- Licences. Each new line needs its own licences and permits. Open the matching file in `04-business-types/`. A vehicle dealer adding a café is now also a food business and needs a Certificate of Acceptability.
- Insurance. Tell your insurer. Cover bought for one activity does not respond to claims from another.
- Zoning. Your premises may be zoned for motor trade but not for food preparation, or for retail but not for workshop noise. Ask town planning before you start.
- SAPS second-hand goods registration, if you deal in used goods. The holder of a certificate of registration must notify the National Commissioner in writing within 30 days if there is any change to the information submitted in the application, or any change that affects the dealer's ability to meet the registration requirements. Adding a new category of used goods, or a new premises, is such a change.

> **In plain words:** If anything on your SAPS dealer registration changes, including what you deal in or where, you must tell SAPS in writing within 30 days.

---

### Registered name and trading names

Your company has one registered name, the one on the CoR 14.3. You can market under other names.

This is where a decision made at registration pays off or costs you. A company registered as "Mokoena Motors (Pty) Ltd" that now sells solar panels has an awkward name on every invoice. A company registered as "Mokoena Holdings (Pty) Ltd" or simply under its registration number can trade as "Mokoena Motors" and "Mokoena Solar" without friction.

If you have not registered yet: register a neutral name, or no name at all, and put the specific brand in the trading name. See [Branding prompts](#doc-branding-prompts).

If you have already registered a narrow name: a name change at CIPC is possible and not expensive, but not urgent. Trading names solve most of it.

#### What the law requires either way

Section 32 of the Companies Act says the company's registered name and registration number must appear on all its documents. That means invoices, receipts, delivery notes, letters, orders and notices, including emails and your website.

So the rule is simple. Use the trading name for marketing. Then put "Trading as" with the full registered name and number on every quote, invoice, contract, website footer and email signature. Something like:

```
Mokoena Solar
A trading name of Mokoena Holdings (Pty) Ltd, Reg. No. 2026/123456/07
```

Be aware the Consumer Protection Act also expects a supplier to trade under its registered name or under a business name registered under section 80 of that Act, and the section 80 register has never been brought into operation. The universal practice is the "trading as" disclosure above, which gives the customer the information the law is trying to protect. Do it consistently and you are in the strongest position available.

Each trading name should have its own domain, social handles and, if it matters, its own trade mark search. The company name protects nothing in the market.

---

### Tax: one company, several activities

For income tax, the company has one taxable income. All activities go on one ITR14. Profits from one line offset losses in another. That is an advantage of a single company.

For VAT, one registration covers every activity the company carries on. If you registered because of vehicle sales, your tyre sales are also VAT sales, and you can claim VAT on the tyres you buy in.

Two traps.

**Turnover tax.** If the company is on turnover tax, adding a professional-services line can push it out. A business is excluded where more than 20% of income comes from professional services. A dealer adding a consulting arm is at risk. Also, turnover tax is charged on total sales across everything the company does. A line with small margins gets taxed on its sales just like a line with big margins. That usually hurts the low-margin one.

**Small Business Corporation status.** SBC rates depend on every shareholder being a natural person, and no shareholder may hold shares in another company, apart from limited exceptions. This matters when you consider the next question.

---

### Should the new line be a separate company

The instinct is often yes: keep the risky business away from the safe one. Sometimes that is right. But the costs are real and one of them is hidden.

#### What a second company costs

Everything in [Running a Pty Ltd](#doc-running-a-pty-ltd), twice. Two annual returns, two beneficial ownership filings, two sets of financial statements, two ITR14s, two sets of provisional returns, two bank accounts. If you found one company's admin heavy, two will not be lighter.

#### The hidden cost: you may lose SBC rates on both

The condition that disqualifies most companies from Small Business Corporation status is a shareholder holding shares in another company.

You are the shareholder of Company A. You form Company B and hold its shares. You now hold shares in another company. Depending on the exact exceptions, Company A may lose SBC status, and Company B may never qualify. Both then pay the flat 27% instead of a scale that starts at 0%.

On R300,000 of taxable income the difference is tens of thousands of rand a year. Get a tax practitioner to run your specific structure before forming a second company. This is not a question to guess at.

#### When splitting is still worth it

A separate company makes sense when the new line could sink the first one if something goes badly wrong, and when that risk is bigger than the extra tax you would pay by losing SBC rates. A vehicle dealer adding a transport or logistics operation with heavy vehicles on the road is the kind of case where separation can be justified. A vehicle dealer adding tyre fitment usually is not.

Remember too that a bank or landlord will ask you to sign surety for the new company anyway. That takes away much of the protection you were splitting for. See the "When the company does not protect you" section in `01-core/06`.

#### The middle route

One company, separate trading names, separate bank sub-accounts or clearly labelled transactions, separate insurance schedules. You get the marketing separation and the records separation without the double compliance. Most one-person businesses adding a second line should start here.

---

### Records when you have more than one line

Add a "Line" column to your cash book. Vehicles, Tyres, Workshop. Tag every transaction.

This costs nothing and it tells you, within three months, which line is actually making money. Owners routinely discover that the line they enjoy most is the one losing money, and the boring one is paying for it.

It also makes the trading stock calculation at year end far easier if stock belongs to more than one line.

---

### Checklist before you add a line

- [ ] Checked the matching file in `04-business-types/` for licences the new line needs
- [ ] Confirmed zoning allows the new activity at my premises
- [ ] Told my insurer and received written confirmation of cover
- [ ] Notified SAPS within 30 days if it changes my second-hand goods registration
- [ ] Decided the trading name, and checked domain, handles and trade marks
- [ ] Put "trading as" plus the registered name and number on the new line's documents
- [ ] Checked whether the new line affects my turnover tax or SBC position
- [ ] Decided against a second company, or got advice on the SBC consequence first
- [ ] Added a Line column to the cash book

---

<a id="doc-working-from-home-and-meeting-safely"></a>

## Running it from home, and meeting customers safely

Many one-person businesses have no shop, no yard and no office. The business lives on a phone. Customers find you online, talk to you on WhatsApp, and if they need to see the product, you meet them somewhere.

This works well. It is cheap, it is legal for most businesses, and it is how a large share of South African small businesses operate. It just needs a few habits that a shop does not.

This file covers three things: what the rules allow when you work from home, how to handle money when you never see the customer, and how to meet a stranger to hand over a product or a vehicle without putting yourself at risk.

The risks are real. They are also manageable with a handful of habits. Nothing here should stop you trading.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Home enterprise / home occupation | The municipal category that allows small, quiet businesses in a house without special permission. |
| Consent use | Municipal permission for a business use that goes beyond what your zoning allows by default. |
| Body corporate / HOA | The management body of a complex or estate. It has its own rules on top of the municipality's. |
| POP | Proof of payment. A screenshot or PDF showing a transfer was made. Easy to fake. |
| Reflected / cleared | The money is actually in your account balance, not just "pending". |
| PayShap | An instant bank-to-bank payment that arrives in seconds and cannot be reversed. |
| Instant EFT / RTC | Other fast payment options. Also arrive quickly. |
| Overpayment scam | A "buyer" pays too much on purpose, then asks for the difference back. The original payment was never real. |
| Geotag | Location data hidden inside a photo. It can reveal where you live. |
| OTP | One-time PIN. The code your bank sends you. Never share it with anyone. |
| SABRIC | The banking industry's fraud body. |

---

### The short version

You can run most businesses from home without any special permission, as long as the house still looks and behaves like a house.

Never hand over anything until the money is in your account balance. A screenshot is not money.

Meet strangers in busy, public places with cameras. Take someone with you. Keep the keys.

If you are a vehicle dealer working from home, storing stock at the house is the one thing that usually breaks the zoning rules. Read the vehicle section below.

---

### Part 1: what the rules allow when you work from home

#### Zoning

Every property has a zoning. Most houses are zoned for living, not for business. But every municipality allows some business use in a home.

Working alone from home in a home office is highly unlikely to cause any issues legally or practically, and you are likely to be allowed to run small-scale business activities without consent where they fall into your municipality's "micro-business" or "home enterprise" category. As soon as your activities go further, with limits on the type of business, number of staff, share of the house used, parking, noise and nuisance, you will probably have to apply for municipal consent.

> **In plain words:** If it is just you, a phone, a laptop and a spare room, you almost certainly need no permission. If customers start arriving, staff start working there, or the house starts looking like a business, you need to ask.

The usual conditions for a home enterprise, which differ between municipalities:

The business must stay secondary to living there. No more than one or two non-resident employees. No signage beyond a small nameplate. No storage of goods, materials or vehicles that gives the property a commercial appearance.

Typically such exceptions permit no more than a quarter of the home's floor area to be used for business, provided the activity stays unobtrusive and does not generate excessive traffic, noise or risk.

The activities that usually push past a home enterprise and need consent use: a salon or personal services business with a steady stream of clients, a guesthouse, food production or catering with significant preparation or storage, or more staff than allowed.

A business with no customers at the house, where everything happens by phone and at meeting points, is the easiest case. It is the model this file assumes.

#### Two extra layers

**Complexes and estates.** Courts have repeatedly confirmed that body corporate conduct rules are enforceable, even when they are stricter than municipal zoning. Read your scheme's conduct rules before you start. In sectional title schemes, business activities can be allowed if written consent is given by all owners of the section.

**Title deed.** Check your title deed for restrictive conditions and the zoning certificate of the property. A visit to a municipal or private town planner gives you certainty. Some older title deeds forbid any trade from the property. That is separate from zoning.

#### Using your home address officially

You can use your home as the registered address for a company, as the address on your SARS profile, as the premises on your SAPS second-hand goods registration, and as the proof of address for a BRNC. A utility bill or bank statement in your name, under three months old, is what each of these wants.

The address will then appear on public records and on every invoice. If that worries you, a PO box does not solve it, because most registrations require a physical address. Accept it, and manage the safety side with the habits in Part 3.

#### Insurance

If there is a fire caused by business equipment, or a client trips and falls on your property, your standard household policy might not cover you at all. Tell your household insurer you run a business from home and what equipment or stock is there. Ask specifically about public liability and about stock cover. It usually costs little to add and it is a claim refusal waiting to happen if you do not.

#### Home office deduction

If you have a room used only for the business, you may be able to claim a share of your home costs against tax. The rules are strict. See [Tax and SARS](#doc-tax-and-sars).

---

### Part 2: doing business by phone, WhatsApp and voice note

Most of your customer contact will be messages, calls and voice notes. That is fine. It needs three habits.

**Confirm everything that matters in writing.** A voice note that says "R4,500 including delivery, Thursday" is easy to misremember and impossible to search. After a voice note or a call, send a short text summary: what, how much, when, where. Ask them to reply "yes". That text is your agreement.

**Use one business number.** WhatsApp Business on a separate SIM, not your personal number. Your personal number stays private, your business chats stay in one place, and you can hand the number to someone else one day. See [Marketing prompts](#doc-marketing-prompts) for setting it up.

**Keep the records.** Export or back up WhatsApp chats for any sale worth arguing about. Voice notes about a deal are business records too. Under POPIA you must also be careful with what you keep: customer ID copies and addresses should be stored somewhere safe, kept only as long as you need them, and never forwarded. Your privacy notice in [Which template to use when](#doc-which-template-to-use-when) tells customers what you keep and why.

**Do not put your home address in adverts or photos.** Be careful not to give out too much information in the advert or on the phone. Make sure photos posted online do not have geotag data embedded in them, which could give away your home address. Most phones let you switch off location in the camera settings. Do that before you photograph stock at home. Give your area, not your street.

---

### Part 3: money, when you never see the customer

This is where most losses happen, and almost all of them are avoidable with one rule.

#### The one rule

Nothing leaves your hands until the money is in your account balance.

Release nothing, deliver nothing, and start nothing that costs you money until the funds reflect in your account balance, not "pending". If your bank app has not shown the money in your balance, you have not been paid.

Not a screenshot. Not an SMS. Not a PDF on a bank letterhead. Not a phone call from "the bank". Your own banking app, showing the balance has gone up.

A screenshot, SMS or notification a customer shows you is never proof that a payment has gone through. Only your own portal or app can confirm that.

#### Why this rule exists

Fake proof of payment is the most common scam aimed at small sellers in South Africa. It is not new and it is not rare.

Fake proof of payment is becoming more common as scammers up the ante. Gumtree South Africa described a seller who was sent a fake proof of payment, and the scammer even sent an Uber to collect the goods. The account behind it had been opened two days earlier and had done the same thing 24 times in those two days.

According to SABRIC's 2024 crime statistics, digital banking fraud incidents rose 86%, with losses up 74% to R1.888 billion.

> **In plain words:** This is a normal business risk, like shoplifting is for a shop. The habit above defeats nearly all of it.

#### Use instant payment methods

Ordinary EFT can take a day or two to reflect, which is exactly the gap scammers use. South Africa now has instant options that remove the gap.

PayShap supports single payments up to R50,000, raised from R3,000 in November 2024, but each bank sets its own per-transaction and daily limits inside that ceiling. Payments clear in under ten seconds, 24 hours a day, with no cut-off times. You do not need the customer to have a ShapID; they can pay to your normal account number and it still clears in seconds.

PayShap and Real-Time Clearing give you immediate confirmation and cannot be reversed.

For a sale under your bank's PayShap limit, ask the customer to pay by PayShap or immediate payment while you watch your app. The money arrives before they leave. For larger amounts, an immediate payment through their banking app does the same job at a higher fee, or you meet at a bank branch and the transfer is done at the counter.

Ask your bank what your own PayShap limit is. It differs by bank and by account.

#### The scams, plainly

You will meet some of these. Knowing the shape of them is most of the defence.

| What it looks like | What is really happening | What to do |
|---|---|---|
| A convincing proof of payment arrives. Buyer is in a hurry. | The transfer was never made, or was made and will be reversed. | Wait for your balance. Never release on a POP. |
| Buyer "accidentally" pays too much and asks for the difference back. | The original payment later fails, reverses, or never existed. | Refund nothing until the original has cleared for days. Refund only to the account it came from. |
| Buyer cannot come, sends a driver, courier or Uber to collect. | The pattern SAPS warns about: a fake proof of purchase, a fake ID, then a driver sent to collect. | No collection by third party until money has reflected. Then it is fine. |
| A call or voice note from "your bank" about a suspicious payment, asking you to approve something in your app. | Social engineering. They talk you into approving a payment yourself. | Hang up. Phone your bank on the number on your card. Never approve anything on someone's instruction. |
| A payment link or "delivery fee" link arrives by message. | A phishing page. One login and your account is theirs. | Never log in through a link someone sent. Open your app yourself. |
| Someone asks for the OTP the bank just sent you, for any reason. | The OTP authorises a payment out of your account. | Never. Not to a customer, not to "the bank", not to anyone. |
| Payment by cheque. | Cheques take time to clear; the buyer collects the goods and then cancels the cheque. | Do not accept cheques. South African banks stopped accepting them at the end of 2020, so a "cheque" today is a red flag on its own. |
| Buyer agrees your full price instantly, asks no questions, is overseas, or wants to move the chat to a different number. | Classic red flags for a fake buyer. | Slow down. Real buyers negotiate and ask things. |

Losing a sale to caution costs you nothing. Losing the goods costs you everything.

#### If you take cash

Cash is legal and for small amounts it is simple. For large amounts it carries two risks: fake notes, and being robbed on the way to the bank.

The South African Reserve Bank teaches a three-step check.

Look, feel and tilt. Feel: the portrait and the words SOUTH AFRICAN RESERVE BANK feel slightly raised or rough. The raised lines at the bottom corners count up with the value, one line on a R10 up to five on a R200. Tilt: the metallic thread reflects light and shifts colour, and the numerals at the bottom right are printed in colour-changing ink.

Look: held up to the light, the security thread becomes a continuous solid line, and the watermark is an embedded image of Nelson Mandela to the left of the front of the note.

Fake notes can be surprisingly convincing. Even if the person paying you thinks they are genuine and you accept them in good faith, you still suffer the loss. By law counterfeit money cannot be exchanged for real money.

Anyone who comes into possession of counterfeit notes must report it to their nearest police station. The SARB also has a free Currency App that shows the security features.

For any amount worth worrying about, the simplest protection: do the cash transaction inside a bank branch, where the money can be checked for authenticity before you deposit it. The buyer deposits directly into your account at the counter. You watch it reflect. Nobody walks around with an envelope.

#### For a vehicle dealer

Vehicle sales are large. PayShap limits will often be too low. The options that work:

- Immediate payment from the buyer's banking app while you watch your balance
- Meet at the buyer's bank; the transfer is done at the counter
- A bank-to-bank transfer the day before, with collection only once it has reflected

Never hand over keys, the registration certificate, or a signed NCO until the full amount is in your balance. Do not hand over the keys or documents until full payment clears.

---

### Part 4: meeting a customer to inspect or test drive

At some point a customer needs to see the product before paying, or drive the vehicle. That means meeting a stranger. The habits below are what the security industry and the motoring industry both recommend. Follow them and this is a routine part of doing business.

#### Choose the place

Meet in a public place. Never meet in quiet or deserted locations. A well-lit public location, like a petrol station or a police station.

Good choices: the parking area of a police station, a bank, a busy petrol station with cameras, or a shopping centre car park during trading hours. Choose somewhere you know, with people around and cameras present.

Not at your home. You would not normally invite strangers to your home, so do not do it while selling. Rather take the vehicle to a public place.

Not at their home, for a first meeting.

Daylight. Not after dark.

South Africa does not have a formal "safe exchange zone" scheme at police stations. Many stations are happy for people to meet in the visitor parking. Ask at your local station before you rely on it.

#### Before you go

**Get their details first.** Get a copy of their ID or driver's licence sent to you before meeting, "to draw up paperwork so the transaction goes quicker on the day." A real buyer sends it. Under POPIA, keep it safely and delete it once the deal is done or abandoned.

**Tell someone.** Let a friend or relative know where you will be and whom you will be meeting. Share your live location on WhatsApp with them for the duration.

**Take someone.** Have a friend or family member accompany you, both when meeting the buyer and on the test drive. Two people change the whole calculation for anyone with bad intentions.

**Trust the feeling.** If something does not seem right, or it feels unsafe, trust your gut and remove yourself from the situation. That can mean not meeting them at all, or leaving the moment something feels wrong. Have a back-up plan.

#### During a test drive

This is where vehicles are stolen from sellers, and every one of these rules exists because of a real case.

They will be driving your vehicle, so they must bring their driver's licence. Ask for a copy or take a photo. A legitimate buyer will not mind; a criminal is deterred. Check that the face on the licence is the person in front of you, and matches the ID they sent.

Do not hand over the keys until you are actually in the vehicle, and get them back before you get out. Otherwise they can simply drive off.

Never let a buyer test drive alone, because they may never come back. Watch your keys at all times. Some thieves do a key swap during the viewing and return later to steal the vehicle.

Keep the fuel low. A near-empty tank limits how far anyone can go.

Your companion stays with the buyer's own vehicle and their companion, if they brought one. It is your vehicle; you decide who rides.

Check your insurance covers a prospective buyer driving. For a dealer, that is motor trade insurance with test drive cover, covered in [Vehicle dealer](#doc-vehicle-dealer). For a private seller, phone your insurer and ask.

#### After the meeting

If the deal goes ahead: payment reflects first, then keys and papers, then the NCO is lodged the same day. See [Vehicle dealer](#doc-vehicle-dealer) for the full paperwork.

If it does not: delete the ID copies you were sent. You have no reason to keep them.

#### For products other than vehicles

The same rules, simplified. Meet in public, in daylight, with someone. Let the customer inspect. They pay by instant transfer while you watch your app. Then you hand it over. A customer who insists on taking the item "to show someone" before paying is not a customer.

---

### Part 5: if something goes wrong

**A fake payment, and the goods are gone.** Open a case at your nearest SAPS station and get the case number. Report it to your bank's fraud line. Report the buyer's account or profile to the platform they contacted you on. Keep every message. Recovery is unlikely, but the case number matters for insurance and for the bank's investigation.

**A fake note.** Report it to your nearest police station. Do not try to pass it on; that is a crime.

**Someone got into your banking.** Phone your bank's fraud line immediately, from the number on the back of your card. Change passwords. Report to SABRIC at www.sabric.co.za.

**A test drive that ended in theft.** SAPS immediately, then your insurer and your tracking company if fitted. The licence photo you took is now the most important thing you have.

**You feel threatened at a meeting.** Leave. Do not argue over the product. Nothing you are selling is worth it.

---

### Your checklist

**Setup**
- [ ] Checked my zoning, title deed, and complex rules allow what I do at home
- [ ] Told my household insurer about the business and its stock
- [ ] Location switched off in my phone camera before photographing stock at home
- [ ] Separate WhatsApp Business number
- [ ] Asked my bank what my PayShap and immediate payment limits are

**Every sale**
- [ ] Deal confirmed in a text message the customer replied "yes" to
- [ ] Money reflected in my balance before anything left my hands
- [ ] No refund of any "overpayment" until the original cleared and only to the same account
- [ ] Cash checked with look, feel, tilt, or deposited at a bank counter

**Every meeting**
- [ ] Public place, daylight, cameras
- [ ] ID or licence received before the meeting
- [ ] Someone told, location shared, someone with me
- [ ] Keys in my hand until I am in the vehicle, and back in my hand before I get out
- [ ] ID copies deleted afterwards if no deal

---

<a id="doc-you-are-the-business"></a>

## You are the business

Everything else in this toolkit is about the business. This one is about you, because in a one-person business you are not the owner of the asset. You are the asset.

Three things follow from that, and none of them are covered anywhere else:

1. If you cannot work, income stops. What protection you have depends on how you are structured, and one structure has almost none.
2. If a customer does not pay, how you chase them also depends on how you are structured. One structure has a free court. The other does not.
3. If something happens to you, someone has to be able to run or close the business. For a company this is much harder than people expect.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| UIF | Unemployment Insurance Fund. Pays you if you cannot work. Employees contribute; the self-employed generally cannot. |
| COIDA | The workplace injury fund. Pays out if you are hurt while working. |
| Letter of Good Standing | Proof your COIDA account is up to date. Expires yearly. Often needed for tender work. |
| Income protection | Private insurance that pays you a monthly income if illness or injury stops you working. |
| Letter of demand | A formal written demand for payment, usually the last step before court. |
| Prescription | A debt you leave too long stops being claimable. Three years for most ordinary debts. |
| Executor | The person the Master of the High Court appoints to wind up a dead person's estate. |
| Master of the High Court | The office that oversees deceased estates. |
| MOI | Memorandum of Incorporation. The company's rulebook. |

---

### Part 1: if you cannot work

#### Sole proprietor: you have nothing

This is not an exaggeration. Section 1 of the Unemployment Insurance Contributions Act defines an employee as a natural person who receives remuneration for services rendered, but expressly excludes an independent contractor. A sole proprietor therefore cannot contribute to UIF, because a sole proprietor cannot be an employee who becomes unemployed.

COIDA is the same story. Its definition of employee covers someone working under a contract of service, and a working director of a company, but a sole proprietor or a partner is not an employee under the Act.

So as a sole proprietor: no unemployment benefit, no illness benefit, no maternity benefit, and no cover if you are injured while working. Break your wrist on a Tuesday and your income is zero until you can work again.

#### Pty Ltd paying you a salary: you may have some

A one-person company changes this, and it is one of the few real advantages of incorporating that nobody mentions in the register-or-not conversation.

**COIDA is the clearer one.** The Act's definition of employee includes a working director of a company who has entered into a contract of service, in so far as they act within the scope of that employment. It excludes shareholders or silent partners who only receive dividends or profit share. Guidance for 2026 puts it plainly: a working director paid a monthly salary qualifies as an employee, must be registered, and must submit a Return of Earnings even where that director is the only employee.

That matters because COIDA is exactly the cover a sole proprietor cannot get: it pays out for injury on duty.

**UIF is the contested one.** Some sources say a director on the company payroll is an employee for UIF and must contribute, which would also mean they can claim. Others say UIF does not apply where the director takes director's fees rather than a salary under a contract of service. [Paying yourself from a Pty Ltd](#doc-paying-yourself-from-a-pty-ltd) sets out both positions.

The point for this file is the one that gets missed. **UIF is usually described as a cost. For a one-person company it may also be the only illness benefit you will ever have access to.** If you were going to argue your way out of contributing, weigh that first.

Unemployment benefit is the odd one for a sole director, because being unemployed from your own company is a strange event. The illness and maternity benefits are the realistic ones.

#### What both structures still need

**Income protection or disability cover.** Private insurance that pays a monthly income if you cannot work. For a one-person business this is arguably more important than the public liability cover recommended elsewhere in this toolkit, because public liability protects against something you might do to someone else, and income protection protects against the thing most likely to actually happen to you.

Ask a broker for income protection, and ask specifically what the waiting period is before it pays, since a policy that starts paying after three months does not help with a six-week injury.

**A cash buffer.** Not an investment, just money in a separate account that you do not touch. Whatever number of months you could not work and still pay rent. This is the whole safety net for a sole proprietor and the first line for a company.

**COIDA registration and Letter of Good Standing, if you have a company and pay yourself a salary.** The Letter of Good Standing expires every year, and you will need a current one to tender for government work.

---

### Part 2: when a customer does not pay

#### The escalation ladder

Most unpaid invoices are resolved in the first two steps. Work through them in order and keep every message.

1. **A friendly reminder**, three days after the due date. Assume it was forgotten, because usually it was.
2. **A firm reminder**, a week later, restating the amount, the invoice number, and the date it was due.
3. **A final demand**, giving a clear deadline and saying what happens next. Put it in writing, not a voice note.
