### Next

Go to [Brand applications and polish](#doc-brand-applications-and-polish). That covers the files you actually need, everywhere the brand gets used, and how to check whether it looks professional before you spend money.

Do not redesign the brand for a year. Customers recognise you through repetition. Consistency is worth more than perfection.

---

<a id="doc-mood-and-materials-use-after-prompt-4"></a>

## Mood and materials: decide this before the logo

Run this after Prompt 4 and before Prompt 5 in [Branding prompts](#doc-branding-prompts).

### Why this step exists

Without it, three prompts each make their own aesthetic decision and they can disagree.

Prompt 5 gives you logo directions. Prompt 7 gives you colours and type. Prompt 8 gives you photography. Each is good on its own. But nothing tells them to agree with each other, so you can end up with a rugged workshop logo, a soft pastel palette, and clinical white-background photography. Three good answers that do not belong to the same business.

A designer sets the mood first, then derives everything from it. This is that missing step.

It also covers something the toolkit does not touch anywhere else: what your brand is physically made of. Signage board, print stock, what a shirt feels like, what the vehicle lettering is cut from. For a small South African business those choices cost real money and fail in specific, predictable ways.

### Before you start: what the research actually says about colour

Ask an AI about brand colours and it will hand you a meaning chart. Blue is trust, red is urgency, green is nature. **Treat that as decoration, not evidence.**

Elliot and Maier's review in the Annual Review of Psychology (2014) found no universal meaning for any colour. Effects are context-dependent and shift with saturation, brightness, product category and culture.

The widely repeated claim that 62 to 90% of a snap product judgment comes from colour traces back to a 2006 literature review by Satyendra Singh, not to a controlled buying experiment. It is quoted everywhere as though it were a finding. It is not.

What does hold up is narrower and more useful. Labrecque and Milne, in the Journal of the Academy of Marketing Science (2012), found that **consistency between a brand's colour and its perceived personality predicts preference better than the colour itself does.** In other words the question is not "what does green mean", it is "does this colour match what my business actually is, and does it separate me from the shop down the road".

That is why the prompts below ask about fit and difference, and never about colour meanings. If an AI starts telling you what a colour signifies, ignore that part of the answer.

### Prompt A: the visual territory

```
Here is my brand brief:

[PASTE YOUR BRAND BRIEF]

My business name is: [NAME]

Before I choose a logo, colours or photography, I want to settle the
overall mood so that all three agree with each other.

Give me 5 different visual territories for this business.

A visual territory is a short description of the world the brand lives
in. Describe each one in words, as a designer would brief it.

For each territory give me:
- A two or three word name for it
- Three words that describe the feeling
- What it is made of: two or three real materials, surfaces or textures
- What it is NOT. Name the nearest thing it could be mistaken for, and
  say how it differs.
- One real-world reference a South African would recognise: a kind of
  shop, a workshop, a market, a place. Not a brand name.
- What this territory would make my logo look like, in one sentence
- What it would make my photographs look like, in one sentence

Rules:
- Base at least two territories on the distinctive detail in my brief.
- Do not tell me what colours mean or what emotions they cause. I am
  not interested in colour psychology.
- No mood-board words with no content: elevated, curated, timeless,
  authentic, premium, bespoke.
- At least one territory must be deliberately plain, because plain and
  consistent beats interesting and inconsistent.
- Each territory must be describable to a sign maker in one sentence.

Number them 1 to 5. Then tell me which is weakest and why. Then stop.
```

Pick one. Everything after this is derived from it.

Then paste your chosen territory into Prompt 5 (logo direction), Prompt 7 (colours and type) and Prompt 8 (photography), and add this line to each:

```
This must sit inside the visual territory I chose: [PASTE THE TERRITORY].
Anything that contradicts it is wrong, however good it looks on its own.
```

That single line is what makes the three prompts agree.

### Prompt B: materials and finishes

```
Here is my visual territory:

[PASTE THE ONE YOU CHOSE]

I am a [BUSINESS TYPE] in [TOWN/CITY], South Africa. I am the owner and
the only worker, with very little money.

Give me 5 material and finish directions that fit this territory.

For each one cover:
- Signage: what the board or panel is made of, and what finish
- Print: what my quote, invoice and business card are printed on, and
  at what weight
- Vehicle: what the lettering or graphics are cut from, and whether it
  is cut vinyl or printed
- Clothing: what a work shirt looks like and how the logo goes on it
- One cheap version and one better version of each, because I will
  start cheap

For each direction also tell me:
- What it costs relative to the others, roughly, not in rands
- What fails first in strong sun
- Whether a small sign shop in a South African town can actually make it

Rules:
- South African sun is harsh. Assume high UV.
- Nothing that needs a specialist supplier in another city.
- Nothing that needs me to order a minimum quantity of 500.
- Be honest when the cheap version is good enough. I do not want to be
  sold the better version of everything.

Number them 1 to 5. Then tell me which is weakest and why. Then stop.
```

### What the sun does to signage in South Africa

This is the part most branding advice ignores, and it is the part that costs you money twice.

South Africa sits in a Zone 3 UV climate, which is among the most intense in the world. One South African vinyl supplier, Sytech, puts the practical effect plainly: vertical vinyl that lasts up to ten years in Europe can fade in three years here, and horizontally installed graphics, like awnings or the roof of a vehicle, can lose 50 to 70% of their expected life.

Treat that as an order of magnitude rather than a precise number, but design for it.

**Cast vinyl versus calendered vinyl** is the choice your sign maker will make for you unless you ask. Cast film is made in a liquid state, has no built-in tension, conforms to curves and rivets, and typically runs 5 to 10 years outdoors. Calendered film is rolled, carries internal stress, shrinks over time, and typically runs 1 to 5 years, often 3 to 5 outdoors.

The practical rule: cast for anything on a vehicle or on a curve, calendered for flat panels and short-term work. Calendered vinyl on a vehicle commonly lifts at the edges and shrinks within two to three years, and the saving is not worth it.

**Pre-coloured film outlasts printed film**, because the colour is part of the film rather than ink sitting on top of it. If your logo is one or two flat colours, cut vinyl will outlive a printed and laminated version of the same thing. This is another argument for a simple, one or two colour logo.

**A laminate over a printed graphic extends its life.** Ask for it. It is not automatic on a cheap quote.

**Ask your sign maker two questions** before you accept a quote: is this cast or calendered, and is the print laminated. If the quote is unusually cheap, one of those two is why.

### The cheap version is usually fine

The point of Prompt B is not to talk you into expensive materials. For most one-person businesses the cheap version is the right call, because:

- Your brand will probably change something in year two
- A worn sign on a working business reads as busy, not as poor
- Money spent on a better vehicle graphic earns more than money spent on a better business card

Spend on the vehicle, because it moves and is seen. Save on the card.

### Where this fits

1. [Branding prompts](#doc-branding-prompts) Prompts 0 to 4 — brief, description, competitors, name, tagline
2. **This file** — territory, then materials
3. [Branding prompts](#doc-branding-prompts) Prompt 5 onwards, with your territory pasted into each
4. [Brand applications and polish](#doc-brand-applications-and-polish) — the files, the print specs, the quality tests

If your name is already decided, start at [Already have your name (shortcut)](#doc-already-have-your-name-shortcut) instead, then come here.

### Checklist

- [ ] Chosen one visual territory and written it down in one sentence
- [ ] Pasted it into Prompts 5, 7 and 8 with the "must sit inside" line
- [ ] Chosen a material direction, cheap version
- [ ] Asked the sign maker: cast or calendered, and is the print laminated
- [ ] Checked that my logo still works as one or two flat colours, so cut vinyl is an option

---

<a id="doc-brand-applications-and-polish"></a>

## Brand applications and polish

You have a name, a logo, colours, type and a voice. This file turns that into files you can actually use, and checks whether it looks professional before you spend money.

Most small businesses skip this step and then discover the problem at the sign maker, at the printer, or at 3am when a client asks for "the logo in vector".

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Vector | A logo file that can be made any size without going blurry. SVG, EPS, PDF, AI. |
| PNG | A picture file. Fine for screens. Goes blurry when enlarged. Can have a see-through background. |
| Transparent background | No white box around the logo. |
| Lockup | A fixed arrangement of your mark and your name. |
| Reversed | The white version of your logo, for dark backgrounds. |
| Favicon | The tiny icon in a browser tab. |
| RGB / CMYK | RGB is colour for screens. CMYK is colour for printing. They do not match exactly. |
| DPI | Dots per inch. Print needs 300. Web pictures are usually 72 and look blurry printed. |
| Bleed | Extra 3mm of colour past the edge of the paper so trimming leaves no white line. |
| Pantone | A numbered system for exact print colours. |
| Typeface / font | The style of lettering. |
| Contrast | How easily text can be read against its background. |

---

---

### Part 1: the files you must have

Make these once. Put them in one folder. Send that folder to anyone who makes anything for you.

#### Logo files

| File | Format | What it is for |
|---|---|---|
| Full colour, vector | SVG and PDF | Printers, sign makers, anything large |
| Full colour, transparent background | PNG, 2000px wide | Websites, documents, email |
| One colour black | SVG and PNG | Stamps, faxes, cheap printing, embroidery |
| One colour white (reversed) | SVG and PNG | Placing on a dark photo or coloured background |
| Square icon | PNG, 1000 x 1000 | Social profile pictures, WhatsApp Business |
| Favicon | PNG, 512 x 512 | The little icon in a browser tab |

You also want two arrangements of the same logo, called lockups:

**Horizontal** — mark next to the name. Use on letterheads, vehicle doors, website headers.

**Stacked** — mark above the name. Use in square spaces, social profiles, stamps.

A logo that only exists in one arrangement will be stretched or squashed by somebody eventually.

#### Two rules that protect the logo

**Clear space.** Decide a minimum empty space around the logo and write it into your brand guide. A simple rule: leave space equal to the height of the first letter on every side. Nothing else goes in that space.

**Minimum size.** Test how small it can go before it becomes mud. Write that number down, in millimetres for print and pixels for screen. Below that size, use the icon only.

#### Prompt: generate the file checklist for your brand

```
My logo is: [DESCRIBE IT, or say you will attach it]
My colours are: [HEX CODES]
My typefaces are: [NAMES]

Write me a one-page "brand assets" checklist listing every logo file,
size and format I need for a small South African business, and what
each one is used for.

Include: vector files, transparent versions, one-colour versions, a
reversed version, social profile sizes, a favicon, and the two lockups.

Then give me a clear space rule and a minimum size rule for my logo,
written so a printer or sign maker can follow them.

Keep it to one page in simple English.
```

---

### Part 2: print basics that save money

Printers ask questions that surprise people. These are the answers.

**RGB or CMYK.** Screens use RGB. Printing uses CMYK. A bright screen colour can print dull and muddy. Ask your printer for a CMYK version of your brand colours, and check a printed proof before you order a thousand of anything.

**Resolution.** Print needs 300 DPI. A photo pulled off Facebook is usually 72 DPI and will look blurry printed. Keep your original photo files.

**Bleed.** If a colour or a photo runs to the edge of the paper, the printer needs 3mm extra beyond the edge to trim into. Ask for "3mm bleed" and your artwork will not come back with white slivers on the edges.

**Pantone.** If exact colour matters, for example on packaging, ask about a Pantone spot colour. It costs more. For most small businesses it is not needed.

**Embroidery.** Shirts and caps need a simple, one-colour, chunky design. Fine lines and small text disappear in thread. This is why the one-colour version of your logo exists.

**Vinyl and signage.** Sign makers need vector. A PNG will be refused or will come back with soft edges.

---

### Part 3: where the brand actually gets used

Work through these in order of how much they earn you. Most one-person businesses should stop after item 6.

#### 1. WhatsApp Business profile

The most-seen thing you own. Square icon version of the logo as the profile picture. Business name, hours, address and a one-line description in your voice.

#### 2. Google Business Profile

Logo, cover photo, and at least ten real photos of real work. See [Marketing prompts](#doc-marketing-prompts).

#### 3. Your quote and invoice

Logo top left, your colours used sparingly, your body typeface throughout. Nothing else. An invoice is not the place for a design.

Templates are in [Which template to use when](#doc-which-template-to-use-when). Keep the layout plain and the details correct. A wrong VAT line matters more than a nice header.

#### 4. Email signature

Name, business name, role, phone, email, website. One line of colour at most. No large images, no quotes, no "sent from my phone", no social icons that break in half the email clients.

#### 5. Vehicle signage

Often the cheapest advertising a local business can buy, and a deductible expense.

Rules: the phone number is the biggest thing after the name. What you do must be readable in under three seconds from another lane. Two colours maximum. No fine detail. Check your municipal by-laws for size restrictions before you order.

#### 6. Business card

Still useful in South Africa, especially for trades and at markets. Name, what you do, phone, WhatsApp, email. Everything else is optional. A QR code to your WhatsApp is worth more than a website address.

#### 7. Social profile and cover images

Each platform crops differently. Keep anything important in the middle. Test your profile picture at thumbnail size on a phone before you commit.

#### 8. Signage and window

If you have premises: name, what you do, hours. Readable from across the road.

#### 9. Packaging and labels

If you sell a physical product. For food, check [Food business](#doc-food-business) first, because labelling is regulated under R146 of 2012 and getting it wrong means reprinting.

#### 10. Uniform or shirt

One-colour logo, embroidered or printed. Small on the chest, larger on the back with the phone number.

#### Prompt: plan your applications

```
My business is: [BUSINESS TYPE] in [TOWN/CITY], South Africa.
I am the owner and only worker.
My brand guide says: [PASTE THE ONE-PAGE BRAND GUIDE]

List the brand applications I should make, in order of how much each one
is likely to earn me, for my specific kind of business.

For each one tell me:
- What goes on it, exactly
- What to leave off
- The file format and size to give the supplier
- A rough idea of whether this is a do-it-yourself job or a paid job
- Whether it can wait

Be honest about which ones I do not need. I have very little money.

Stop after the list. Do not design anything yet.
```

---

### Part 4: the quality tests

Run every one of these before you spend money. They take ten minutes and they catch almost everything.

**The competitor swap test.** Put a competitor's name on your logo, tagline and description. Does it still work? If yes, it is generic.

**The one-colour test.** Print it in black only. Does it still read? If the design relies on colour to be understood, it will fail on a stamp, a fax, a photocopy and a shirt.

**The thumbnail test.** Shrink it to about the size of a WhatsApp profile picture on a phone. Can you still tell what it is?

**The squint test.** Squint at it until it blurs. Is the shape still distinctive, or does it become a grey blob?

**The photocopy test.** Print it, photocopy the print, then photocopy the copy. This is what your logo looks like after it has been through three of your customers' offices.

**The distance test.** Put it on the floor and walk five metres back. This approximates a vehicle passing you.

**The phone call test.** Say your business name and web address out loud to someone who has never heard it. Can they spell it back correctly?

**The three-second test.** Show your business card or vehicle to someone for three seconds. Ask what the business does and what the phone number was. If they cannot answer both, there is too much on it.

**The consistency test.** Put your invoice, your business card, your WhatsApp profile and one social post side by side. Do they look like the same business? Inconsistency is the most common reason small brands look amateur, and it costs nothing to fix.

---

### Part 5: the critique prompt

Use this when you think you are finished. It is written to be harsh on purpose.

```
I am a one-person business in South Africa. Here is my branding:

Business name: [NAME]
Tagline: [TAGLINE or "none"]
One-sentence description: [PASTE]
Logo: [DESCRIBE IT IN DETAIL, or attach the image]
Colours: [HEX CODES]
Typefaces: [NAMES]
Voice: [THREE WORDS]

Critique this honestly. Do not be encouraging. Assume I can take it.

Go through each of these and answer plainly:

1. Does any part of this look AI-generated or template-made? Point at
 the specific element and say why.
2. Would this still work if a competitor put their name on it? If yes,
 which parts?
3. What visual cliché from my industry am I repeating without realising?
4. Where will this break: small sizes, one colour, embroidery, a vehicle,
 a black and white print?
5. Which text-on-background pairs look risky for readability? I will
 check the exact contrast ratios myself at webaim.org, so do not
 claim to have calculated them.
6. Does the name, tagline and description all say the same thing, or do
 they contradict each other?
7. What is the single weakest element, and what would you replace it with?
8. Was any part of this generated by an image tool rather than set in a
 real font or drawn? If so, tell me which parts I may struggle to own
 or protect, and what to redo by hand.

End with the three changes that would improve this most, in order of
impact. Be specific. Do not suggest a full redesign.
```

Then run one more:

```
Based on that critique, give me 5 specific fixes for the weakest element
only. Keep everything else the same.

Number them 1 to 5. After the 5 options, stop.
```

---

### Part 6: what to pay for and what to do yourself

Do yourself, free:

- The brand brief, name, tagline and voice, using [Branding prompts](#doc-branding-prompts)
- Colours and typeface choice from Google Fonts
- Social profile and cover images in Canva
- Photography of your own work on your phone
- Your invoice and quote templates

Worth paying for, in this order, when you have money:

1. **A vector redraw of your logo.** The single highest-value design purchase for a small business. Get two or three quotes.
2. **Vehicle signage.** Earns its cost back faster than almost anything else for a local business.
3. **A sign for your premises**, if you have them.
4. **A proper photo shoot**, only once you have work worth photographing.
5. **A designer to review your brand**, cheaper than a designer to build it, and often more useful.

Not worth paying for in year one:

- A full brand identity package
- A website, before you have customers
- Printed brochures
- Branded merchandise of any kind

---

### Part 7: your brand folder

Set this up in Google Drive or on your laptop. See [Free tools](#doc-free-tools).

```
07 Brand/
├── brand-guide.pdf The one-page guide from Prompt 10
├── logo/
│ ├── logo-colour.svg
│ ├── logo-colour.pdf
│ ├── logo-colour-2000px.png
│ ├── logo-black.svg
│ ├── logo-white.svg
│ ├── icon-1000px.png
│ └── favicon-512px.png
├── fonts/ The font files you downloaded
├── colours.txt Hex codes, and CMYK from your printer
├── photos/ Your best 20 work photos, full size
└── templates/ Quote, invoice, email signature
```

Back it up somewhere other than your laptop. If your laptop is stolen and the only copy of your vector logo goes with it, you pay to have it drawn again.

---

### The one rule

Pick it, use it everywhere, and leave it alone for a year.

A consistent ordinary brand beats a brilliant brand used inconsistently. Customers recognise you by repetition, not by cleverness.

---

<a id="doc-marketing-prompts"></a>

## Marketing prompts and assets

### Where South African small businesses actually get customers

Be honest about this before you spend a day making Instagram posts.

For a local service business (plumber, hairdresser, tutor, cleaner, mechanic), most first customers come from a Google Business Profile, WhatsApp, word of mouth, and local Facebook groups. Instagram and TikTok are slow starts.

For a product business selling nationally, Facebook Marketplace, Instagram, and a WhatsApp catalogue usually come first. A full online store is a later step.

Pick two channels. Do those two properly. Being weak on six channels is worse than being good on two.

### Do these two things first, before any content

#### 1. Google Business Profile

This is free and it is the highest-value thing a local business can do. When someone searches "plumber near me" in your town, this is what decides whether you appear.

Go to business.google.com. Claim your business. Fill in everything: hours, service area, phone, WhatsApp link, categories, and at least 10 photos.

Then ask every happy customer for a review. Reviews are the single biggest factor in whether you show up above your competitors.

#### 2. WhatsApp Business

Free app, separate from normal WhatsApp. Install it on the business number.

Set up:
- A business profile with your address, hours, and a description
- A catalogue with your products or services and prices
- Quick replies for the three questions you answer every day
- An away message for after hours
- Labels to track leads: New, Quoted, Waiting, Paid

Most South African customers will contact you on WhatsApp whether you invite them to or not. Make that experience good and you will beat competitors who only have a contact form.

### Prompt 1: content pillars

```
Here is my brand brief:

[PASTE YOUR BRAND BRIEF FROM 05-branding-prompts.md]

Here is my brand voice:

[PASTE YOUR VOICE GUIDE]

Give me 5 sets of content pillars. A content pillar is a repeating topic
I post about.

Each set must have exactly 4 pillars. For each pillar give me:
- The pillar name
- Why my specific customers care about it
- Three example post ideas

Make the 5 sets genuinely different in approach. At least one set must be
built around things I can photograph with a phone while working, with no
planning.

Remember I am one person with no time and no budget.

Number the sets 1 to 5. After the 5 sets, stop. Ask me which set is
closest and what I would change.
```

### Prompt 2: a month of posts

Run this after you choose your pillars.

```
My content pillars are:

[PASTE THE 4 PILLARS YOU CHOSE]

My brand voice is:

[PASTE YOUR VOICE GUIDE]

I am a [BUSINESS TYPE] in [TOWN/CITY], South Africa.

Write me 12 posts for one month. That is 3 posts a week.

For each post give me:
- Which pillar it belongs to
- The caption, ready to paste, maximum 60 words
- What photo or video to take, described so I can shoot it on my phone
 in under 5 minutes
- 5 hashtags that a South African customer would actually search
- One line telling the reader what to do next

Rules:
- Simple English
- South African spelling and money in rands
- No emojis on every line, maximum two per post
- Do not invent claims, statistics, or customer stories
- Mention load shedding, weather, or local events only if it is genuinely
 relevant to what I sell

Spread the 12 posts across the pillars evenly.
```

### Prompt 3: WhatsApp messages you send every week

```
I am a [BUSINESS TYPE] in South Africa. My brand voice is:

[PASTE YOUR VOICE GUIDE]

Write me short WhatsApp message templates for these 8 situations. Each
one must be under 60 words, polite, and easy to read on a phone.

1. Someone asks "how much?" and I need more information before quoting
2. Sending a quote
3. Following up on a quote after 3 days with no reply
4. Confirming a booking with date, time, and address
5. Reminding a customer the day before
6. Telling a customer I am running late
7. Asking for payment politely when an invoice is overdue
8. Asking for a Google review after a job went well

Use [SQUARE BRACKETS] for anything I must fill in.

Give me each message twice: once in English and once in a warmer, less
formal tone.
```

Save these as Quick Replies in WhatsApp Business. You will use them every day.

### Prompt 4: your Google Business Profile description

```
I am a [BUSINESS TYPE] serving [AREAS] in South Africa.

What I do: [DESCRIBE IN YOUR OWN WORDS]
What makes me different: [DESCRIBE]

Write 5 different Google Business Profile descriptions.

Rules:
- Maximum 700 characters each
- Include the words a customer would actually type into Google
- Name the areas I serve
- No marketing language
- Say what to do next

Also give me a list of the Google Business categories I should choose,
primary and secondary.

Number them 1 to 5. After the 5 options, stop.
```

### Prompt 5: answering the questions that stop a sale

```
I am a [BUSINESS TYPE] in South Africa selling [PRODUCT OR SERVICE] at
around [PRICE].

List the 10 questions or worries that most often stop a South African
customer from buying from a small, unknown business like mine.

For each one, write a short answer I can use on WhatsApp, on my price
list, and on social media. Under 40 words each.

Be honest. Do not write answers that make promises I cannot keep.

Then tell me which three of these I should answer publicly, before the
customer even asks.
```

This one is usually the most useful prompt in this file. The answers become your FAQ, your sales script, and half your content.

### Making the images

Free tools:

- Canva free plan — social templates, sizes already set, brand colours saveable
- Photopea — free in the browser, opens PSD files
- GIMP — free desktop photo editor
- Your phone camera — better than any stock photo for a local business

Advice: real photos of your real work beat designed graphics almost every time for a small local business. A clear photo of a finished job, taken in daylight, outperforms a polished graphic from a template.

Keep image files small. Many of your customers pay for data by the megabyte. Export social images under 500 KB.

### What to ignore in your first year

Paid advertising. Do not spend money on ads until you know exactly which message converts. Organic posts and a Google Business Profile teach you that for free.

Posting daily. Three good posts a week beats seven rushed ones. Nobody is counting.

Being on every platform. If you are not going to answer messages on a platform within a day, do not open an account there.

A blog. Only worth it if search traffic is genuinely how your customers find your kind of business, and only if you will write it for a year.

Buying followers or joining engagement groups. It damages your reach and it sells nothing.

### The one metric that matters

Count how many people contacted you this month, and how many of those bought.

Not likes. Not followers. Enquiries and sales.

Write these two numbers down every month in the same spreadsheet where you track money. After three months you will know which channel is actually working, and you can stop doing the other one.

---

<a id="doc-which-template-to-use-when"></a>

## Documents and templates

You need six documents to run a small business properly. Everything else can wait.

1. A quote
2. An invoice
3. A receipt
4. Terms and conditions
5. A privacy notice
6. A cash book

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Quote / quotation | A price you offer before the work. Not yet a sale. |
| Invoice | The bill you send after the work, asking for payment. |
| Receipt | Proof you received the money. |
| Credit note | A document that cancels or reduces an invoice you already sent. Use it instead of deleting an invoice. |
| Qty | Quantity. How many. |
| Unit price | The price for one. |
| Subtotal | The total before VAT is added. |
| excl. / incl. VAT | Excluding VAT (before it is added) / including VAT (after). |
| Due date | The last day the customer may pay without being late. |
| EFT | Electronic funds transfer. A bank payment. |
| Branch code | The number that identifies your bank branch. Ask your bank. |
| Abridged tax invoice | A shorter VAT invoice allowed for sales between R50 and R5,000. |
| Recipient | The customer. The person or business receiving the goods. |
| Reconcile | Check that your records and your bank statement agree. |
| Odometer | The kilometre counter on the dashboard. |
| Deposit | Money paid up front before work starts. |

---

Build these once, in the tool you chose in [Free tools](#doc-free-tools), and reuse them.

### Invoice numbering

Number invoices in order with no gaps: INV-0001, INV-0002, INV-0003.

A gap in the sequence looks like a hidden sale to SARS. If you cancel an invoice, do not delete it. Issue a credit note against it and keep both.

Use the same idea for quotes: QUO-0001.

---

### If you are a Pty Ltd: the header line that is required by law

Every quote, invoice, receipt, delivery note and letter must carry the company's full registered name and registration number, under section 32 of the Companies Act. If you use a trading name, show both:

```
MOKOENA MOTORS
A trading name of Mokoena Holdings (Pty) Ltd | Reg. No. 2026/123456/07
```

The templates below show `[Company registration number, if you have one]`. For a company it is not optional.

### Template 1: quotation

```
[YOUR BUSINESS NAME]
[Your name] | [Phone] | [Email]
[Physical address]
[Company registration number, if you have one]

QUOTATION

Quote number: QUO-0001
Date: [DD Month YYYY]
Valid until: [DD Month YYYY]

TO
[Customer name]
[Customer address]
[Customer phone / email]

-------------------------------------------------------------
Description Qty Unit price Amount
-------------------------------------------------------------
[Item or service] 1 R 0.00 R 0.00
[Item or service] 1 R 0.00 R 0.00
-------------------------------------------------------------
 TOTAL R 0.00
-------------------------------------------------------------

WHAT IS INCLUDED
- [Be specific]
- [Be specific]

WHAT IS NOT INCLUDED
- [List it, or you will be asked to do it free]

TIMING
Work starts [when] and takes approximately [how long] once the deposit
is received.

PAYMENT
Deposit of [X]% before work starts. Balance due [on completion / within
X days of invoice].

This quote is valid for [X] days. Prices may change after that date.
Accepting this quote means you accept my terms and conditions, attached
or available at [link].

Accepted by: ______________________ Date: ______________
```

Three rules that save you money:

Put a validity date on every quote. Material prices move.

Write down what is not included. Most disputes are about work the customer assumed was in the price.

Get a deposit before you buy materials. A signed quote plus a deposit is a contract.

---

### Template 2: invoice if you are NOT registered for VAT

Most small businesses are here. Turnover under R2.3 million a year means you are not required to register.

Rules you must follow: do not use the words "Tax Invoice". Do not show a VAT line. Do not show a VAT number. Do not add 15% to anything.

If you are not registered for VAT, issue a regular invoice showing your total price. Do not mention VAT, do not include a VAT line, and do not use the words "Tax Invoice".

```
[YOUR BUSINESS NAME]
[Your name] | [Phone] | [Email]
[Physical address]
[Company registration number, if you have one]

INVOICE

Invoice number: INV-0001
Date: [DD Month YYYY]
Due date: [DD Month YYYY]

TO
[Customer name]
[Customer address]

-------------------------------------------------------------
Description Qty Unit price Amount
-------------------------------------------------------------
[Item or service] 1 R 0.00 R 0.00
-------------------------------------------------------------
 AMOUNT DUE R 0.00
-------------------------------------------------------------

PAYMENT DETAILS
Bank: [Bank name]
Account name: [Exactly as it appears on the account]
Account number: [Number]
Branch code: [Code]
Reference: INV-0001

Please use the invoice number as your payment reference.
Payment is due by [date]. [State your late payment terms here.]
```

---

### Template 3: invoice if you ARE registered for VAT

Only use this once SARS has issued your VAT number.

There are three levels, based on the total including VAT.

A tax invoice is not required where the amount is R50 or less, although you still need supporting documentation for an input tax deduction.

For supplies from R50 up to R5,000, an abridged tax invoice is allowed. It needs most of the same information, but you do not have to include the recipient's details.

For a supply of more than R5,000, a full tax invoice must contain: the words "Tax Invoice", "VAT Invoice" or "Invoice"; the supplier's name, address and VAT registration number; the recipient's name, address and, where they are registered, their VAT registration number; a serial number and the date of issue; and a description of the goods or services supplied and the quantity or volume. It must also show the value of the supply, the VAT charged, and the total.

> **In plain words:** For any sale over R5,000, your invoice must show: the words "Tax Invoice", your name, address and VAT number, the customer's name and address (and their VAT number if they have one), an invoice number and date, what you sold and how much of it, the price before VAT, the VAT, and the total.

You must issue the document within 21 days of completing the sale or delivering the service.

All the required criteria must be met. SARS checks invoices against these criteria, and if a single one is missing the entire invoice is disallowed.

```
[YOUR BUSINESS NAME]
[Physical address]
VAT Registration Number: 4XXXXXXXXX
[Phone] | [Email]

TAX INVOICE

Invoice number: INV-0001
Date: [DD Month YYYY]

TO
[Customer name]
[Customer physical address]
[Customer VAT number, if they are a vendor]

-------------------------------------------------------------
Description Qty Unit price Amount
-------------------------------------------------------------
[Goods or services] 1 R 0.00 R 0.00
-------------------------------------------------------------
 Subtotal (excl. VAT) R 0.00
 VAT @ 15% R 0.00
 TOTAL (incl. VAT) R 0.00
-------------------------------------------------------------

[Banking details]
```

Working with 15%: to add VAT, multiply by 1.15. To find the VAT inside a VAT-inclusive price, multiply by 15/115.

If you sell second-hand goods, the description must say so.

---

### Template 4: receipt

Send this when money arrives. It stops the "did you get my payment?" message.

```
[YOUR BUSINESS NAME]

RECEIPT

Receipt number: REC-0001
Date received: [DD Month YYYY]
Received from: [Customer name]
Amount received: R 0.00
Method: [EFT / Cash / Card]
For invoice: INV-0001

Balance still owing: R 0.00

Thank you.
```

---

### Template 5: privacy notice (POPIA)

Put this on your website, or send it as a PDF when a customer asks. It is short on purpose.

```
PRIVACY NOTICE

[Business name] ("we", "us") respects your privacy. This notice explains
what personal information we collect and what we do with it.

WHO WE ARE
[Business name]
[Physical address]
[Email] | [Phone]
Information Officer: [Your full name]

WHAT WE COLLECT
- Your name
- Your contact details (phone, email, address)
- Details of what you bought or asked about
- Payment information (we do not store card numbers)
[Add or remove lines so this is true for your business]

WHY WE COLLECT IT
- To give you a quote and do the work you asked for
- To send you an invoice and receive payment
- To keep records that SARS requires by law
- To contact you about your order

WHO WE SHARE IT WITH
We do not sell your information. We share it only with:
- Our bank, to receive payment
- Our accountant, if we use one
- SARS or another authority, where the law requires it
[Add any supplier or courier who receives customer details]

HOW LONG WE KEEP IT
We keep records for five years, as tax law requires. After that we
delete them.

MARKETING
We will only send you marketing messages if you agreed to receive them.
You can tell us to stop at any time by replying STOP or emailing us.

YOUR RIGHTS
You may ask us what information we hold about you, ask us to correct it,
or ask us to delete it where the law allows. Contact us using the
details above.

If you are unhappy with how we handled your information, you may
complain to the Information Regulator at inforegulator.org.za.

Last updated: [DD Month YYYY]
```

Register yourself as the Information Officer at inforegulator.org.za. It is free. See [Register: what you actually need](#doc-register-what-you-actually-need).

---

### Template 6: cash book

One spreadsheet. Seven columns. This is your whole accounting system in year one.

| Date | Description | Customer or supplier | Money in | Money out | Category | Slip? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

Categories to use: Sales, Stock, Materials, Fuel, Data and airtime, Tools and equipment, Rent, Bank charges, Advertising, Insurance, Other.

Rules:

Enter every transaction. Every one, including the R38 you spent on cable ties.

Take a photo of every slip the day you get it. Put it in a folder named by month. Tick the "Slip?" column.

Reconcile against your bank statement once a month. If the spreadsheet total and the bank balance disagree, find out why that same day.

If you use turnover tax, your records are lighter. You only track amounts received, dividends declared, and assets or liabilities over R10,000.

---

### Template 7: vehicle logbook

Required by SARS for any travel claim. See [Vehicles for your business](#doc-vehicles-for-your-business).

| Date | Odometer start | Odometer end | Km | Destination | Business reason |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|

Record your odometer on 1 March and again on 28 or 29 February so you have total kilometres for the tax year.

Fill it in when you park, not at the end of the month.

---

### Prompts to generate the rest

#### Terms and conditions

```
I am a [BUSINESS TYPE] in South Africa. I sell [WHAT] to [WHO].
I am [a sole proprietor / a registered company].
[I sell online / I sell in person / both.]

Write terms and conditions for my business in simple English that a
Grade 8 learner can read.

Cover:
- What I will do and what the customer must do
- Quotes, deposits, and payment terms
- What happens if a customer cancels
- What happens if I have to cancel
- Delivery or completion timing
- Returns, refunds, and faults
- Limits on what I am responsible for
- How disputes get resolved

Make sure it complies with the Consumer Protection Act 68 of 2008.
Where the Act gives the customer a right I cannot remove, say so in the
document rather than trying to remove it.

[If selling online, add:] I sell online, so also comply with sections 43,
44, and 46 of the Electronic Communications and Transactions Act 25 of
2002, including the seven-day cooling-off right.

After the document, list every clause where you are unsure and where I
should check with a lawyer.
```

The Consumer Protection Act requires plain language. A document your customer cannot understand can be unenforceable, so do not ask the AI to make it sound legal.

#### A simple service agreement

```
I am a [BUSINESS TYPE] in South Africa. I need a short written agreement
for jobs over R[AMOUNT].

Write a one-page agreement in simple English covering:
- Who the parties are
- Exactly what work I will do
- Exactly what is not included
- Price, deposit, and payment dates
- Start date and expected finish date
- What happens if the customer changes the scope
- What happens if the customer does not pay
- Who owns the work when it is done
- How either side can cancel
- Which South African law applies

Use [SQUARE BRACKETS] for anything I fill in per job.
Keep it to one page. Do not use legal words where a plain word works.
```

#### Delivery note (only if you deliver physical goods)

```
Write a simple delivery note template for a South African small business
delivering [WHAT] to customers. Include fields for delivery number, date,
customer details, items and quantities, condition on arrival, and a
signature block for the person receiving the goods. Keep it to one page.
```

---

### Documents you probably do not need yet

A business plan. Only needed to raise money from a bank or investor.

An employment contract. Only when you hire someone. When you do, get a proper one, because the Basic Conditions of Employment Act is strict and mistakes are expensive.

A non-disclosure agreement. Rarely needed by a small supplier. Most clients who need one will send you theirs.

A shareholders agreement. Only if you have more than one shareholder. If you ever take a partner, get this before you take their money, not after.

A company stamp. It has no legal meaning in South Africa.

---

<a id="doc-free-tools"></a>

## Free tools

### Which office software to use

Three free options are worth considering. They are not equally good for a South African one-person business.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Browser | The app you use to open websites: Chrome, Safari, Firefox. |
| Offline | Working with no internet. |
| Sync | Your files copy themselves to the cloud automatically. If you delete one, it deletes everywhere. |
| Backup | A separate copy you make on purpose, kept somewhere else. |
| PDF | A file format that looks the same on every device and cannot easily be changed. Send customers PDFs. |
| Vector | A logo file that can be made any size without going blurry. SVG is a vector format. |
| Hotspot | Using your phone's data to give your laptop internet. |
| UPS | A battery box that keeps your router on during load shedding. |
| Domain | Your website address, like yourname.co.za. |

---

#### Google Docs, Sheets and Slides

Free with any Gmail account. Runs in a browser and on a phone.

Strengths: works on a cheap phone, saves automatically, easy to share a link with a customer, easy to work from a tablet at a job site, and your files survive if your laptop is stolen. Templates are easy to duplicate.

Weaknesses: needs internet for most things. Weaker at exact page layout than the others. Complex Word files from clients can shift when opened.

#### LibreOffice

Free desktop software. Download from libreoffice.org. Runs on Windows, Mac, and Linux.

Strengths: fully offline, so no data cost and no problem during load shedding if your laptop is charged. Strong at exact page layout. Good mail merge for sending the same letter to many customers. Excellent PDF export.

Weaknesses: no practical mobile editing. There is an Android viewer, but you will not run your admin from a phone on it. The menus look different from Microsoft Office and take a week to get used to. Nothing is backed up unless you do it.

#### OnlyOffice

Free desktop editors from onlyoffice.com, plus a free personal cloud tier.

Strengths: the closest match to Microsoft Office. If a client sends you a.docx with tricky formatting and it must come back looking identical, OnlyOffice usually handles it best. The interface looks like Microsoft Office, so there is almost nothing to learn.

Weaknesses: smaller community, so fewer free templates and less help online. The free cloud tier has limits.

### The recommendation

Use Google Docs and Google Sheets as your daily tools. Install LibreOffice on your laptop as a backup.

Why:

You will do most of your admin on your phone, between jobs. Google works there. LibreOffice does not.

Your quote and invoice templates live in one place and you duplicate them for each job. That is easier in Google Drive than in folders on a laptop.

If your laptop is stolen or the hard drive dies, your business does not stop. This matters more than any feature.

LibreOffice covers you when there is no data and no signal, and it makes better PDFs for anything you print.

Add OnlyOffice only if you regularly receive Word documents from corporate clients that must be edited and returned without any change in formatting. If that is not your situation, skip it.

### Set up Google Drive properly on day one

Folders, in this structure:

```
01 Admin (registration documents, tax documents, licences)
02 Templates (quote, invoice, terms, privacy notice)
03 Quotes (one file per quote, named QUO-0001 Customer Name)
04 Invoices (one file per invoice, named INV-0001 Customer Name)
05 Slips (one sub-folder per month: 2026-03, 2026-04,...)
06 Customers (one folder per regular customer)
07 Brand (logo files, colours, fonts, brand guide)
```

Name files so they sort correctly. Put the number first, and use dates as YYYY-MM-DD. `2026-09-13 Invoice Nkosi.pdf` sorts properly. `13 Sept invoice.pdf` does not.

Always send a PDF to customers, never the editable file. In Google Docs: File, then Download, then PDF. In LibreOffice there is a PDF button on the toolbar.

### Other free tools worth having

Email with your own domain. A `@gmail.com` address on a quote looks less established than `you@yourbusiness.co.za`. Google Workspace charges per user per month. Zoho Mail has offered a free tier for a small number of users on a custom domain, so check whether it is still available before you pay for anything. Your domain seller may also include basic email with the domain, which is often enough.

Invoicing software. You do not need it in year one. A Google Sheets template is fine. When you outgrow that, Wave and Zoho Invoice both have free tiers, and several South African bank business accounts include basic invoicing.

E-signatures. A signed PDF is legally valid in South Africa for most ordinary business contracts under ECTA. For simple agreements, a typed name plus a returned email is usually sufficient. Some documents, such as property transfers and wills, require a real handwritten signature. Do not use electronic signatures for those.

Password manager. Bitwarden has a free plan. Use it. You will have logins for SARS, CIPC, your bank, your domain, and every social account. Writing them in a notebook is how businesses get robbed.

Backups. Google Drive is a sync, not a backup. If you delete a file by mistake, it deletes everywhere. Once a month, download your key folders to a USB drive and keep it somewhere else.

Design. Canva free plan for social images. Inkscape for logo vector files. Photopea in the browser for editing photos.

Fonts. fonts.google.com. Free and free for commercial use.

### Practical notes for South African conditions

Load shedding. Keep your phone charged as a hotspot. Keep a copy of today's quote or invoice offline. A small UPS for a router costs a few hundred rand and saves hours.

Data cost. Turn off automatic photo backup on mobile data. Set Google Drive to sync on Wi-Fi only. Export images for social media under 500 KB.

Working offline. Google Docs has an offline mode, but you must switch it on before you lose signal, on each device. Do it now, not on the day you need it.

### What to avoid

Cracked Microsoft Office. It is illegal, it carries malware, and it will fail at the worst time. LibreOffice and OnlyOffice do the same job legally and free.

Paying for accounting software in year one. A spreadsheet and a folder of photographed slips is enough until you are past about R500,000 turnover or have stock to track.

Buying a website builder subscription before you have a customer. A Google Business Profile and a WhatsApp catalogue cost nothing and often convert better.

---

### Three rules that save you money

> Put a validity date on every quote. Material prices move.

Write down what is not included. This is the section that prevents arguments.

Get a deposit before you buy materials. A signed quote plus a deposit is a contract.

<a id="doc-invoice-not-vat-registered-template"></a>

## INVOICE

**Use this one if you are NOT registered for VAT.** That is most small businesses. You are not required to register until your turnover passes R2.3 million in any rolling 12 months.

> Three rules you must follow on this invoice. Do NOT use the words "Tax Invoice". Do NOT show a VAT line. Do NOT show a VAT number. Do NOT add 15% to anything. Charging VAT when you are not registered is fraud, not a paperwork mistake.

*Make a copy, rename it to the invoice number, replace everything in [SQUARE BRACKETS], then export to PDF.*

---

**[YOUR BUSINESS NAME]**
[Your name] | [Phone] | [Email]
[Physical address]
[If a company: Trading as a name of [REGISTERED NAME] (Pty) Ltd, Reg. No. [NUMBER]]

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| Invoice number | INV-0001 |
| Date | [DD Month YYYY] |
| Due date | [DD Month YYYY] |

### To

[Customer name]
[Customer address]

### What this is for

| Description | Qty | Unit price | Amount |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Item or service] | 1 | R 0.00 | R 0.00 |
| [Item or service] | 1 | R 0.00 | R 0.00 |
| **AMOUNT DUE** |  |  | **R 0.00** |

### Payment details

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| Bank | [Bank name] |
| Account name | [Exactly as it appears on the account] |
| Account number | [Number] |
| Branch code | [Code] |
| Reference | INV-0001 |

Please use the invoice number as your payment reference.

Payment is due by [date]. [State your late payment terms here.]

---

### Invoice numbering

Number invoices in order with no gaps: INV-0001, INV-0002, INV-0003.

> A gap in the sequence looks like a hidden sale to SARS. If you cancel an invoice, do not delete it. Issue a credit note against it and keep both.

### Before you hand anything over

Wait until the money reflects in your account balance, not "pending". A screenshot, an SMS or a PDF proof of payment is not money.

---

<a id="doc-tax-invoice-vat-registered-template"></a>

## TAX INVOICE

**Only use this once SARS has issued your VAT number.** If you are not registered, use the other invoice template instead.

> SARS checks tax invoices against a fixed list. If one required item is missing, the whole invoice can be disallowed. No tax invoice is required at R50 or less. An abridged tax invoice is allowed from R50 up to R5,000. A full tax invoice is required above R5,000. You must issue it within 21 days of the sale.

---

**[YOUR BUSINESS NAME]**
[Physical address]
**VAT Registration Number: 4XXXXXXXXX**
[Phone] | [Email]
[If a company: Trading as a name of [REGISTERED NAME] (Pty) Ltd, Reg. No. [NUMBER]]

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| Invoice number | INV-0001 |
| Date of issue | [DD Month YYYY] |

### To

[Customer name]
[Customer physical address]
[Customer VAT number, if they are a vendor — required on invoices over R5,000]

### Supply

| Description | Qty | Unit price | Amount |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Goods or services supplied] | 1 | R 0.00 | R 0.00 |
| Subtotal (excl. VAT) |  |  | R 0.00 |
| VAT @ 15% |  |  | R 0.00 |
| **TOTAL (incl. VAT)** |  |  | **R 0.00** |

### Payment details

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| Bank | [Bank name] |
| Account name | [Exactly as it appears on the account] |
| Account number | [Number] |
| Branch code | [Code] |
| Reference | INV-0001 |

---

### The seven things a full tax invoice must show

For any supply over R5,000:

1. The words "Tax Invoice", "VAT Invoice" or "Invoice"
2. Your name, address and VAT registration number
3. The customer's name and address, and their VAT number if they are registered
4. A serial number and the date of issue
5. A description of the goods or services supplied
6. The quantity or volume supplied
7. The value of the supply, the VAT charged, and the total

### Working with 15%

To add VAT to a price, multiply by 1.15. To find the VAT inside a VAT-inclusive price, multiply by 15/115.

If you sell second-hand goods, the description must say so.

---

*Put this on your website, or send it as a PDF when a customer asks. It is short on purpose. Make a copy, replace the [SQUARE BRACKETS], delete any line that is not true for your business.*

> Before you use this, register yourself as the Information Officer at inforegulator.org.za. It is free. POPIA makes registration compulsory for all private and public bodies. There is no small-business exemption. For a one-person business, the Information Officer is you.

### If you take ID copies

Vehicle dealers, second-hand goods dealers and anyone who checks a buyer's identity will hold ID copies. Keep them somewhere safe, keep them only as long as you need them, and never forward them. If a deal does not go ahead, delete the copies.

<a id="doc-pick-your-business-type"></a>

## Pick your business type

The core files in `01-core/` apply to everyone. This folder adds the rules that apply only to your kind of business.

Find yourself in the table, then open that file.

| If you... | Open |
|---|---|
| Buy and sell vehicles | [Vehicle dealer](#doc-vehicle-dealer) |
| Make, cook, package or sell food or drinks | [Food business](#doc-food-business) |
| Do hair, nails, beauty, massage, tattoos or piercings | [Beauty and personal care](#doc-beauty-and-personal-care) |
| Sell physical products, in a shop, at markets, or online | [Retail and online shop](#doc-retail-and-online-shop) |
| Fix, build, install, clean or deliver | [Services and trades](#doc-services-and-trades) |
| Sell your skill or your time: design, consulting, coding, tutoring, writing, photography | [Professional and creative work](#doc-professional-and-creative-work) |

If two files apply to you, read both. A food truck is a food business and a vehicle owner. An online clothing brand is retail and online.

### How much regulation each type carries

This is a rough guide to how much setup work to expect before you can legally trade.

| Business type | Setup effort | Main reason |
|---|---|---|
| Professional and creative | Lowest | Usually no licence at all |
| Services and trades | Low to medium | Some trades need professional registration |
| Retail and online | Medium | Consumer law, and product rules for some goods |
| Beauty and personal care | Medium to high | Health inspection and municipal licence |
| Food | High | Certificate of Acceptability, training, inspections |
| Vehicle dealing | High | Three separate registration layers |

If you are choosing between business ideas and money is tight, the left column of this table is worth reading as a cost comparison. A food business can take two to six months before it may legally open. A consulting business can invoice tomorrow.

### What everyone needs, regardless of type

From `01-core/`:

- A separate bank account for business money
- SARS registration and filing, even at zero income
- POPIA information officer registration, free, at inforegulator.org.za
- Honest advertising and Consumer Protection Act compliance
- Records kept for five years

### What nobody needs on day one

- A registered company, unless a customer demands it
- VAT registration, unless turnover passes R2.3 million or the maths favours it
- A paid B-BBEE certificate, since the affidavit is free under R10 million turnover
- A business plan, unless you are asking someone for money
- A website, before you have customers

---

<a id="doc-vehicle-dealer"></a>

## Business type: buying and selling vehicles

Read `01-core/` first. This file adds the rules that apply only to a vehicle dealer.

Vehicle dealing is one of the most regulated small businesses in South Africa. It has three registration layers that other businesses do not have, and one legal risk that can destroy a small dealer in a single transaction.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| SAPS 601 | The free form to register as a second-hand goods dealer with the police. |
| Register (noun) | The book or spreadsheet where you record every vehicle bought and sold. |
| VIN | The vehicle identification number, stamped on the vehicle. |
| BRNC | The certificate that lets a company own vehicles on eNaTIS. |
| Motor trade number | A special number that lets a dealer drive unregistered stock on public roads. |
| Voetstoots | Afrikaans for "as is". Selling with no responsibility for faults. No longer works when a dealer sells to the public. |
| Implied warranty | The six-month guarantee the law adds to every dealer sale. |
| Notional input tax | VAT you can claim back on a vehicle bought from a private person, even though they charged no VAT. |
| VAT264 | The form you and the seller sign so you can claim that VAT. |
| Title holder | Whoever has the right to sell the vehicle. If it is financed, that is the bank. |
| NCO | The form the seller lodges to say the vehicle has changed hands. |
| RLV | The form the buyer lodges to register the vehicle. |
| Roadworthy | A certificate from a testing station that the vehicle is safe. Needed on every sale. |

---

Read the Consumer Protection Act section before you buy your first vehicle. It is the part that costs people money.

---

### Quick answer: what you must have

Required, in this order:

1. A registered business, if you want to hold vehicles in a business name (see `01-core/02`)
2. A Business Register Number Certificate or Traffic Register Number, so vehicles can be registered to the business
3. Registration as a second-hand goods dealer with SAPS (free, but compulsory)
4. A municipal business licence, in most municipalities
5. Correct trading premises zoning
6. A written sale agreement that complies with the Consumer Protection Act
7. Insurance covering stock vehicles and test drives

Optional but often needed:

8. Motor trade numbers, if you drive unregistered stock on public roads
9. VAT registration, which becomes valuable earlier than for most businesses
10. NCR registration, if you ever extend credit yourself
11. An FSP licence, if you sell insurance, warranties, or finance products

---

### Layer 1: SAPS second-hand goods dealer registration

This is compulsory and most small dealers do not know about it.

The Second-Hand Goods Act 6 of 2009 sits under the Minister of Police. Its purpose is to stop trade in stolen goods.

The principal business activities that must register include an Auctioneer, General Dealer, Jeweller, Motor Vehicle Dealer and Scrap metal Dealer. The Act applies to a person who carries on a business of dealing in second-hand goods.

Second-hand goods are items worth over R100 that have been in use by a person other than the manufacturer or producer. Dealers must register with SAPS, keep detailed registers of transactions for five years, verify supplier identities, and report suspected stolen items.

> **In plain words:** If you buy and sell used goods, you must register with the police, write down every item you buy and sell, keep those records for five years, check who you are buying from, and report anything that looks stolen.

#### How to register

Obtain the application form from the office of the Designated Police Officer or from the SAPS website at www.saps.gov.za, complete and sign it, and lodge the completed form and all required documents with the office of the Designated Police Officer.

> **In plain words:** Get the form from your police station or the SAPS website, fill it in, sign it, and hand it in at the station with the documents they ask for.

The form is the SAPS 601. No payment is required. Registration is free.

Required documents: two colour ID photos of the applicant no older than 3 months, and a certified copy of the RSA ID document or foreign passport of the applicant and of every person responsible for the day-to-day management of the business.

> **In plain words:** Bring two recent colour ID photos and a certified copy of the ID of everyone who runs the business day to day.

Any person other than a natural person may only be registered if a natural person who is not disqualified under the Act is appointed to manage the business. For a company, you name yourself as the responsible person.

After considering the application, the National Commissioner may issue a certificate of registration or a temporary registration, depending on the circumstances.

Apply at the SAPS station in the area where your business premises are situated.

#### The register you must keep

This is the part that gets dealers in trouble during an inspection.

A dealer dealing in second-hand motor vehicles must record in the prescribed register the particulars of every acquisition and disposal of a motor vehicle, including: the vehicle identification number (VIN) and the chassis and engine number; the odometer reading; the exterior and trim colour; and any distinguishing mark or feature, such as microdot particulars.

> **In plain words:** For every vehicle you buy or sell, write down in your register: the VIN, the chassis number, the engine number, the mileage, the colours inside and out, and any special marks like microdots.

You also record who you bought from and who you sold to.

A person acquiring or disposing of a motor vehicle from or to a dealer must furnish the dealer with proof of identity. The dealer must obtain and keep a copy of the identity document or passport, and must obtain and keep proof of registration or deregistration. The dealer must retain these copies for not less than five years from the date of the transaction.

> **In plain words:** Anyone you buy a vehicle from, or sell one to, must show you their ID. You keep a copy of it, plus proof of the vehicle's registration, for five years.

Practical rule: never take a vehicle in without a photocopy of the seller's ID and the registration certificate. Never. A vehicle bought from someone who will not show ID is either stolen or about to become your problem.

#### Keeping your registration valid

The holder of a certificate of registration must notify the National Commissioner in writing within 30 days if there is any change to the information submitted in the application, a change in the control or ownership of the dealer, or any change that affects the dealer's ability to meet the requirements for registration.

> **In plain words:** If anything on your SAPS dealer registration changes, including what you deal in or where, you must tell SAPS in writing within 30 days.

If the responsible person recorded on the certificate changes, the dealer must complete a SAPS 604 form and submit it to the SAPS station where the original application was made, even if the certificate has not expired.

> **In plain words:** If the person named on your certificate as running the business changes, fill in form SAPS 604 and hand it in at the same station, even if your certificate has not expired.

If an application for renewal is lodged within the required period, the registration remains valid until the renewal is decided. Diarise your renewal date.

#### Inspections

A police official may require the dealer or the person in control of the premises to produce the certificate of registration relating to those premises for inspection.

Keep the certificate on the wall at the premises, not in a drawer at home.

---

### Layer 2: getting vehicles registered to the business

A vehicle you hold as stock has to sit somewhere on the national system.

#### If you trade as a company

You need a Business Register Number Certificate (BRNC). Full process, documents, costs, and the proxy rules are in [Vehicles for your business](#doc-vehicles-for-your-business).

Short version: eNaTIS identifies every vehicle owner by an identity number. A company does not have one, so the registering authority issues it a business register number the first time it needs to own a vehicle. You apply in person at your local traffic department with form ABR, and you nominate a proxy.

#### If you trade as a sole proprietor

You have two routes.

Register vehicles against your own ID number. Simple, and legal.

Or apply for a Traffic Register Number. If you are an organisation that serves as a non-legal person, for example a partnership, a one-man business, a church, a trust, or a statutory body, you must apply for a traffic register number to register motor vehicles in South Africa.

> **In plain words:** If you trade under a business name but are not a registered company, you can get a traffic register number so vehicles can be registered under that name.

A traffic register number lets a trading name appear on the vehicle record rather than only your personal ID. Apply at your registering authority. Bring proof of address, ID photographs, and the prescribed fee.

For a dealer, a TRN or BRNC is worth having. It separates stock vehicles from your personal vehicle on the system, which matters when fines arrive.

---

### Layer 3: motor trade numbers

If you drive an unregistered or unlicensed vehicle on a public road as part of your business, you need this.

To drive an unregistered vehicle on a public road in South Africa you must have a motor trade permit and number. A motor trade number is used by registered manufacturers, builders or importers, motor transport contractors, motor dealers or financial institutions to operate an unregistered and unlicensed motor vehicle on a public road in the course of their business.

> **In plain words:** You may not drive an unregistered or unlicensed vehicle on a public road unless you have a motor trade number. Dealers, manufacturers, importers, transporters and banks can get them for business use.

A motor dealer may use it to deliver, sell, exchange or repair a motor vehicle.

#### How to apply

Complete the Application for motor trade number (MTN1) form. Indicate why you need trade numbers and how many you need. The application is processed on the same day. Get the fees from your local registering authority.

The applicant must apply for a motor trade number and then license the motor trade number. You need the identification of the company, which is the business registration certificate or traffic register number.

#### Cost

Fees are set per province. As published by the Western Cape Government:

Application for each motor trade number: R102. Licensing of a motor trade number for a motor vehicle other than a motor cycle, by a motor dealer, manufacturer, builder, importer or deposit-taking institution: R762. Licensing for a motor cycle: R168. Licensing by a motor transport contractor: R168.

> **In plain words:** In the Western Cape: R102 to apply for each number, then R762 a year to licence it for a car or bakkie, or R168 for a motorcycle. Other provinces charge differently.

Confirm the current figure with your own province. Fees differ.

#### Do you actually need one

Only if you move unlicensed stock on public roads. If every vehicle you buy is already registered and licensed, and you only move it after transfer, you can trade without trade numbers.

Most working dealers end up needing at least one, because you will buy a vehicle with an expired licence at some point.

---

### The legal risk that matters most: the Consumer Protection Act

This is the section to read twice. Get this wrong once on a R200,000 vehicle and the profit from ten deals disappears.

#### Voetstoots does not protect you

Section 56 provides an implied six-month warranty of quality, allowing consumers to return defective goods within six months and choose a repair, replacement, or refund. As a result of this implied warranty, dealers can no longer rely on "voetstoots" or "as-is" clauses in CPA-governed sales. Such clauses remain relevant only in private sales under common law.

> **In plain words:** Every vehicle you sell to a private person comes with a six-month guarantee, whether you write it down or not. If it breaks, the customer chooses: fix it, replace it, or refund. If your repair fails within three months, you must replace or refund. "Sold as is" does nothing in a dealer sale.

The Consumer Tribunal has ruled that a voetstoots sale does not apply to any transaction falling under the CPA, and that this kind of sale is also not allowed in terms of section 5 of the CPA.

Section 2(10) provides that no provision of the CPA may be interpreted as excluding a right a consumer would have under the common law, and section 56(4) states that the implied warranty of quality is in addition to any other warranty under common law. So a used car dealer cannot say he already guarantees the car for two years and that the terms of that guarantee apply instead.

> **In plain words:** The six-month guarantee is added on top of any other guarantee. You cannot replace it with your own warranty terms, even a longer one.

Putting "sold voetstoots" on your invoice does nothing. It will not save you.

#### What the warranty actually says

Section 56 of the CPA provides an implied warranty of quality on all goods sold to a consumer by a supplier in the ordinary course of business. Even though the vehicle is second-hand, it must be reasonably suitable for the purposes for which it is generally intended, of good quality, in good working order, and free of any defects.

> **In plain words:** Even a used vehicle must work as a vehicle should, be in good condition, and have no hidden faults. That is the law, not a promise you choose to make.

If the vehicle fails to meet these standards within six months of delivery, section 56(2) gives the buyer the right to demand, at the buyer's choice, that you repair the vehicle, replace the vehicle, or refund the price paid.

> **In plain words:** If something goes wrong within six months, the buyer picks the fix. Not you.

The choice is the customer's, not yours. You cannot insist on repairing when they have asked for a refund.

If defects recur after a repair within three months, the supplier must replace or refund. You get one attempt at a repair.

#### The one defence that works

The implied warranty does not apply if you specifically informed the buyer of the defects and they expressly or tacitly agreed to accept the goods in that condition. A general "voetstoots" clause is not sufficient. Specific defects must have been disclosed. It also does not apply where the breakdown is due to ordinary wear and tear, having regard to the age and mileage of the vehicle. However, a sudden major mechanical failure typically goes beyond ordinary wear and tear.

> **In plain words:** Your one defence: tell the buyer about each specific fault before the sale, in writing, and have them agree to buy anyway. A general "sold as is" is not enough. Normal wear for the car's age and mileage is also not covered, but a sudden big failure usually is.

So your protection is disclosure, in writing, defect by defect.

Practical system for a small dealer:

Before you list any vehicle, have it inspected. Write down every fault you find. Photograph each one.

Put a defect schedule in your sale agreement. Not "sold as is". A numbered list: "1. Air conditioning does not cool. 2. Driver's door trim is loose. 3. Rear tyres at 3mm and will need replacing within 6 months. 4. Oil weep at the rocker cover gasket."

Have the buyer initial each line and sign the schedule.

Keep the signed schedule for five years alongside your SAPS register entry.

This single document is the difference between a disclosed defect and a section 56 refund claim.

#### Where disputes go

The Motor Industry Ombudsman of South Africa (MIOSA) provides independent dispute resolution for the motor industry and is free to consumers. Below that, the Small Claims Court needs no lawyer. Its limit is R30,000, determined in Government Gazette 55038, Government Notice 7717 of 20 July 2026, effective 1 August 2026. That replaced the R20,000 limit in place since 2019. Many websites still quote R20,000.

Note which way that runs. If you trade through a company, you cannot bring a claim in the Small Claims Court yourself: only a natural person may institute an action there. Your company can be taken there by a buyer, and a nominated director may appear for it, but to chase a customer who has not paid you will need the magistrates' court and an attorney.

Above that, the magistrates' courts. A district court has civil jurisdiction where the claim is R200,000 or less, and a regional court between R200,000 and R400,000. Larger claims go to the High Court.

A December 2025 Western Cape High Court ruling reinforced that buyers do not have to exhaust ombud or commission processes before approaching a court under the CPA, and that dealerships cannot hide behind voetstoots clauses or limited warranties when selling defective vehicles. The court upheld an order requiring a dealership to refund R175,000 against return of a defective vehicle.

> **In plain words:** A court confirmed in late 2025 that a buyer can go straight to court, and that a dealer's "as is" clause or limited warranty does not protect it. That dealer had to refund R175,000.

Build the cost of the occasional refund into your margin. A dealer who prices for zero returns is pricing wrong.

---

### VAT: register earlier than other businesses

The general advice in [Tax and SARS](#doc-tax-and-sars) says do not register for VAT voluntarily. For a vehicle dealer, that advice flips, and the reason is notional input tax.

#### How notional input tax works

Most of your stock comes from private individuals who are not VAT vendors. They charge you no VAT. Normally that would mean you pay VAT on the full selling price with nothing to claim back.

The VAT Act solves this.

When a non-vendor acquires goods, that non-vendor is the final consumer and bears the VAT. When those goods are later sold as second-hand goods to a vendor, there is a tax element included in the selling price which must be unlocked. The vendor accounts for VAT on the margin, which is the difference between the selling price and the cost of the goods. To achieve this, the vendor is allowed a notional input tax deduction on acquisition of the second-hand goods.

> **In plain words:** When a private person bought a car new, they paid VAT and could not claim it back. When you buy that car from them, the law lets you claim a piece of VAT back as if they had charged it, so you only pay VAT on your profit, not on the whole price.

The notional input tax is calculated by multiplying the tax fraction (15/115) by the actual purchase price or the open market value of the goods, whichever is the lesser.

SARS worked example:

X, a non-vendor, sells a second-hand vehicle to a motor dealer and registered VAT vendor for R115,000, which is also the open market value. If the motor dealer paid the full purchase price and meets all conditions, it may claim R15,000 as notional input tax (15/115 × R115,000). If the dealer sells it for R120,000, the dealer's mark-up of R20,000 is correctly taxed at 15%, which is R3,000.

> **In plain words:** Example: you buy a car from a private person for R115,000. You can claim R15,000 back as VAT. You sell it for R120,000 plus VAT. The VAT you actually pay to SARS is R3,000, which is 15% of your R20,000 profit.

Without this mechanism, VAT on the R120,000 sale would be around R15,650. With it, you pay R3,000. That difference is your entire margin on a small deal.

#### The conditions you must meet

You must be a VAT-registered vendor, and you must acquire the goods under a non-taxable transaction in the course or furtherance of an enterprise.

SARS requires that the amount must actually be paid, in full or in part, before you can claim. The payment must be traceable and verifiable. Cash deals with no bank trail put the claim at risk.

The dealer is required to be in possession of a completed VAT264 form declaring that the supply of the motor vehicle is not a taxable supply.

The VAT264 form must be completed by both the purchaser and the seller. Get it signed at the same moment you take the vehicle, not later.

SARS publishes a dedicated guide: VAT 420 Guide for Motor Dealers. Read it before you register for VAT. The link is in [Sources and verification register](#doc-sources-and-verification-register).

#### The decision

Below R2.3 million turnover you are not required to register. But a dealer turning over R900,000 a year buying mostly from private sellers is probably losing money by staying out of the VAT system, because notional input tax is worth more than the compliance cost.

Also understand how fast a dealer reaches the compulsory threshold. Turnover is gross sales, not margin. Sixteen vehicles sold at an average of R150,000 is R2.4 million. A dealer moving two cars a month crosses R2.3 million within the year, and you must register within 21 business days from the date you exceed R2.3 million, or from the date it becomes clear that you will. Track your rolling 12-month sales from month one.

Model it on your actual numbers, or pay a tax practitioner for one hour. This is the single highest-value hour a small dealer can buy.

#### Turnover tax is almost certainly wrong for a dealer

The core tax file describes turnover tax, with 0% on the first R600,000. It looks attractive. For a vehicle dealer it is usually a mistake, for two reasons.

It taxes turnover, not profit. A car bought for R140,000 and sold for R155,000 adds R155,000 to your turnover and R15,000 to your profit. At 1% on the band above R600,000, you would pay R1,550 in turnover tax on that deal against a R15,000 margin. Under normal tax, with SBC rates starting at 0%, a small dealer's actual profit is often taxed at nothing.

It removes you from the VAT system. Turnover tax replaces income tax, VAT, provisional tax, capital gains tax and dividends tax. That means no notional input tax, which is the single most valuable tax mechanism a used vehicle dealer has.

Stay on normal tax, check whether the company qualifies for SBC rates, and register for VAT when the numbers say so.

---

### Paperwork on every deal

#### When you buy a vehicle in

- Copy of the seller's ID, kept for five years
