- The original registration certificate (RC1)
- Confirm the seller's name on the RC1 matches the ID. If it does not, stop.
- Confirm the title holder. If the vehicle is financed, the title holder will be the financing institution. If the title holder is not the seller named in the paperwork, pause before paying, and ask which documents prove authority to sell. A settlement letter from the bank is required.
- A signed NCO (Notification of Change of Ownership) from the seller
- A signed VAT264, if you are VAT registered
- Your SAPS register entry, with VIN, chassis, engine number, odometer, colour, and distinguishing marks
- Photographs of the vehicle and of every defect

Possession of the keys is not the same as clear title. Never pay before the paperwork matches.

#### When you sell a vehicle out

- A valid roadworthy certificate. The Department of Transport vehicle registration page says used vehicle registration requires the certificate of roadworthiness if the current certificate is older than 60 days. Western Cape Government says a roadworthy test must be conducted every time a vehicle changes ownership.
- Your NCO, submitted so the vehicle stops being linked to you
- The buyer has 21 days to complete registration by submitting the RLV form with the RC1, roadworthy certificate, certified documentation, and the required fees.
- The signed defect schedule
- Your written sale agreement
- A tax invoice, or a normal invoice if you are not VAT registered (see `03-documents/01`)
- Copy of the buyer's ID for your SAPS register

> **In plain words:** When a vehicle changes hands, it needs a roadworthy certificate that is less than 60 days old.

Failing to meet the 21-day deadline carries penalties.

Do not hand over keys before the NCO is lodged. A vehicle still linked to you collects fines, e-tolls, and liability that are yours until the record changes.

---

### Two things about stock that surprise new dealers

#### Unsold vehicles at year end are taxed as trading stock

Income tax is calculated on profit, and profit includes the value of stock you still hold at financial year end. Section 22 of the Income Tax Act requires closing trading stock to be brought into account, generally at cost.

In plain terms: if you bought four vehicles in February and have not sold them by your 28 February year end, their cost is not simply an expense that reduces this year's profit. It is carried as stock and only becomes cost of sales when each vehicle is sold.

This catches dealers who buy heavily before year end expecting a tax deduction. Keep a stock list with cost price per vehicle, dated, and give it to your accountant at year end.

#### If a bank finances your stock, the bank holds title

Dealers who grow often use floor plan finance, where a lender funds the purchase of stock. On eNaTIS the lender then appears as the title holder and you as the owner. If the vehicle is financed, the title holder will be the financing institution.

You cannot transfer a financed vehicle to a buyer until the lender's interest on that vehicle is settled and the lender releases title. Build the settlement step into your sales process, or a sale will stall at the registering authority with an unhappy buyer waiting.

### Holding deposits

Customers will ask to "put down a deposit to hold the car".

Take it, but understand the Consumer Protection Act position. A consumer who makes a booking or reservation may cancel it, and you may charge a reasonable cancellation fee. What is reasonable takes into account the notice given and whether you could have sold the vehicle to someone else in the meantime.

A written deposit receipt stating the amount, the vehicle by VIN, the hold period, and the cancellation terms is what makes the fee enforceable. A WhatsApp saying "deposit non-refundable" is not.

### Other registrations that may apply

#### Municipal business licence and zoning

Vehicle sales premises almost always need zoning approval for motor trade. Residential zoning does not permit a car lot. Check with municipal town planning before you sign a lease, because you cannot fix this afterwards.

Many municipalities also require a trading licence under the Business Act 71 of 1991. Ask your Business Licensing Officer.

#### If you extend credit yourself

If you let a buyer pay you in instalments, you are a credit provider under the National Credit Act.

Any person who is a credit provider under a credit agreement to which the Act applies must register with the National Credit Regulator before extending credit. The number or size of the loans no longer matters.

The old monetary threshold is gone. There is no small-dealer exemption. Registering as a credit provider is a serious undertaking with ongoing reporting duties.

The simple answer for a small dealer: do not offer your own instalment terms. Refer the buyer to a bank or a registered finance house. Take full payment before delivery.

#### If you sell warranties, insurance, or arrange finance

That is financial services. It requires an FSP licence under FAIS, or you must operate as a representative of a licensed FSP. Selling a service plan or a warranty product off your own bat, unlicensed, is an offence.

Partner with a licensed provider instead. They will appoint you correctly.

#### Imported vehicles

Imported vehicles bring a separate stack of requirements: SARS customs clearance and proof of duty paid, a SAPS clearance, a letter of authority from the SABS, and correct eNaTIS entry.

If a motor vehicle is registered outside the Republic, you need the registration and licensing documents issued in the country where the vehicle is registered, a South African Police Service clearance of the motor vehicle, and a letter of authority issued by the South African Bureau of Standards. The Customs and Excise Division of SARS provides the required proof of compliance with their regulations.

> **In plain words:** For an imported vehicle you need its foreign registration papers, a police clearance, a letter of authority from the SABS, and proof from SARS Customs that the duty was paid.

Do not import as your first move. Learn the local trade first.

#### Voluntary industry membership

The Retail Motor Industry Organisation (RMI) and its used car division are not compulsory. Membership signals credibility to buyers and gives you access to dispute support and industry guidance. Worth considering once you are established, not on day one.

---

### Dealing from home, with no yard

Many small dealers start this way: stock is advertised online, the conversation happens on WhatsApp, and viewings and test drives happen at an agreed public place. It is a workable model. Three things are different from a dealer with a yard.

**Zoning is the sticking point.** A home enterprise normally allows a quiet business run by you from a room. The usual conditions include no storage of goods, materials or vehicles on the premises that creates a commercial appearance. Three bakkies for sale on the verge will breach that in most municipalities and will bring a complaint from a neighbour. Options: keep only one or two vehicles at home and rotate; rent a bay at a secure storage yard; or apply for consent use with the municipality. Ask town planning before you buy your first stock, not after the complaint.

**Your home becomes the registered premises.** Your SAPS second-hand goods registration, your BRNC, and your company address all sit at your home. A police official may inspect the premises and your register there. That is normal. Keep the register and certificate at hand.

**Every viewing is a meeting with a stranger.** Read [Working from home and meeting safely](#doc-working-from-home-and-meeting-safely) in full. It covers where to meet, what to get from the buyer before the meeting, how to run a test drive without losing the vehicle, why a proof-of-payment screenshot is never enough, how to check cash, and the common scams aimed at sellers. For a dealer, the amounts involved make those habits mandatory, not optional.

The short version for a dealer:

- Licence copy and ID copy received on WhatsApp before you agree to meet
- Meet in daylight at a police station, bank, or busy petrol station car park
- Someone with you; keys in your hand until you are in the passenger seat
- Full payment reflected in your balance before keys, papers or a signed NCO change hands
- Large amounts: immediate payment while you watch, or at a bank counter

### Growing beyond vehicles

Most dealers add lines: tyres, servicing, parts, detailing, sometimes an unrelated business under the same company.

Before you do, read [Adding new lines of business](#doc-adding-new-lines-of-business). It covers trading names, and which documents must still carry the registered company name. It covers what licences each new line needs. And it explains the tax trap that catches people who form a second company: owning shares in more than one company can cost you the lower Small Business Corporation tax rates on both.

The short advice for a dealer: keep one company, use trading names, and only split into a second company for a line whose liability risk is large enough to justify losing SBC rates.

### Insurance

This is where small dealers are most often underinsured.

You need cover that a normal motor policy does not give you:

- Stock cover for vehicles you own but have not sold, parked on your premises
- Cover for test drives, including damage caused by a prospective buyer
- Cover for vehicles driven under motor trade numbers
- Public liability for people on your premises
- Theft cover appropriate to how secure the premises actually are

Ask a broker specifically for motor trade insurance. A private vehicle policy will not respond to a claim involving stock.

---

### Your startup checklist

Before your first purchase:

1. Decide sole proprietor or company (`01-core/02`)
2. Get BRNC or Traffic Register Number (`01-core/05`)
3. Apply for SAPS second-hand goods dealer registration, form SAPS 601, free
4. Check zoning for your premises, before signing any lease
5. Apply for a municipal business licence if required
6. Get motor trade insurance quotes
7. Work out whether VAT registration pays, given notional input tax, and confirm you are NOT on turnover tax
8. Build your sale agreement and defect schedule template
9. Set up your SAPS acquisition and disposal register
10. Decide your inspection process, and who does it

Before your first sale:

11. Roadworthy certificate in hand
12. Defect schedule signed and initialled
13. Written sale agreement signed
14. NCO lodged the same day
15. Buyer's ID copied into your register

Ongoing:

16. SAPS register updated on the day of every transaction
17. VAT264 for every purchase from a non-vendor
18. SAPS registration renewal diarised
19. Five years of records kept for SAPS and SARS
20. Rolling 12-month sales tracked against the R2.3 million VAT threshold
21. Stock list with cost per vehicle, ready for year end
22. If working from home: zoning checked for storing stock, and the meeting-safety habits in `01-core/09` in use on every viewing

---

<a id="doc-food-business"></a>

## Business type: food and drinks

Read `01-core/` first. This file adds the rules that apply only to food.

This covers anyone who handles, prepares, stores, transports or sells food for money: a home baker, a caterer, a food truck, a spaza shop, a takeaway, a market stall, a small manufacturer.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| COA | Certificate of Acceptability. The permit you cannot open without. |
| R638 | The 2018 food hygiene rules. |
| R146 | The 2012 food labelling rules. |
| EHP | The municipal health inspector who checks your premises. |
| Person in charge | Whoever runs the kitchen day to day. Must have accredited training. |
| Zoning | Whether the municipality allows your kind of business at that address. |

---

Food is the most regulated small business in South Africa after vehicles. Budget two to six months before you may legally open.

### The one document you cannot trade without

A Certificate of Acceptability, usually called a COA.

Regulation R638 of 2018 sets general hygiene requirements for food premises and requires all processed food handling businesses that sell food for profit to hold a Certificate of Acceptability.

It applies to a restaurant in Sandton, a spaza shop in Khayelitsha, a catering company in Durban, and a game lodge kitchen in Limpopo equally. It is sometimes called the R638 certificate, an environmental health permit, or a food business licence.

> **In plain words:** Every food business needs this certificate, big or small. People also call it the R638 certificate, a health permit, or a food licence. Same thing.

R638 governs the structure and cleanliness of premises, how food is transported, the duties of the person in charge, the training of food handlers, and the issuing of the COA by your local municipality. If you handle, store, transport or sell food to the public, R638 applies to you.

> **In plain words:** R638 is the set of rules for how a food place is built and kept clean, how food is moved, what the person in charge must do, what training staff need, and how the certificate is issued. If you sell food, it applies to you.

#### Why applications fail

Training under R638 Regulation 10 is not optional. You cannot get a COA without accredited food safety training for the person in charge and every food handler.

The single most common reason COA applications fail is the person in charge not holding an accredited training certificate when the Environmental Health Practitioner arrives.

Do the training before you book the inspection. Not after.

#### How to get it

Contact the Environmental Health Department of the municipality where your premises are located and request the COA application form. An Environmental Health Practitioner will inspect the premises to verify compliance with R638. If compliant, the COA is issued. If not, the EHP provides a list of required improvements.

> **In plain words:** Ask your municipality's health department for the food certificate form. A health inspector will visit. If your kitchen passes, you get the certificate. If not, they give you a list of things to fix.

Once issued, the COA must be displayed prominently on your premises. Periodic inspections will follow.

The COA is specific to the premises and cannot be transferred to new premises or a new owner. It has no fixed expiry under R638, but becomes void when key conditions change.

If you move, you apply again. If you buy an existing café, the previous owner's COA does not come with it.

### Everything else you may need

Nine documents cover most food businesses: CIPC registration, SARS tax, a Business Licence under the Businesses Act 71 of 1991, a Certificate of Acceptability under R638, a Zoning Certificate if the premises are not zoned for business, a Fire Compliance Certificate (SANS 10400-T), a Gas Certificate of Compliance if using gas, a Liquor Licence if selling alcohol, and accredited food safety training certificates for the person in charge and all food handlers.

> **In plain words:** Most food businesses need: company or tax registration, a municipal business licence, the food certificate (COA), zoning approval, a fire certificate, a gas certificate if you use gas, a liquor licence if you sell alcohol, and food safety training certificates for everyone who handles food.

You may not begin trading in a business that requires a licence before the licence has been issued. Trading without a valid licence is punishable with a fine. Licensing is governed by the Business Act 71 of 1991.

> **In plain words:** You cannot open until the licence is in your hand. Opening early can get you fined.

If you comply with all requirements a licence is issued. If not, you can apply for a 14-day grace period during which you make the changes needed.

### Realistic timing

Expect two to six months depending on municipality responsiveness and premises readiness. CIPC registration takes days, training one to three weeks, premises preparation two to eight weeks, and the COA application two to eight weeks. A liquor licence alone can add three to twelve months.

> **In plain words:** Realistic timing: a few days for company registration, one to three weeks for training, two to eight weeks to get the kitchen ready, two to eight weeks for the certificate. Alcohol adds three months to a year.

Plan your lease around this. Paying rent on premises you may not legally trade from is how food startups run out of money before they open.

### Working from home

Home food businesses are legal but scrutinised closely during inspection. You need a COA, zoning approval, an R638-compliant kitchen area separated from family living space, R638 training, a business licence, and possibly neighbour consent.

A shared family kitchen will usually not pass. Budget for a separate prep area.

### Labelling

Regulation R146 of 2012 governs the labelling and advertising of foodstuffs, including allergen warnings.

Read R146 before you print anything. Reprinting labels because the allergen declaration is wrong is a common and avoidable cost.

Do not make health claims. "Boosts immunity" or "helps with diabetes" can push your product into being regulated as a medicine.

### The wider law

Food businesses are governed by the Foodstuffs, Cosmetics and Disinfectants Act 54 of 1972, the Consumer Protection Act 68 of 2008, the Business Act 71 of 1991, the Agricultural Products Standards Act 119 of 1990, the Occupational Health and Safety Act 85 of 1993, and the Standards Act 8 of 2008.

> **In plain words:** Several laws apply to food. The important ones for a small business are the food hygiene rules (R638), the labelling rules (R146), consumer law, and the municipal licence.

One exception worth checking: to legally sell any foodstuff in South Africa, except unprocessed agricultural products, you must apply for a Certificate of Acceptability from the local authority. If you only sell raw produce as it comes out of the ground, confirm your position with your municipality rather than assuming.

### Branding and marketing notes

Use [Branding prompts](#doc-branding-prompts), with two additions.

When you run the naming prompt, add this line:

```
My business sells food. Check that no name implies a health benefit,
a medical claim, or a protected term like "organic" or "free range"
that I would have to prove.
```

For photography, daylight and a plain background beat any filter. Photograph the food the moment it is plated.

### Your checklist

1. Confirm zoning for food preparation at your premises
2. Book accredited R638 training for yourself and anyone who will handle food
3. Prepare the premises to R638 standards
4. Apply for the COA through your municipal Environmental Health Department
5. Apply for the municipal business licence
6. Get fire compliance and a gas CoC if relevant
7. Check R146 for your labels before printing
8. Display the COA on the wall
9. Only then, open

---

<a id="doc-beauty-and-personal-care"></a>

## Business type: beauty and personal care

Read `01-core/` first. This file adds the rules that apply only to businesses that work on people's bodies.

This covers hairdressing, barbering, nails, beauty therapy, lashes, massage, tattooing and piercing.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Health establishment | The legal category for hair, beauty, massage and tattoo businesses. |
| EHP | The municipal health inspector. |
| Zoning | Whether your address is allowed to be used for business. |
| Treatment liability insurance | Cover for when a treatment harms a client. |
| Patch test | A small skin test before a chemical treatment. Record it. |

---

### You need a municipal licence

These are health establishments under the Business Act.

The categories that typically require a municipal trading licence under the Business Act include health establishments: businesses providing health, beauty, or body treatment services, including hairdressers, barbers, beauty salons, nail salons, tattoo studios, and similar premises.

No person may carry on certain service-related businesses unless they hold a licence issued by the council, local authority or municipality where the business premises are situated.

You may not begin trading in a business that requires a licence before the licence has been issued. Trading without a valid licence is punishable with a fine.

Apply through your municipal Business Licensing office. An Environmental Health Practitioner will inspect the premises.

What the inspector usually looks at: hand washing facilities, ventilation, surface cleanability, waste disposal, storage of chemicals, and separation from living space if you work from home.

### Tattoo and piercing studios

These face the strictest inspection in this category, because they break the skin.

Expect questions about sterilisation equipment, single-use needles, sharps containers, waste removal contracts, and record keeping of clients and batch numbers.

Talk to your Environmental Health Practitioner before you sign a lease. The premises requirements can make a cheap space unusable.

### Working from home

This is common and usually legal. But you need zoning approval, and usually a separate treatment room with its own basin.

Ask town planning about your zoning before you spend money on a room. Some residential zonings allow a home occupation with limits on client numbers, parking and signage.

### If you make or sell products

Selling retail products someone else manufactures is ordinary retail. See [Retail and online shop](#doc-retail-and-online-shop).

Making your own oils, creams, scrubs or hair products changes things. The Foodstuffs, Cosmetics and Disinfectants Act 54 of 1972 regulates cosmetics, including labelling and ingredient claims.

Do not claim a product treats a medical condition. "Clears eczema" or "cures acne" can reclassify your product as a medicine, which brings SAHPRA into your life. "Soothes dry skin" is a cosmetic claim. "Treats dermatitis" is not.

### Insurance you actually need

Professional indemnity or treatment liability cover. One burn from a relaxer, one infected piercing, or one bad reaction to a product can cost you more than a year of profit.

Ask a broker for cover appropriate to the treatments you actually perform, and tell them everything you do. Cover bought for "hairdressing" will not respond to a lash lift claim.

### Consumer law applies to services too

A service must be performed with reasonable skill and care. If a treatment goes wrong, the customer has a right to have it put right or refunded.

Protect yourself with a consultation record: what the client asked for, what you advised, any allergy or patch test, and what was agreed. Have them sign it. For colour, chemical and skin treatments, a patch test record is the difference between a defensible position and a settlement.

### Bookings, deposits and no-shows

No-shows are the biggest hidden cost in this industry.

A deposit is legal and enforceable if the terms are clear and reasonable before booking. Under the Consumer Protection Act a customer may cancel a booking. You may charge a cancellation fee, but it must be reasonable. Reasonable depends on how much notice they gave, and whether you could fill the slot with someone else.

Put your cancellation terms in writing, send them with the booking confirmation, and apply them consistently. Terms you only mention when someone misses an appointment are hard to enforce.

### Branding and marketing notes

Use `02-branding-and-marketing/`. Two things matter more in this industry than in most:

Before-and-after photos are your most effective marketing, but you need permission. Ask every client, in writing, before posting their face. This is a POPIA requirement as well as basic courtesy. Keep the permission on file.

Google Business Profile reviews drive local bookings more than any social post. Ask every happy client. See [Marketing prompts](#doc-marketing-prompts).

### Your checklist

1. Confirm zoning for your premises, including home premises
2. Apply for the municipal health establishment licence
3. Prepare the premises for the health inspection
4. Get treatment liability insurance appropriate to your services
5. Build a consultation and patch test record template
6. Write your booking, deposit and cancellation terms
7. Get written photo permission from clients before posting
8. Register your POPIA information officer, since you hold client health information

---

<a id="doc-retail-and-online-shop"></a>

## Business type: retail and online selling

Read `01-core/` first. This file adds the rules that apply when you sell physical products.

This covers a shop, a market stall, an online store, an Instagram shop, and selling on Takealot or Facebook Marketplace.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| ECTA | The law for selling online. |
| Cooling-off | The customer's seven-day right to return an online purchase for a refund. |
| Implied warranty | The six-month guarantee the law adds to every sale. |
| Importer's code | A free SARS number you need to bring goods into the country. |
| NRCS / LOA | The regulator, and the Letter of Authority, for certain products like electrical goods. Without the letter, the goods may not be sold. |
| Second-Hand Goods Act | The law requiring anyone who resells used goods to register with the police. |

---

### Do you need a licence

Usually not, if you sell ordinary goods like clothing, homeware, stationery, gifts, or accessories.

You do need one if you sell food. See [Food business](#doc-food-business).

Food retail, meaning any business that sells, prepares, or handles food for sale to the public, including spaza shops, requires a municipal trading licence under the Business Act.

A general trader's licence is still required by some municipalities for a fixed shop. Ask your municipal Business Licensing office. Market stalls usually need a trading permit from the market or the municipality rather than a business licence.

### If you sell second-hand goods

This catches more people than expected. Buying used items to resell makes you a dealer.

The Second-Hand Goods Act 6 of 2009 mandates that dealers register with SAPS, keep detailed registers of transactions for five years, verify supplier identities, and report suspected stolen items. The Act applies to businesses dealing in used items worth over R100.

> **In plain words:** If you buy used goods to resell, you must register with the police, keep records of every item for five years, check who you buy from, and report anything that looks stolen. Registration is free.

The principal business activities that must register include an Auctioneer, General Dealer, Jeweller, Motor Vehicle Dealer and Scrap metal Dealer.

If you flip used phones, laptops, furniture, jewellery, or vintage clothing, this applies to you. Registration is free through the SAPS 601 form at your local station.

### Selling online

Two laws apply on top of everything else: the Consumer Protection Act 68 of 2008 and the Electronic Communications and Transactions Act 25 of 2002.

#### What must appear on your site

Section 43 of ECTA requires suppliers to disclose the full price of goods or services including additional costs, and to provide information on their legal status and physical address.

Put all of this on your site:

- Your full legal name, and company registration number if you have one
- A physical address, not only a PO box
- Email address and phone number
- Full price including delivery and every other cost
- Returns and refund policy
- Privacy notice (template in `03-documents/01`)

#### The order review step

Your checkout must include a step where the customer can review the entire order, including products, quantities, pricing, delivery costs, and terms, and correct any errors before finalising. This satisfies section 43(2). Failing to provide it gives the consumer a 14-day cancellation right under section 43(3).

> **In plain words:** Before the customer pays, your website must show them a summary of everything they are buying, with all costs, and let them fix mistakes. If you skip this step, the customer gets 14 days to cancel.

A one-click buy button with no review screen costs you a week of extra cancellation exposure on every order.

#### The seven-day cooling-off right

Section 44 lets a consumer cancel an electronic transaction without reason and without penalty within seven days after receiving the goods, or after the agreement for services was concluded. It applies where the consumer is a natural person, regardless of the transaction value, subject to the exclusions in section 42(2).

> **In plain words:** Anyone who buys from your website can send it back within seven days for a full refund, without giving a reason.

The seven-day window is statutory and cannot be contracted out of. A "no refunds" sign on an online store is unenforceable against a cooling-off claim.

The only charge you may pass to the consumer for the cancellation is the direct cost of returning the goods.

For services the cooling-off right lapses as soon as the services are used, and certain transactions are excluded, including some financial services and auctions.

Do not copy a shop-front returns policy onto a website. They are different legal situations.

#### Delivery timing

Under section 46 you must execute the order within 30 days of receiving it unless you agreed otherwise. If you cannot perform because the goods are unavailable, you must notify the customer immediately and refund any payment within 30 days of that notification.

> **In plain words:** Deliver within 30 days unless you agreed something else. If you cannot deliver at all, say so immediately and refund within 30 days.

If your product page promises delivery in three days, that promise becomes your agreement. Promise what you can actually do.

### The six-month warranty

Consumers are entitled to goods that are reasonably suitable for their ordinary purpose, of good quality and in good working order, free from defects, reasonably durable, and compliant with applicable standards.

Section 56 gives a six-month implied warranty. If goods fail within six months and it is not the customer's fault, they can generally choose repair, replacement, or refund.

Price this in. A retailer who assumes zero returns is pricing wrong.

### Importing stock

You need an importer's code from SARS Customs. It is free and separate from your income tax registration.

You pay import duty plus 15% VAT on the landed value at the border, even if you are not VAT registered. That VAT is a real cost you cannot recover unless you are a vendor. Build it into your landed cost before you order, not after.

Some goods need a Letter of Authority from the National Regulator for Compulsory Specifications (NRCS) before they may be sold in South Africa. This covers many electrical products, lighting, plugs, chargers, and some automotive parts. Importers get caught by this constantly and the stock cannot legally be sold.

Check the NRCS requirement before you pay a supplier deposit.

### Payments

Compare payment gateway fees carefully. For a small store, the percentage per transaction matters more than the monthly fee.

Ask about: percentage per transaction, fixed fee per transaction, monthly fee, settlement time, chargeback handling, and whether they support instant EFT, which many South African customers prefer over cards.

If you take cash, check your bank's cash deposit fees. They are often the largest single bank cost for a small retailer.

### Stock records

Once you carry stock, the spreadsheet in `03-documents/01` needs one more sheet: stock on hand.

Track cost price, selling price, quantity, and date received. You need cost of goods sold for your tax return, and you need to know which items are actually making money.

### Branding and marketing notes

Use `02-branding-and-marketing/`. For products, add this to the naming prompt:

```
I sell physical products. Check the name is not already a registered
trade mark in South Africa for similar goods, and tell me how to check
this myself on the CIPC trade marks database.
```

Product photography on a plain background, shot in daylight, converts better than styled photos for most small sellers. Keep image files under 500 KB, because many customers pay for data.

### Your checklist

1. Check whether your municipality requires a trader's licence
2. If you sell used goods, register with SAPS under the Second-Hand Goods Act
3. If you import, get a SARS importer's code and check NRCS requirements
4. Put ECTA section 43 information on your site
5. Add an order review step before checkout
6. Write a returns policy that respects the seven-day cooling-off right
7. Publish your privacy notice
8. Set up stock tracking alongside your cash book

---

<a id="doc-services-and-trades"></a>

## Business type: services and trades

Read `01-core/` first. This file adds the rules that apply when you fix, build, install, clean, or deliver.

This covers plumbers, electricians, builders, painters, mechanics, gardeners, cleaners, handymen, couriers, and installers.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| CoC | Certificate of Compliance. The document an electrician or gas installer signs. Only registered people may sign one. |
| Public liability | Insurance for damage you cause to other people or their property. |
| Scope | Exactly what work is included in the price, and what is not. |
| Variation | Extra work outside the original scope. Agree it in writing before doing it. |
| Progress payment | Being paid in stages as the job goes along. |

---

### Check whether your trade is regulated

Most trades need no licence. Some trades may not legally be done without registration. If you do the work anyway, your customer's insurance may refuse to pay, and you carry the risk.

Trades that commonly require registration or certification:

Electricians who issue a Certificate of Compliance must be registered with the Department of Employment and Labour as a registered person. An electrical CoC signed by someone not registered is worthless, and the customer finds out when their house sale falls through.

Gas installers must be registered and authorised to issue a gas Certificate of Conformity.

Plumbers issuing plumbing certificates need the relevant registration, and some municipalities maintain their own lists.

Builders doing residential work for sale need NHBRC registration. Construction contractors bidding for public work need CIDB registration and grading.

Security services require PSIRA registration. This is strictly enforced.

Pest control operators handling scheduled chemicals need registration under the relevant agricultural and health legislation.

If your trade has a professional or statutory body, assume registration is compulsory until you confirm otherwise. Search the body's site, or phone them. One call is cheaper than a disputed job.

### Occupational health and safety

The Occupational Health and Safety Act applies to you even with no employees. You work on other people's property and around other people, so their safety is your responsibility.

For construction work specifically, the Construction Regulations bring extra duties, and a client may require a safety file before you may start on site. Corporate and government clients will always ask. Build a basic safety file once and reuse it.

### Consumer law on services

A service must be performed with reasonable skill, care and diligence, and within a reasonable time. If it is not, the customer can require it to be put right or refunded.

Goods you install carry the six-month implied warranty separately from your workmanship.

### Quoting: where trades lose money

Most disputes in trade work are not about quality. They are about scope.

Use the quote template in [Which template to use when](#doc-which-template-to-use-when) and fill in both lists. What is included, and what is not included. The second list is the one that saves you.

Write down:

- Exactly what work is covered
- What is excluded, in plain words
- Who supplies materials
- Who removes rubble and waste
- What happens if you open a wall and find something unexpected
- What a variation costs, and that it must be agreed in writing before you do it
- Whether the price includes travel, and how far

"Additional work will be quoted separately and must be approved in writing before it starts" is one sentence that prevents most arguments.

### Deposits and payment

Take a deposit before you buy materials. A signed quote plus a deposit is a contract.

For longer jobs, use progress payments tied to stages, not to dates. "50% on completion of first fix" is enforceable and clear. "50% after two weeks" invites a fight about whether you were on site.

Send the invoice the day you finish. Trades that invoice weekly get paid weekly. Trades that invoice monthly get paid whenever.

### Insurance

Public liability cover. If you flood a house, burn a roof, or drop a tool on a car, this is what stands between you and personal bankruptcy. It is not expensive for a one-person trade.

Tell your vehicle insurer the vehicle is used for business, and ask about goods in transit cover if you carry customer property or expensive tools. See [Vehicles for your business](#doc-vehicles-for-your-business).

Tool insurance is worth it once your tools are worth more than a few months of profit.

### Vehicles

Most trades need one. Read [Vehicles for your business](#doc-vehicles-for-your-business) before deciding whether to put it in a business name. For a single owner-operator, personal ownership plus a logbook is usually the better answer.

Extra rules apply if your vehicle weighs over 3,500 kg fully loaded, or if you carry passengers for money. You need a Professional Driving Permit and a roadworthy test every year. For passenger transport you also need an operating licence.

Vehicle signage is usually the cheapest advertising a local trade can buy, and it is a deductible expense.

### Branding and marketing notes

Use `02-branding-and-marketing/`. For a local trade, priority order is:

1. Google Business Profile, filled in completely, with photos of real work
2. Reviews from every happy customer
3. WhatsApp Business with quick replies and a catalogue of common jobs with prices
4. Local Facebook community groups
5. Vehicle signage

Social media is fifth, not first. A plumber with 40 Google reviews beats a plumber with 4,000 Instagram followers.

Photograph every completed job before you leave. Two photos, before and after, taken in daylight. That is your entire content strategy.

### Your checklist

1. Confirm whether your trade requires professional or statutory registration
2. Get public liability insurance
3. Tell your vehicle insurer you use the vehicle for work
4. Build your quote template with a proper exclusions list
5. Set your deposit and progress payment terms in writing
6. Set up a vehicle logbook
7. Claim and complete your Google Business Profile
8. Set up WhatsApp Business with quick replies

---

<a id="doc-professional-and-creative-work"></a>

## Business type: professional and creative work

Read `01-core/` first. This file adds the rules that apply when you sell your skill or your time.

This covers consultants, designers, developers, copywriters, photographers, videographers, tutors, bookkeepers, virtual assistants, translators, and marketers.

### Words used in this file

Plain meanings for the terms used in this file. The full list is in [Glossary](#doc-glossary).

| Word | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Turnover tax | A simple tax on sales. Many professionals are not allowed to use it. |
| Home office deduction | Claiming a share of your home costs because you work from a dedicated room. |
| IP | Intellectual property. Who owns the work you create. |
| Personal service provider | SARS's term for someone who looks like an employee of one client. Loses most deductions. |
| Revision rounds | How many times a client may ask for changes before paying extra. |

---

This is the lightest-regulation business type in South Africa. You can usually invoice tomorrow.

### What you need

Almost certainly nothing beyond the core files. No municipal licence, no health inspection, no product regulation.

Two exceptions.

Some professions are statutorily regulated and may not be practised without registration: accountants signing off certain work, auditors, financial advisors under FAIS, estate agents, engineers signing designs, architects, attorneys, and health professionals. If you hold a professional title, check your body's rules on practising independently.

If you give financial advice or sell financial products, you need an FSP licence under FAIS or you must be a representative of a licensed FSP. Calling it "coaching" does not change the legal position.

### The turnover tax trap

This is the most important tax point for this group, and it is easy to miss.

Turnover tax looks attractive: 0% on the first R600,000 of turnover. But most of this group cannot use it.

Certain businesses are excluded, such as professional service providers, labour brokers, personal service providers, and businesses carrying on other excluded activities.

A business is excluded where more than 20% of income comes from professional services.

The exclusion catches accountants, lawyers, engineers, architects, consultants and similar. Whether a designer, developer, or copywriter falls inside the exclusion is not always obvious.

Check the SARS list for your exact trade before you elect turnover tax. Electing it wrongly and having SARS reverse it later is expensive.

If you cannot use turnover tax, your levers are the ones in [Tax and SARS](#doc-tax-and-sars): claim every real expense, stay below the threshold while you are small, and consider the home office deduction if you qualify.

### The home office deduction

This group qualifies more often than others, because the work genuinely happens at home.

SARS generally expects a specific part of the home used regularly and exclusively for the trade, and properly equipped for it. A dedicated room qualifies. The kitchen table does not.

If you qualify, you can claim a share of rent or bond interest, rates, and electricity based on floor area. Measure the room, measure the house, keep a photo and the calculation on file.

### Contracts matter more here than anywhere else

Your risk is not a faulty product. It is scope creep and non-payment.

Use the service agreement prompt in [Which template to use when](#doc-which-template-to-use-when). Make sure it covers:

What is included, in specific deliverables, not vague descriptions.

How many rounds of revision are included, and what an extra round costs. "Two rounds of revisions included" is the single most valuable line in a creative contract.

Who owns the work when it is finished, and when ownership transfers. Standard practice: ownership transfers on full payment, not on delivery. This is your leverage when an invoice goes unpaid.

What happens if the client cancels midway, and what you are owed for work already done.

Payment terms, and what happens when they are missed.

### Intellectual property

By default in South African law, the person who creates a work generally owns the copyright, unless it was made by an employee in the course of employment or the contract says otherwise.

That means your client does not automatically own what you made for them, unless your contract transfers it. Most clients assume they do.

Deal with it explicitly. Either transfer ownership on full payment, or licence the work for a defined use. Say which, in writing, before you start.

If you use AI tools to produce client work, say so in your contract, and understand that the copyright position for AI-generated material is unsettled. Do not promise a client exclusive ownership of something you cannot guarantee.

### Getting paid

Freelancers in South Africa lose more money to late payment than to low rates.

Deposit up front. 50% is normal for project work. No deposit, no start.

Invoice on a fixed day, every month, with clear due dates. See `03-documents/01`.

State your late payment terms in the contract, and apply them. Interest on overdue amounts is enforceable if it was agreed in advance.

Withhold final files until final payment. This only works if your contract says ownership transfers on payment, which is why that clause matters.

### Tax point most freelancers miss

If you work mainly for one client, who controls your hours and how you work, SARS may treat you as a personal service provider or as an employee. That changes your tax treatment significantly and removes most of your deductions.

Signs of risk: one client providing most of your income, working set hours at their premises, using their equipment, having a manager.

If that describes you, get advice. It is fixable, but not after an assessment.

### Branding and marketing notes

Use `02-branding-and-marketing/`. For this group, the priority order is different from a local trade:

1. Portfolio of actual work, even two or three pieces
2. A clear description of what you do and who you do it for
3. LinkedIn, if you sell to businesses
4. Referrals from past clients, asked for directly
5. A simple website

The naming prompt in [Branding prompts](#doc-branding-prompts) works, but consider trading under your own name. For a solo consultant or creative, "Naledi Mokoena Design" often builds more trust than an invented agency name, and it never becomes a lie when someone asks how big the team is.

### Your checklist

1. Confirm whether your profession requires statutory registration
2. Check whether you are excluded from turnover tax before electing it
3. Build a service agreement covering scope, revisions, IP and payment
4. Decide your deposit and revision policy, and stick to it
5. Set up the home office calculation if you qualify
6. Register as a provisional taxpayer and diarise the IRP6 dates
7. Assess whether you look like an employee of one client, and fix it if so

---

<a id="doc-glossary"></a>

## Glossary

South African business is full of abbreviations. Here is what they mean, in plain words.

### Registration and companies

**BizPortal** — The government website where you register a company yourself. www.bizportal.gov.za

**CIPC** — Companies and Intellectual Property Commission. The government body that keeps the register of companies and trade marks.

**CoR 14.3** — Your company registration certificate. Proof the company exists.

**CSD** — Central Supplier Database. The list you must be on to sell to government.

**MOI** — Memorandum of Incorporation. The rulebook of your company. CIPC gives you a standard one free.

**Pty Ltd** — Private company. A separate legal person from you.

**Sole proprietor** — You, trading as yourself. Not a separate legal person. No registration needed.

**Beneficial ownership filing** — A free CIPC filing naming the real humans behind a company. Compulsory.

**Annual return** — A yearly filing with CIPC to confirm the company still exists. Not a tax return. Both are required.

**Customer code** — A six-character CIPC account ID you need before you can file anything with CIPC.

**PIS** — Public Interest Score. A points calculation done each financial year end that decides whether your company needs an audit, an independent review, or neither.

**Owner-managed company** — A company where every shareholder is also a director. It gets the lightest financial reporting obligations.

**AFS** — Annual Financial Statements.

**FAS** — Financial Accountability Supplement. A short financial summary filed with the CIPC annual return instead of full statements, where that is allowed.

**Audit** — Full checking of the financial statements with a positive opinion. Expensive. Rarely required for a one-person company.

**Independent review** — A lighter check giving limited assurance. Cheaper than an audit.

**Compilation** — An accountant prepares the statements from your records with no opinion on them. Usual route for a small owner-managed company.

**CoR 40.5** — The CIPC form used to apply to reinstate a deregistered company.

**Bona vacantia** — Property with no owner, which can pass to the state. What can happen to a deregistered company's assets.

**Solvency and liquidity test** — The section 4 check a board must pass before paying a dividend: assets exceed liabilities, and the company can pay its debts for the next 12 months.

**Resolution** — A written record of a decision by the directors or shareholders. Needed even when you are the only one.

**Registered name** — The company's legal name on its CoR 14.3. Must appear with the registration number on all company documents.

**Trading name** — A name you market under that is different from the registered name. Allowed, but the registered name and number must still appear on documents, shown as "trading as".

**Trading stock** — Goods you hold to sell. Unsold stock at financial year end is brought into account at cost under section 22 of the Income Tax Act, so buying stock does not by itself reduce that year's profit.

**Floor plan finance** — A lender funds a dealer's stock. The lender becomes title holder on eNaTIS until each vehicle is settled.

### Tax

**SARS** — South African Revenue Service. The tax authority.

**eFiling** — SARS's online system. www.sarsefiling.co.za

**ITR12** — The annual income tax return for individuals, including sole proprietors.

**ITR14** — The annual income tax return for companies.

**IRP6** — The provisional tax return. Submitted twice a year.

**Provisional taxpayer** — Someone who earns income with no PAYE deducted, so they pay tax in advance during the year. Every business owner is one.

**PAYE** — Pay As You Earn. Tax deducted from a salary by an employer.

**EMP201** — The monthly payroll return declaring PAYE, UIF and SDL. Due by the 7th of the following month.

**EMP501** — The twice-yearly reconciliation of your EMP201 submissions.

**SDL** — Skills Development Levy. A payroll levy that only applies once your payroll passes a threshold.

**Dividends tax** — 20% tax on dividends, withheld and paid over by the company.

**Deemed remuneration** — An old PAYE rule for private company directors, repealed with effect from 1 March 2017. Many websites still describe it as current. Directors' PAYE is now based on actual remuneration.

**Loan account** — The record of money moving between you and your company outside salary and dividends. Credit means the company owes you. Debit means you owe the company.

**Deemed dividend** — Where a company lends money to a shareholder interest-free or below the SARS official rate, the shortfall is treated as a dividend and taxed at 20%.

**Official rate of interest** — The rate SARS publishes for judging whether a loan is low-interest. 7.75% per annum from 1 December 2025.

**Tax threshold** — The income level below which you owe no income tax. R99,000 for people under 65 in 2026/2027.

**Turnover tax** — A simplified tax for micro businesses, charged on sales rather than profit. 0% on the first R600,000.

**Turnover** — Total sales, before any costs. Not profit.

**Taxable income** — Profit after allowable expenses. This is what income tax is calculated on.

**SBC** — Small Business Corporation. A company that qualifies for reduced tax rates starting at 0%.

**VAT** — Value-Added Tax, 15%. Only charged by registered vendors.

**Vendor** — A business registered for VAT.

**Input tax** — VAT you paid on purchases and can claim back. Only if you are a vendor.

**Output tax** — VAT you charged customers and must pay to SARS.

**Notional input tax** — A VAT claim allowed when you buy second-hand goods from someone who is not a vendor. Important for vehicle dealers.

**VAT264** — The form both buyer and seller sign so a notional input tax claim is valid.

**Tax invoice** — An invoice issued by a VAT vendor with specific required fields. Only vendors may issue one.

**Assessed loss** — A loss carried forward to reduce tax in a future year.

**Ring-fencing (s20A)** — A rule that can stop you using a business loss against your salary.

### Compliance

**POPIA** — Protection of Personal Information Act. South Africa's data protection law.

**Information Officer** — The person legally responsible for POPIA in your business. For a solo owner, you. Must be registered with the Information Regulator.

**Information Regulator** — The body that enforces POPIA. inforegulator.org.za

**PAIA** — Promotion of Access to Information Act. Sits alongside POPIA.

**CPA** — Consumer Protection Act 68 of 2008.

**ECTA** — Electronic Communications and Transactions Act 25 of 2002. Governs online selling.

**Implied warranty** — A guarantee the law puts into every sale, whether you write it down or not. Six months, under CPA s56.

**Voetstoots** — Afrikaans for "as is". A clause sellers used to avoid liability for defects. It no longer protects a business selling to a consumer.

**Cooling-off period** — The right to cancel and get a refund without giving a reason. Seven days for most online purchases.

**B-BBEE** — Broad-Based Black Economic Empowerment.

**EME** — Exempted Micro Enterprise. Turnover under R10 million. Uses a free affidavit instead of a paid certificate.

**QSE** — Qualifying Small Enterprise. Turnover between R10 million and R50 million.

**Commissioner of Oaths** — A person who can witness a sworn affidavit. Free at any police station.

**UIF** — Unemployment Insurance Fund. Only applies once you have employees.

**COIDA** — Compensation for Occupational Injuries and Diseases Act. Workplace injury cover. Only applies once you have employees.

### Licensing

**COA** — Certificate of Acceptability. The compulsory permit for any premises handling food.

**EHP** — Environmental Health Practitioner. The municipal official who inspects food and health premises.

**R638** — The 2018 regulation setting hygiene rules for food premises. Replaced R962.

**R146** — The 2012 regulation on food labelling and advertising.

**Business Act licence** — A municipal trading licence required for food, health and entertainment businesses.

**Zoning certificate** — Municipal confirmation that your premises may legally be used for your kind of business.

**NRCS** — National Regulator for Compulsory Specifications. Issues Letters of Authority for certain imported products.

**LOA** — Letter of Authority. Permission from the NRCS to sell certain regulated goods.

**SAHPRA** — South African Health Products Regulatory Authority. Regulates medicines and medical devices.

**NCR** — National Credit Regulator. You must register with them before extending credit.

**FSP** — Financial Services Provider. A licence required to sell or advise on financial products.

**FAIS** — Financial Advisory and Intermediary Services Act. The law behind FSP licensing.

**PSIRA** — Private Security Industry Regulatory Authority.

**NHBRC** — National Home Builders Registration Council.

**CIDB** — Construction Industry Development Board.

### Vehicles

**eNaTIS / NaTIS** — The national traffic information system. Every vehicle and owner is recorded here.

**BRNC** — Business Register Number Certificate. Lets a company own and register vehicles.

**TRN** — Traffic Register Number. The equivalent for non-legal persons: sole proprietors, trusts, partnerships, churches.

**Proxy** — The named person authorised to handle vehicle transactions for a business.

**ABR** — Application for Business Registration. The form used to apply for a BRNC.

**RC1** — The vehicle registration certificate.

**RLV** — Application for Registration and Licensing of Motor Vehicle. The buyer's form.

**NCO** — Notification of Change of Ownership. The seller's form. Lodge it the day you sell.

**MTN1** — Application for a motor trade number.

**Motor trade number** — Lets a dealer drive unregistered stock on public roads.

**Title holder** — The party with the right to sell. If the vehicle is financed, this is the bank, not the seller.

**Owner** — The party recorded as owner on eNaTIS. Can be different from the title holder.

**RWC / CoR** — Roadworthy Certificate, or Certificate of Roadworthiness. Required when a vehicle changes ownership.

**PrDP** — Professional Driving Permit. Needed for heavy vehicles and passenger transport.

**GVM** — Gross Vehicle Mass. Over 3,500 kg brings extra rules.

**VIN** — Vehicle Identification Number.

**Logbook** — The record of business kilometres SARS requires before allowing a travel claim.

### Working from home and getting paid

**Home enterprise / home occupation** — The municipal category allowing a small, quiet business in a house without special permission. Conditions apply.

**Consent use** — Municipal permission for a business use beyond what your zoning allows by default.

**Body corporate / HOA** — The management body of a complex or estate, with its own rules on top of the municipality's.

**Geotag** — Location data hidden inside a photo. Switch it off in your camera settings before photographing stock at home.

**POP** — Proof of payment. A screenshot or document showing a transfer was made. Easy to fake. Never release goods on one.

**Reflected / cleared** — The money is actually in your account balance. "Pending" is not reflected.

**PayShap** — An instant bank-to-bank payment that arrives in seconds and cannot be reversed. System limit R50,000 per payment; your bank sets its own lower limits.

**ShapID** — A phone number or ID number linked to a bank account so people can pay you without typing account details.

**Immediate payment / RTC** — Older instant payment options. Also arrive quickly, usually at a higher fee.

**Overpayment scam** — A "buyer" pays too much on purpose and asks for the difference back. The original payment was never real.

**OTP** — One-time PIN from your bank. Never share it with anyone, for any reason.

**SABRIC** — The banking industry's fraud body. www.sabric.co.za

**Look, feel, tilt** — The Reserve Bank's three-step check for a genuine banknote.

### Money and documents

**Quote / quotation** — A price offer, valid for a stated period. Not yet a sale.

**Invoice** — A demand for payment after goods or services are supplied.

**Credit note** — A document that cancels or reduces an invoice already issued.

**Proof of payment** — The bank confirmation a customer sends. Not the same as money in your account. Wait for the money.

**Cash book** — The spreadsheet recording every rand in and out.

**Small Claims Court** — A court for small civil claims where no lawyer is needed. Limit R30,000 from 1 August 2026, up from R20,000. **Only a natural person may bring a claim.** A company cannot sue here, though it can be sued here.

**FICA** — The Financial Intelligence Centre Act. The reason banks demand proof of address.

---

<a id="doc-master-checklist"></a>

## Master checklist

Print this. Tick as you go.

### Key to the short words

This page is meant to be printed, so the abbreviations are explained here.

| Short | Means |
|---|---|
| SARS | The tax office |
| CIPC | The company register |
| Pty | A registered private company |
| POPIA | The customer data protection law |
| IRP6 | The twice-a-year advance tax form |
| ITR12 / ITR14 | The yearly tax return, for a person / for a company |
| EMP201 / EMP501 | Monthly payroll form / twice-yearly payroll reconciliation |
| VAT | 15% sales tax, only if registered |
| VAT264 | The form both parties sign when a VAT dealer buys from a private seller |
| B-BBEE | The empowerment status affidavit, free at a police station |
| BRNC | The certificate letting a company own vehicles |
| SAPS 601 | The police form to register as a second-hand goods dealer |
| COA | The food premises certificate |
| R638 / R146 | Food hygiene rules / food labelling rules |
| CoC | Certificate of compliance, for electrical or gas work |
| ECTA | The online selling law |
| POP | Proof of payment. A screenshot. Not money. |
| PayShap | Instant bank payment that cannot be reversed |
| NRCS | The regulator whose letter some imported goods need |
| MOI | The company's rulebook |

---

### Part A: everyone, in order

**Before you trade**

- [ ] Sold something once and got paid, to prove people want it
- [ ] Opened a separate bank account for business money only, and asked the bank my instant payment limits
- [ ] If working from home: checked zoning, title deed and complex rules; told my household insurer
- [ ] Location switched off in my phone camera before photographing stock at home
- [ ] Checked whether my business type needs a licence (`01-core/04` and `04-business-types/`)
- [ ] Applied for that licence, if needed, and waited for it to be issued
- [ ] Checked zoning, if I work from home or from fixed premises

**Brand and documents**

- [ ] Chosen a name, and checked it on CIPC, as a.co.za domain, and on social media
- [ ] Bought the domain, even if there is no website yet
- [ ] Built a brand one-pager (`02-branding-and-marketing/01`)
- [ ] Built a quote template
- [ ] Built an invoice template, with correct VAT wording for my situation
- [ ] Written terms and conditions
- [ ] Written a privacy notice
- [ ] Set up a cash book spreadsheet
- [ ] Set up folders in Drive with a consistent naming system (`03-documents/02`)

**Compliance**

- [ ] Registered my Information Officer at inforegulator.org.za (free)
- [ ] Confirmed I have a SARS income tax number
- [ ] Registered as a provisional taxpayer
- [ ] Decided sole proprietor or company, and registered if needed
- [ ] If a company: confirmed the SARS registered representative
- [ ] If a company: filed beneficial ownership information (free)
- [ ] Got a B-BBEE affidavit signed, if a customer has asked (free)

**Marketing**

- [ ] Claimed and completed my Google Business Profile
- [ ] Set up WhatsApp Business with a catalogue and quick replies
- [ ] Asked my first three customers for reviews

**Every sale where I never meet the customer, or meet them once**
- [ ] Money reflected in my balance, not "pending", before anything is handed over
- [ ] No goods released on a screenshot, SMS, or PDF proof of payment
- [ ] Meeting in a public, busy, daylight place with cameras; someone told; someone with me
- [ ] For a vehicle: licence and ID copy received first, keys kept in my hand, no solo test drive

**Money hygiene**

- [ ] Photographing every slip on the day I get it
- [ ] Reconciling the cash book against the bank statement monthly
- [ ] Invoice numbers running in order with no gaps
- [ ] Recording enquiries and sales each month, not likes and followers

### Part A2: extra, only if you registered a Pty Ltd

See [Running a Pty Ltd](#doc-running-a-pty-ltd) and [Paying yourself from a Pty Ltd](#doc-paying-yourself-from-a-pty-ltd).

**Once, after registration**
- [ ] SARS registered representative (public officer) confirmed on eFiling
- [ ] Beneficial ownership filed with CIPC (free)
- [ ] Company records folder created: MOI, share certificate, director record, securities register
- [ ] Bank account opened in the company name, used only for company money
- [ ] Decided how I will pay myself, and why
- [ ] Registered name and registration number on every document, with "trading as" if I use a brand name
- [ ] If planning to add lines later: registered a neutral company name, brand in the trading name

**Every year**
- [ ] Beneficial ownership declaration current
- [ ] CIPC annual return filed within 30 business days of the registration anniversary
- [ ] Annual financial statements prepared
- [ ] Public Interest Score calculated at year end
- [ ] Financial Accountability Supplement or statements filed with the annual return
- [ ] ITR14 filed within 12 months of financial year end
- [ ] Two IRP6 provisional returns filed
- [ ] Loan account checked and cleared BEFORE financial year end

**Every month, if the company pays anyone a salary including me**
- [ ] EMP201 submitted and paid by the 7th

**Never**
- [ ] Pay a personal expense from the company account
- [ ] Move money out without recording it as salary, dividend, or loan
- [ ] Declare a dividend without a written resolution

### Part B: additions by business type

**Vehicle dealer** (`04-business-types/01`)
- [ ] BRNC or Traffic Register Number
- [ ] SAPS second-hand goods dealer registration (SAPS 601, free)
- [ ] Acquisition and disposal register set up with VIN, engine, odometer, colour fields
- [ ] Motor trade numbers, if I move unregistered stock
- [ ] Motor trade insurance including stock and test drives
- [ ] Sale agreement with a numbered defect schedule the buyer initials
- [ ] VAT decision made, with notional input tax modelled
- [ ] VAT264 process in place, if registered

**Food** (`04-business-types/02`)
- [ ] Accredited R638 training for me and every food handler
- [ ] Premises prepared to R638 standards
- [ ] COA applied for through the municipal Environmental Health Department
- [ ] COA displayed on the wall
- [ ] Municipal business licence
- [ ] Fire compliance and gas CoC, if relevant
- [ ] Labels checked against R146 before printing

**Beauty and personal care** (`04-business-types/03`)
- [ ] Municipal health establishment licence
- [ ] Premises ready for health inspection
- [ ] Treatment liability insurance matching what I actually do
- [ ] Consultation and patch test record template
- [ ] Booking, deposit and cancellation terms in writing
- [ ] Written photo permission from clients before posting

**Retail and online** (`04-business-types/04`)
- [ ] SAPS registration, if I deal in second-hand goods
- [ ] SARS importer's code and NRCS check, if I import
- [ ] ECTA s43 information published on my site
- [ ] Order review step before checkout
- [ ] Returns policy respecting the seven-day cooling-off right
- [ ] Stock tracking alongside the cash book

**Services and trades** (`04-business-types/05`)
- [ ] Professional or statutory registration for my trade, if required
- [ ] Public liability insurance
- [ ] Vehicle insurer told I use the vehicle for business
- [ ] Quote template with a real exclusions list
- [ ] Deposit and progress payment terms
- [ ] Vehicle logbook running

**Professional and creative** (`04-business-types/06`)
- [ ] Checked whether I am excluded from turnover tax before electing it
- [ ] Service agreement covering scope, revision rounds, IP and payment
- [ ] Ownership-transfers-on-payment clause in every contract
- [ ] Home office calculation done, if I qualify
- [ ] Checked I do not look like an employee of one client

### Part C: recurring calendar

Put every one of these in your phone with a two-week warning.

| When | What |
|---|---|
| Daily | Photograph slips, fill in the logbook |
| Weekly | Send invoices, follow up unpaid ones |
| Monthly | Reconcile cash book to bank statement, record enquiries and sales |
| Every 2 months | VAT return, if registered |
| 31 August | First provisional tax return (IRP6) |
| End September | Optional third provisional payment for the previous year |
| 28/29 February | Tax year ends, second provisional tax return (IRP6). If it is a weekend, the business day before. |
| July to October | Annual income tax return filing season |
| Monthly, if you run payroll | EMP201 by the 7th, or the business day before if the 7th is a weekend |
| Before financial year end | Check and clear the director's loan account |
| Twice a year, if an employer | EMP501 reconciliation |
| Anniversary of registration | CIPC annual return, within 30 business days, if you have a company |
| Before the annual return | Beneficial ownership declaration, or the return is blocked |
| Within 12 months of year end | ITR14 company tax return |
| 12 months after signing | Renew B-BBEE affidavit |
| Per your certificate | Renew SAPS second-hand goods registration |
| Annually | Vehicle licence discs, roadworthy where required |

### Part D: the four things that cause real trouble

In order of how often they catch small South African businesses:

1. Not filing when you earn nothing. Penalties grow monthly on nil returns you never submitted. A dormant company is the worst version of this.

2. Trading before a required licence is issued. You cannot apply retrospectively, and you can be fined.

3. Charging VAT without being registered. This is fraud, not a mistake.

4. Mixing business and personal money. It makes every other problem on this list harder to fix.

<a id="doc-sources-and-verification-register"></a>

## Sources and verification register

Everything in this toolkit that states a fee, a threshold, a form number, or a legal requirement is listed here with the source it came from.

All sources were checked on 13 September 2026.

### How to use this file

This file is for checking, not for reading from top to bottom. Come here when you want to know where a fact came from, or to confirm a number before you act on it.

How the entries are laid out:

- **[Official]** means a government department or regulator wrote it. Trust these first.
- "Supports:" lists the facts in the toolkit that come from that source.
- "Secondary" means a law firm, accountant, or business website. Useful for explanation, but confirm against the official source.
- Act names like "Companies Act 71 of 2008" mean the law itself. Number 71 was passed in 2008. You can read any Act free at www.gov.za.
- "s32" means section 32 of that Act.

If a name or a word here is unfamiliar, it is explained in [Glossary](#doc-glossary) in this folder.

---

Use this file to verify anything before you act on it. Where a claim matters financially or legally, open the official link and confirm the number yourself. Fees and thresholds change, usually in February and April.

Official sources are marked **[Official]**. These are government or regulator publications. Everything else is secondary and should be treated as a pointer to the official source, not as authority.

---

### Legislation this toolkit relies on

| Act or regulation | What it governs | Where it appears |
|---|---|---|
| Income Tax Act 58 of 1962 | Income tax, provisional tax, turnover tax (Sixth Schedule), small business corporations (s12E), assessed loss ring-fencing (s20A) | `01-core/03` |
| Value-Added Tax Act 89 of 1991 | VAT registration, tax invoices (s20), notional input tax on second-hand goods (s20(8)) | `01-core/03`, `03-documents/01`, `04-business-types/01` |
| Companies Act 71 of 2008 | Company registration, directors, annual returns, beneficial ownership | `01-core/02` |
| Consumer Protection Act 68 of 2008 | Consumer rights, implied warranty of quality (s55, s56), plain language, cancellation (s16, s17) | `01-core/04`, all business types |
| Electronic Communications and Transactions Act 25 of 2002 | Online selling: disclosure (s43), cooling-off (s44), performance (s46), scope (s42) | `01-core/04`, `04-business-types/04` |
| Protection of Personal Information Act 4 of 2013 | Data protection, information officer registration (s55) | `01-core/02`, `03-documents/01` |
| Business Act 71 of 1991 | Municipal trading licences for food, health and entertainment businesses | `01-core/04`, `04-business-types/02`, `03` |
| Foodstuffs, Cosmetics and Disinfectants Act 54 of 1972 | Food hygiene (R638 of 2018), food labelling (R146 of 2012), cosmetics | `04-business-types/02`, `03` |
| Second-Hand Goods Act 6 of 2009 | Dealer registration with SAPS, transaction registers, motor vehicle records | `04-business-types/01`, `04` |
| National Road Traffic Act 93 of 1996 | Vehicle registration, business register numbers, motor trade numbers, roadworthiness | `01-core/05`, `04-business-types/01` |
| National Credit Act 34 of 2005 | Credit provider registration with the NCR | `04-business-types/01` |
| Financial Advisory and Intermediary Services Act 37 of 2002 | FSP licensing for financial products and advice | `04-business-types/01`, `06` |
| Broad-Based Black Economic Empowerment Act 53 of 2003 | EME and QSE thresholds, sworn affidavits | `01-core/02` |
| Occupational Health and Safety Act 85 of 1993 | Workplace and construction safety duties | `04-business-types/05` |

Full text of any South African Act is available free at www.gov.za and at www.saflii.org.
